## 0. [What RL Brings to Reasoning: A Few Decisions the Base Model Would Not Make](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24663) <a id="link0"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24663
**Authors:** Siyu Zhang, Yuquan Lu, Yoshua Y. Li, Yifan Ding, Kun Zeng, Yu Huo, Yifu Guo, Rongxiang Weng, Xunliang Cai, Xiaoying Tang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24663v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) substantially improves mathematical reasoning, but what it adds to the base model remains unclear. We investigate this through a sequence of generation-time interventions on Qwen3-4B-Base, measuring how much of its RL-trained counterpart's gain each intervention recovers on 120 AIME problems. Power sampling with the same 8,192-token output limit brings no gain beyond greedy decoding. Yet borrowing the RL model's token only where the two models' greedy choices differ, roughly 4% of generated positions, recovers 90.5% of the pass@1 gain, with similar recovery across three model pairs on MATH-500. We find that even a single opening token has a substantial effect: forcing the base model to begin with "Alright" recovers 35% of the RL gain. The opening alone is insufficient, however. Later replacements after the same opening add another 5.2 percentage points to pass@1, and the fixed rules we test for inserting reflective words such as "Wait" do not reproduce this benefit. Sparse replacement also retains much of the base model's diversity while staying close to the RL model at pass@1. At k=256, it solves 67.5% of the problems, significantly more than the RL model (61.4%) and a mixture of whole responses matched in pass@1 (61.45%). The comparison is at equal attempt counts rather than equal compute, since rl-replace consults both models at every decoding step. These results show that the opening can recover a meaningful part of the RL gain, but a larger contribution comes from later choices, which can be borrowed while retaining much of the base model's diversity. Code is available at https://github.com/CyrusZhang04/WhatRLBrings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Analyzes what reinforcement learning (RL) adds to base language models in reasoning tasks, using token-level interventions to dissect RLHF gains, providing insights into RLHF fine-tuning effects on instruction-following and reasoning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 1. [DiffuPlan: Editable Planning State for Diffusion Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24717) <a id="link1"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24717
**Authors:** Qisheng Zhou, Zhen Xiong, Qiaoyu Tan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24717v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) are emerging as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language models, yet dLLM agents largely inherit an interaction protocol designed for AR generation: once intermediate state is written, later observations can only be incorporated by appending more text. This is a poor match to a defining capability of dLLMs -- reconstructing earlier text conditioned on context that appears both before and after it. We ask whether this capability can support a different agent-state paradigm in which intermediate planning state is directly revisable as evidence arrives. We introduce DiffuPlan, a diffusion search agent that maintains a single editable planning state and reconstructs only the affected steps when later evidence resolves an entity or changes the search route. To support variable-length revisions, we introduce Expansion-Partition Marginalization (EPM), which marginalizes over equivalent expansion paths. We further adapt diffusion trajectory RL to multi-turn search. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, DiffuPlan achieves 50.24 average F1, outperforming strong AR search agents. More importantly, under identical data and training, editable planning state improves the diffusion agent by 2.44 F1 versus only 0.47 for its AR source model. These results show that dLLMs need not imitate append-only AR agents: revising committed state is a distinct agentic advantage.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances diffusion language models by introducing an editable planning state paradigm and new marginalization techniques, showing improved performance over autoregressive agents.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 2. [Inside the Loop: A Mechanistic Study of Weight-Tied Transformers on Depth-Bound Algorithmic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24135) <a id="link2"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24135
**Authors:** Magnus Sesodia, Juil Sock, Philip Torr, Christian Schroeder de Witt

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24135v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Solving algorithmic tasks with many sequential steps typically requires scaling up transformer depth or chain-of-thought length, incurring unfavorable parameter and inference-compute costs, respectively. Looped transformers, which reuse one block of layers across many iterations, are poised to address this, since they scale effective depth without adding parameters or tokens. Yet, it remains unclear what each iteration is actually computing, or whether the loop merely simulates a deeper untied stack. Here, we study directed permutation traversal, in which a model follows $H$ hops from a starting node and reports the node it reaches, so every intermediate hop has a known ground truth. We find that a minimum effective depth is necessary to solve the task, beyond which the degree of weight tying becomes the determining factor. For instance, a looped transformer with a four-layer block iterated eight times solves the task in every seed, while an identically-trained untied transformer of the same effective depth never does, succeeding only with a lower learning rate or supervision of its intermediate layers. Probing and intervening on the residual stream, we find that the looped model advances through the intermediate nodes gradually, forming a staircase that adapts to its iteration budget, whereas the untied model, at that lower rate, exposes them only in its final layers. Taken together, our results recast looped transformers as iterative program learners, which allocate their iterations to successive steps of the algorithm and solve tasks that identically-trained untied models of the same effective depth do not. Code is available at https://github.com/magnus-sesodia/insidetheloop.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability techniques studying internal computations of weight-tied (looped) transformers on algorithmic tasks, with probing and intervention revealing iterative program-like behavior.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 3. [Where RL Breaks: Localizing and Repairing Failures via Targeted Continued Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25836) <a id="link3"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25836
**Authors:** Xinyi Wang, Danqi Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25836v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve reasoning in language models, yet growing evidence suggests that it mostly sharpens what the base model can already do. This raises a practical question: when RL fails, what is the base model missing? We look inside the RL policy's own rollouts and find that failures are localized: whether a rollout fails is strongly predicted by a small set of characteristic n-grams, ranging from reflection phrases such as "let's recheck" to specific operations such as "the inverse of A". These failures persist throughout RL training, although they are not beyond the model's reach: other sampled answers to the same question are often correct. Building on this observation, we use the failure $n$-grams to retrieve text from the pretraining corpus and continue pretraining the base model on it before RL. The resulting corpus has only 35M tokens, 3--4 orders of magnitude smaller than typical mid-training. We evaluate this approach on OLMo2 and Qwen3 models trained with RL on (1) GSM8K; (2) a mixture of math, commonsense, and knowledge questions; and (3) general math and science reasoning. Despite its size, the corpus yields consistent gains: our approach outperforms continued pretraining on the same amount of text sampled from the pretraining corpus across 10 out of 11 settings, including by $2.0$ points on GSM8K when inserted before mid-training. The retrieved text also transfers across model families: text retrieved for OLMo2-1B's failures improves Qwen3-4B on the mixture by $1.9$ points over the same baseline. Together, our results suggest that where RL fails is a cheap and effective signal for selecting pretraining data.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a novel method to improve RLHF-like instruction-following by identifying failure n-grams in RL rollouts and using targeted continued pretraining on retrieved text before RL. This is a new fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following and reasoning, matching criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 4. [Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23935) <a id="link4"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23935
**Authors:** Jiakun Fan, Min Xiao, Qiang Wang, Neiwen Ling, Zhichao Liu, Zhihao Jia, Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos, Zijun Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23935v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves $3.84\times$ and $4.14\times$ mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: presents DScout, a new method improving diffusion language models' inference throughput via factorization-guided training and inference, advancing diffusion-based LLM decoding performance.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 5. [RARE: Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution for Open-Ended Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25434) <a id="link5"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25434
**Authors:** Xueyun Tian, Bingbing Xu, Chuyue Li, Jiaming Liu, Chao Zhang, Weijia Feng, MingHua Ma, Yu Zhao, Jingchu Wang, Ruihua Huang, Yuanzhuo Wang, Huawei Shen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25434v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Verifiable rewards have advanced reinforcement learning in mathematics and code. However, many valuable open-ended tasks share three properties: no automatic verifier of overall quality, multiple requirements to satisfy jointly, and multiple valid responses to each query. Training therefore needs feedback on requirement satisfaction across different valid responses. Rubric-based reinforcement learning provides this feedback by aggregating scores from explicit criteria. As learning progresses unevenly, some criteria become fully satisfied and lose discrimination while others remain informative. Dynamic methods revise shared rubrics or select evolving criteria to restore discrimination, but may also change useful criteria. To address this, we propose Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution (RARE). The core idea is to use current policy feedback to guide both which criteria change and what their replacements assess. Sample-level updates replace criteria fully satisfied across current outputs with checks of remaining quality differences, retaining all other criteria and scores. Within this rule, we compare rewarding desirable qualities with penalizing observed flaws. Video script generation exemplifies these properties and supports content production. Its demands for coherent, executable narratives under constraints make it a challenging testbed. Targeting flaws performs best across three video types with a mean gain of 3.82 points over matched static rubrics. Model and human preferences support these gains. Improvements also extend to writing and general generation benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new reinforcement learning method (RARE) that adaptively evolves rubrics to improve instruction-following and open-ended generation quality, a specific fine-tuning step to enhance instruction-following capabilities.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 6. [Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25725) <a id="link6"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25725
**Authors:** Haoqun Cao, Yurun Yuan, Tengyang Xie

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25725v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. We study when such off-policy deployment is justified, and what roles its core ingredients, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, play in handling the resulting distribution shift. We show that for a general class of off-policy policy gradient updates, consistency, in the sense that the optimal policy remains a stationary point, requires one of two structures at the optimal policy: trajectory-level importance weighting, which suffers from the well-known curse of horizon, or KL regularization with a baseline equal to the optimal soft value, instantiated by the grouped mean. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Provides a theoretical and empirical analysis of off-policy policy gradient methods used in LLM post-training, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, directly relevant to RLHF and instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 7. [Learning to Program Retrievals: Composing Search Primitives via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24854) <a id="link7"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24854
**Authors:** Kyuyoung Kim, Zilin Xiao, Zhiyuan Wang, Avinash Atreya, Ke Yang, Jinwoo Shin, Kecheng Hao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24854v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Retrieval-augmented language models increasingly use multi-step search to solve knowledge-intensive tasks, yet existing systems typically expose retrieval through fixed pipelines or collections of atomic tools. We introduce Retrieval Program Learning (RPL), a programmable search framework that allows a policy to compose search primitives into executable programs with local state and control flow. This enables adaptive search that can retrieve broadly, process intermediate results, and selectively construct model context without tying retrieval breadth to context consumption. However, the resulting flexibility creates a difficult exploration problem: useful programs may require several coordinated decisions, while reinforcement learning often supplies only sparse feedback. To address this challenge, we propose program mutation, a grammar-constrained and execution-verified procedure that generates local variants of suboptimal programs sampled during RL, converts them into training trajectories, and distills them back into the policy. We evaluate RPL on open-domain question answering and corpus-level reasoning tasks, comparing it with a range of retrieval and reasoning baselines. RPL consistently improves open-domain QA performance across model sizes and in- and out-of-domain benchmarks, with particularly large gains on multi-hop tasks. Notably, a 4B model trained with RPL outperforms all 8B model baselines. On corpus-level reasoning, RPL achieves the highest answer F1 and document-coverage F1 for both model sizes while substantially reducing context consumption and retrieval turns. Our results show that programmable search expands the retrieval strategies that agents can learn, while program mutation provides an effective mechanism for exploring useful program structures beyond direct on-policy sampling.

**Comment:** Closely matches criterion 1: introduces a new reinforcement learning method (Retrieval Program Learning) to improve retrieval-augmented language models' instruction-following and reasoning capabilities via program mutation and RL.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 8. [Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25880) <a id="link8"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25880
**Authors:** Xidong Wu, Qi Qi, Yuqiong Ji, Shangqian Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25880v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Unlike on-policy distillation (OPD), which depends on a separate teacher model, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) leverages a single language model acting simultaneously as the student and the teacher, with the teacher conditioned on privileged information. Despite this architectural simplicity, synchronous rollout generation dominates overall training time, creating a severe systems bottleneck when scaling OPSD. To address this, we introduce Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation (AsyncOPSD), a framework that decouples rollout generation from learner updates to improve training throughput. Since the teacher and student share parameters, AsyncOPSD eliminates the multi-model coordination overhead inherent to OPD architectures in asynchronous training. As the student policy evolves, buffered trajectories generated by older policies become stale, potentially degrading performance. To mitigate this, we introduce a distillation objective based on staleness-aware policy control and token-level entropy weighting. Across three model sizes and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, AsyncOPSD improves the training throughput by $1.96$--$2.25\times$ over synchronous OPSD while increasing average benchmark accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Introduces Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation (AsyncOPSD), a novel fine-tuning method improving training throughput and performance in on-policy self-distillation for language models, relevant to instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 9. [Self-Distillation for Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25497) <a id="link9"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25497
**Authors:** Wenxuan Zhu, Bang An, Shuming Liu, Wenqing Cui, Tianqi Xu, Bing Li, Yibo Yang, Peter Wonka, Bernard Ghanem

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25497v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning treats the expert demonstration or gold response as a one-hot target, suppressing valid alternative continuations that the model would otherwise assign probability to. We propose Gold-Conditioned Distillation (GCD), a method that turns expert demonstrations into model-native supervision. GCD uses one model as both teacher and student: the teacher conditions on a prompt containing the gold response in context, the student conditions on the original prompt as at inference, and the student is trained to minimize the forward KL divergence to the teacher's next-token distribution at each gold response token. This lets the model use its prior knowledge to teach itself the new task: correctness is anchored by the gold response, while the soft target preserves plausible alternatives. Across various training tasks on Qwen and Llama models, GCD improves new-task accuracy over SFT and generally reduces degradation on general benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new supervised fine-tuning method (Gold-Conditioned Distillation) that improves instruction-following by using a self-distillation approach to better preserve plausible alternatives during fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 10. [Diffusion Language Models Change Speed Across Tasks and Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25835) <a id="link10"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25835
**Authors:** Mohsen Fayyaz, Ali Modarressi, Daniel Israel, Lucas Bandarkar, Nanyun Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25835v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models can write several tokens in one forward pass, and they decide during generation how many passes to spend. Two outputs of the same token length can therefore vary a lot in cost. To understand the underlying factors of this cost variation, we analyze two diffusion models across 10 tasks. We find that the number of forward passes per token depends heavily on the task. Text copied or lightly edited from the input is cheap, while open-ended writing is costly. In DiffusionGemma, generating a token for story writing requires more than three times as many forward passes as for grammar correction. Two output properties explain much of this gap: how much of it is new relative to the input, and how rare its words are within the task. The same holds within a single response when thinking is involved. The final answer is cheap when it repeats the model's reasoning trace and costly when it adds something new. In multilingual generation, we find that highly tokenized languages also require more passes per token, adding to the cost they already pay with more tokens. Finally, cost tracks correctness: on the same math problem, wrong answers take more passes per token than correct ones, and weighting candidate answers by decoding speed beats majority voting by up to 4.2 points at no extra cost. Taken together, our results show that diffusion's computational advantage depends on how much of the output is already determined, while its decoding effort offers a practical signal for making inference more efficient and reliable.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models with analysis of decoding cost and efficiency signals related to generation tasks and languages.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 11. [UtilityBench: Measuring Language Model Utility When Errors Have Different Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25924) <a id="link11"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25924
**Authors:** Qifeng Lei, Zhiyong Yang, Zitai Wang, Shilong Bao, Linchao Zhang, Qianqian Xu, Qingming Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25924v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world scenarios with important outcomes, the impact of their mistakes often extends far beyond what correctness can capture. In domains like medicine, a single error can lead to serious consequences and put patient safety at risk. However, mainstream evaluation protocols still treat all errors as equal, overlooking the spectrum of real-world costs. To address this, we propose a utility-based evaluation paradigm that explicitly incorporates the varying consequences of errors. Guided by this principle, we develop UtilityBench, a large-scale benchmark of 15,000 instances with verifiable answers, quantifying error severity using Best-Worst Scaling to elicit meaningful, data-driven cost estimates. These quantified utilities shape when a model should answer or abstain, guiding it to make decisions that align with the true importance of each scenario. As another merit, we introduce UtilityAUC, a metric that measures the area under the utility curve determined by a model's response ordering, thereby enabling utility preference alignment beyond error frequency. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that UtilityAUC can distinguish between models where standard accuracy and AURC fall short. Empirically, evaluating 20 state-of-the-art models, we find that greater accuracy or more advanced architectures do not reliably translate to higher UtilityAUC, highlighting a gap between problem-solving ability and responsible decision-making in language model evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating language models by incorporating utility-based evaluation that accounts for different error costs, introducing UtilityBench and UtilityAUC metrics. This fundamentally rethinks open-ended LM evaluation beyond accuracy, addressing real-world cost-sensitive scenarios.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 12. [Who Gets Unmasked? Sampler-Induced Bias in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24795) <a id="link12"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24795
**Authors:** Xujun Che, Farsheed Haque, Depeng Xu, Xi Niu, Haopeng Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24795v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate by gradually unmasking a canvas, so their samplers decide not only which token to write but also when to commit it. We show that this second decision changes who appears in generated text, a sampler-induced bias on top of the model's own. Common commit rules, including LLaDA's default, commit the positions whose sampled tokens are most probable; this peeking favors majority options. To separate the sampler from the model, we read the model's belief about a fork, such as a protagonist's gender, in one forward pass, and show that this belief matches exact sampling from the model. Measured against it, peeking samplers on LLaDA and Dream amplify occupational gender stereotypes and collapse opinion distributions, and confidence-based ordering shifts gender-balanced occupations toward men, more so under faster parallel decoding. We propose Fork-First Sampling, which decides the fork before anything else is written: it follows the belief or any target distribution, improves fluency, and avoids the stories that remark on the protagonist's gender, which prompt-based fixes produce, at the cost of one forward pass. Fairness evaluations of dLLMs should report the commit rule.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper studies diffusion language models and identifies sampler-induced biases in token commitment decisions, proposing a new sampling method (Fork-First Sampling) that improves fairness and fluency. This is a significant advance in understanding and improving diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 13. [PV-Decoding: Progressive Verification Decoding with Decode Gating for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24722) <a id="link13"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24722
**Authors:** Renxi Liu, Yiming Li, Shengyin Sun, Chen Chen, Xian Wang, Shuai Yang, Huiling Zhen, Xianzhi Yu, Yaoyuan Wang, Weizhe Lin, Xiaosong Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24722v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an attractive alternative to autoregressive generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, but their inference efficiency is still limited by the cost of safely committing tentative predictions. In particular, speculative decoding for dLLMs is challenging because speculative tokens within the same diffusion block may depend on one another, making aggressive acceptance prone to error propagation and strict verification computationally expensive. We present PV-Decoding, a confidence-driven speculative decoding framework with progressive verification for block diffusion inference. PV-Decoding partitions masked positions into directly decoded tokens, speculative tokens, and unresolved tokens using two confidence thresholds, and then verifies speculative tokens with progressively stronger policies, including single-batch, dual-batch, and multi-batch verification. This design exposes a practical accuracy--efficiency trade-off: weaker verification minimizes overhead, whereas stronger verification improves acceptance precision and robustness. Experiments on three diffusion language models across GSM8K, MBPP, and HumanEval show that PV-Decoding reduces sequential model forward passes (MFPs) by 8%--37% relative to confidence-based decoding, while keeping the largest observed accuracy drop to 1.3%. On Dream-7B-Instruct/GSM8K, PV-Decoding (1-batch) and PV-Decoding (2-batch) improve throughput by 7.0%--13.6% under the same hardware budget, whereas PV-Decoding (multi-batch) is slower under the current runtime. These results suggest that confidence-guided speculation with structured verification is a practical path toward efficient and reliable dLLM inference.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely. This paper presents PV-Decoding, a progressive verification decoding method for discrete diffusion language models, improving inference efficiency and accuracy trade-offs in diffusion LMs, which is a significant advance in diffusion language model decoding.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 14. [ECHO: Efficient Causal Self-Recursion Beyond Auxiliary Multi-Token Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24766) <a id="link14"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24766
**Authors:** Yuxuan Zhou, Junyuan Shang, Tianyu Wang, Chunrui Zheng, Zichang Liu, Wenbin Chang, Shuohuan Wang, Dianhai Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24766v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multi-horizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbone's own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than a task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives a doubled token budget, a repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), a method that integrates multi-token prediction into the autoregressive objective, improving instruction-following and reasoning capabilities by training the backbone to predict multiple horizons. This is a new methodological improvement to instruction-following fine-tuning, matching criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 15. [Robust Direct Preference Optimization under Preference Label-Flip Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24945) <a id="link15"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24945
**Authors:** Chenye Yang, Weiyu Xu, Lifeng Lai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24945v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is widely used for offline Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback. However, recent works have shown that DPO can be easily manipulated by adversarial preference label flip attacks, which are specifically designed to mislead the learner toward an attacker-chosen target policy. To address this issue, we design robust DPO methods that can defend against such attacks. Building on the property that lable flips induce parameter-independent gradient shifts in log-linear DPO, we propose Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which removes the $K$ highest-impact pairs, and Soft-Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which smoothly downweights them. We establish deterministic gradient perturbation bounds uniformly over all attack sets containing at most $K$ flips, explicitly separating defense-induced bias from surviving attack effects. Under bounded-region assumptions, these bounds yield time-uniform parameter and policy deviation guarantees. We further quantify how quadratic regularization improves curvature while introducing additional bias. Experiments on Stanford Human Preferences, HH-RLHF, and UltraFeedback-Binarized validate our analysis.

**Comment:** This paper directly addresses robustness of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a method used in offline RLHF, against adversarial preference label-flip attacks. It proposes robust DPO variants with provable gradient perturbation bounds and policy deviation guarantees. This is a clear methodological improvement related to RLHF instruction-following fine-tuning (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 16. [Policy Is Not Capability: Some Safety Benchmarks Measure Which Definition of "Unsafe" You Trained On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25060) <a id="link16"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25060
**Authors:** Vahid Jebraeeli, Jonathon Romero, Yijin Wang, Adrian Chan, Jianbang Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25060v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A guardrail classifier is reported as if unsafe were a property of text. It is not: it is a property of a policy, and the policy enters the model through its labels. We exploit an accident of an industrial deployment in which the same ~53k prompts were labelled twice, under two operational definitions ("is this a request for harmful content?" and "would a moderator block this?"), and used to train ten guardrail arms sharing architecture, codebase and optimiser family, at two model scales. First, the guardrail's own training corpus already contained the benchmarks: JailbreakBench-benign is 88% and OR-Bench-hard 82% present in the training pools, two over-refusal benchmarks, the kind a guardrail reports to argue it does not over-block. Everything below is decontaminated. Second, safety benchmarks stratify sharply by how much of their score is decided by the label definition rather than by the model: with architecture and optimiser fixed, the range across the ten arms is 0.99 on HarmBench-copyright and 0.043 on WildGuard-benign, a 23x spread. Third, a benchmark score can be moved across its entire range by 0.03% of the parameters. Across these arms HarmBench-copyright spans 0-100 and OR-Bench-hard over-refusal 3.4-56.4%, and the four arms whose last supervision signal carries a given policy occupy one extreme of both (exact rank p=0.005, or 0.04-0.14 at worst), at unchanged detection capability. For a distilled student the teacher's soft labels do this even when every hard label carries the other policy, so teacher provenance is an unrecorded confound in any released checkpoint. Fourth, validation accuracy on the one split that grades all twenty arms tracks agreement with the annotator's policy (rho=+0.74), not detection capability (rho=+0.34). We argue that guardrail evaluations should report contamination, a seed floor and the definition they were graded against as first-class variables.

**Comment:** Closely matches criterion 2: The paper analyzes contamination in safety benchmark datasets used for language models, showing how training data contamination and label definitions affect evaluation results. It provides empirical evidence of contamination and its impact on guardrail classifiers, which is a novel and important insight into test set contamination and evaluation reliability.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 17. [Mitigating Reward Hacking in Preference Reward Models with Evolving Penalties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26496) <a id="link17"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26496
**Authors:** Afonso Baldo, Ant\'onio Farinhas, Marcos Treviso, Ricardo Rei, Nuno M. Guerreiro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26496v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning for language models has moved from preference reward models to verifiable rewards, which sustain much longer optimization. Preference models are prone to reward hacking: the policy raises their score without improving response quality, or even while degrading it. Yet for open-ended tasks, where quality can neither be verified nor fully written down as rubrics, they remain valuable. We introduce Dynalize, which mitigates reward hacking online, during policy training. Periodically and asynchronously, a language model analyzes training rollouts and writes penalties for undesirable behaviors that the reward model appears to reward; a judge applies them to every rollout. In clinical counseling and creative writing, training on the reward model alone raises its score continuously but ultimately leads to a decline in external benchmarks. Dynalize sustains longer training and results in improved downstream performance. For example, we find that our method suppresses patterns incentivized by preference models that, without penalties, spread to most responses, and even to the model's reasoning, which the reward model does not explicitly score. Furthermore, we show that its gains hold in multi-turn training, and even after we retrain the reward model on the discovered behaviors.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Introduces Dynalize, a novel online penalty method to mitigate reward hacking in preference reward models during RLHF training, improving instruction-following and alignment robustness.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 18. [Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization for Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25092) <a id="link18"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25092
**Authors:** Hui Yang, Wan Tian, Tao Ren, Zhongyi Li, Yijie Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25092v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves the reasoning capability of large language models through verifiable rewards for mathematical and coding tasks. In token-level methods such as GRPO, clipping acts on individual token ratios while the reward and advantage apply to a complete response. Recent sequence-level methods align these units but still use fixed clipping bands, whose tail probabilities change as the score distribution evolves. We propose Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), which adaptively calibrates sequence-level clipping bounds using recent training statistics. The resulting band targets a specified marginal tail budget and adapts to policy drift; calibration conditional on response length requires additional assumptions. We bound the probability that a fresh score falls outside the band by the target miscoverage level plus explicit CDF discrepancy and drift terms. Under additional moment conditions, this bound also controls the objective and gradient error relative to the unclipped GSPO power surrogate. Experiments with the Qwen3-1.7B series show improvements over GSPO on difficult mathematical reasoning benchmarks and ALFWorld, with clipping endpoints that adapt during training.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new method (Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization) to improve reinforcement learning fine-tuning for reasoning language models, specifically addressing adaptive clipping in policy optimization for instruction-following tasks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 19. [Ask to Distill: Learning to Synthesize Data for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27450) <a id="link19"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27450
**Authors:** Jiashuo Sun, Hao Zhang, Xueqiang Xu, Heng Wang, Yixuan Xie, Pengcheng Jiang, Zhenrui Yue, Jiajun Fan, Peiran Li, Ge Liu, Shaowen Wang, Jiawei Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27450v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) can succeed with surprisingly few training questions, motivating a view of questions as carriers of distributional knowledge transfer. How can we assess whether a newly generated question is valuable for distillation, and learn to generate better questions---without access to its correct answer? We introduce Ask-to-Distill (\method), a framework that treats synthetic questions as distribution elicitors and optimizes the teacher supervision they expose along student-generated trajectories. A student-initialized synthesizer is optimized using a distributional surrogate for question value, computed from a frozen student--teacher pair without reference answers, correctness rewards, or per-candidate student updates. The reward combines teacher--student disagreement with teacher probability mass on the student's candidate support, together with length and diversity controls. The generated questions are filtered and deduplicated into a prompt-only bank for standard OPD. Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, and agentic tasks, our method improves average performance and teacher-gap recovery over unoptimized student-generated prompts and KL-filtered real questions, while matching or exceeding real-bank OPD in the evaluated settings. Signal analyses further show that optimized questions can elicit denser supervision and more total signal from shorter student responses, supporting elicited distributional supervision as an objective for synthetic distillation data. Our code, data and models are publicly available at https://github.com/GasolSun36/Ask_to_Distill.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely. The paper proposes a new method for on-policy distillation by synthesizing data (questions) optimized to elicit valuable supervision signals without access to correct answers. This is a novel fine-tuning or data synthesis step that improves instruction-following or distillation performance across tasks, related to improving instruction-following capabilities of language models.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 20. [Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25932) <a id="link20"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25932
**Authors:** Minoo Ahmadi, Seyedarmin Azizi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25932v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Shows a significant advance in the performance of diffusion language models. Proposes lineage-preserving SMC decoding to improve reasoning accuracy and diversity in masked diffusion LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 21. [ReAnchor: Reusing Rejected Verifier States for Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26531) <a id="link21"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26531
**Authors:** Sanghyun Lee, Wonjin Nam, Seungho Jang, Jaeho Lee, Jiho Park, Jaewoon Lee, Seungryong Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26531v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Block diffusion drafters accelerate speculative decoding by generating multiple draft tokens in a single forward pass. However, each pass produces only a fixed-length block from a single target-generated anchor, limiting how far the draft can extend. Meanwhile, target verification computes hidden states even at positions beyond the first rejection, but these states are typically discarded rather than reused. We propose ReAnchor, a training-free method that reanchors a pretrained drafter at shifted positions using discarded verifier states to generate parallel draft blocks. These blocks improve predictions within the original block and extend coverage beyond its endpoint. At sampling temperature of T=0, ReAnchor achieves its largest average gains on Gemma 4-12B, improving acceptance length by 19.4% and decoding speed by 17.9% across eight benchmarks. Averaged equally over four target models and eight benchmarks, it improves acceptance length by 15.2% and decoding speed by 13.4% with pretrained DFlash drafters.

**Comment:** This paper presents ReAnchor, a method to reuse verifier states in diffusion drafting for language models, improving decoding speed and acceptance length. This is a clear advance in diffusion language models (criterion 3) with a novel training-free approach applicable to pretrained drafters.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 22. [Balance-Aware Pivot Selection for Parallel Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26040) <a id="link22"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26040
**Authors:** Yejin Lee, Hyundong Jin, Yo-Sub Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26040v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models enable parallel token generation, offering a promising alternative to sequential autoregressive decoding. Existing acceleration methods use predictive confidence to enable parallel token commitment while preserving generation quality. However, confidence-based selection prioritizes reliable token predictions without explicitly considering how token positions shape the remaining decoding schedule. Moreover, such ordering does not always reflect inter-token dependencies, suggesting that confidence need not be the sole criterion for token selection. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Balance-Aware Pivot Decoding (BPD), which favors central pivots within contiguous masked intervals and decodes the selected positions in parallel. Balanced pivots yield logarithmic decoding depth in the interval length. Our practical method accounts for model uncertainty by combining confidence-based prefilling with confidence-thresholded center selection. We evaluate the resulting quality-efficiency tradeoff on LLaDA and Dream across five mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks. Our implementation is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DLM_random_pivot-EE62/

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper proposes a new decoding method for diffusion language models, improving parallel decoding efficiency and quality, directly advancing diffusion LLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 23. [Rubrics Beyond Evaluation: Exploiting Rubric Structure in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25850) <a id="link23"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25850
**Authors:** Yuxun Huang, Yunyao Zhang, ZeLiang Chen, Yi-Ping Phoebe Chen, Junqing Yu, Zikai Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25850v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rubrics are widely used to evaluate open-ended question answering by breaking overall quality into explicit criteria. These explicit criteria make rubrics a rich source of learning guidance, yet their potential for guiding the training process has been largely overlooked. Existing methods use rubrics mainly as direct feedback signals, without explicitly modeling the capabilities behind their criteria. This leaves their capability structure largely unused in guiding the training process. We introduce Rubric-Structured Reinforcement Learning (RSRL), an RL framework that unlocks the full potential of rubrics by exploiting their structure throughout the training process, rather than treating them simply as direct reward signals. At its core, RSRL builds on three key designs: (1) Rubric-to-Capability Structuring first recovers the capability structure hidden in rubric criteria, (2) Self-Sufficient Capability Learning then uses this structure to bootstrap underdeveloped capabilities into effective learning, (3) Capability-Residual Credit Routing finally preserves capability-specific gains during optimization. Experiments show that RSRL consistently improves both overall performance and underdeveloped capabilities, boosting downstream scientific question-answering performance by up to 7.43 over the initial policy and 7.60 over non-rubric structured RL baselines, showing that reinforcement learning can substantially benefit from the capability structure encoded in rubrics.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely. The paper proposes Rubric-Structured Reinforcement Learning (RSRL) to improve instruction-following by exploiting rubric structure in RL training, which is a novel methodological improvement to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 24. [The Window Is in the Reasoning: When LLM Agents Decide to Follow Prompt Injections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24048) <a id="link24"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24048
**Authors:** Haoxuan Jia, Yang Liu, Bin Chong, Yingguang Yang, Yancheng Chen, Jiayu Liang, Qian Li, Hanning Lu, Kefu Xu, Hao Zheng, Chongyang Zhang, Yulin Huang, Jianshen Zhang, Yongzhi Qi, Yunuo Lin, Hao Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24048v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents that read untrusted content can be hijacked by prompt injections. Runtime monitors that try to catch a hijack before the agent acts, and benchmarks that score each task with a single run, share an assumption: whether an agent follows an injection is settled when the injection is read. We test this assumption with branching rollouts that replay an episode exactly up to the step at which the agent reads an injection and then resample the rest of the episode many times, splitting the outcome variance into a part fixed at read time and a part decided afterwards. Across four AgentDojo suites, a Qwen3 agent without thinking is largely decided at read time (0.91 of the outcome variance lies between states); with thinking on, this share drops to 0.62 (95% CI of the drop [-0.38, -0.22]), in the same direction for three attack templates and two success labels. How much is fixed at read time is a property of the model that the same measurement reveals, and thinking lowers the attack success rate of Qwen3 but raises it for two other models. Resampling from reasoning prefixes shows that the decision forms gradually as the agent reasons. Zero-shot chain-of-thought monitors built on cheaper models miss much of what is already decided, whereas stronger monitors, and a lookahead monitor that judges simulated next actions, come close to what the prefix determines. Finally, single-run labels of thinking agents disagree with a rerun up to 35% of the time, so benchmarks need repeated runs per task.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19 closely: studies prompt injection attacks on LLM agents and evaluates runtime monitors to detect hijacking, with detailed variance analysis and reasoning-based monitoring methods. This is a security-focused study on LLM behavior under adversarial prompt injections.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 25. [Act on Your Behalf, Beyond Your Authorization: Benchmarking LLM Agent Authorization Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27548) <a id="link25"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27548
**Authors:** Zhiping Zhang, Franklin Mingzhe Li, Zihao Huang, Tianshi Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27548v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When an LLM agent acts on behalf of a user, it is expected to act within the authorization scope the user grants. Existing agent evaluations largely measure task completion or harmful consequences and can therefore overlook actions that violate this delegation without producing conventional safety or privacy harms. We introduce AuthorizationBench, a benchmark of authorization alignment grounded in documented real-world cases. A scalable construction pipeline produces 286 executable tasks spanning situational triggers, the presence or absence of direct harmful consequences, and ten application domains. We execute every task with four models at two reasoning-effort settings across three agent harnesses in an authorization-aware sandbox that keeps authorized and out-of-authorization paths executable and observable, yielding 6,864 trajectories. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). Our results show that task capability, additional reasoning, and consequence-oriented safety evaluation do not by themselves ensure authorization alignment. Moreover, agents that consult the principal before completing a task exhibit lower rates of out-of-authorization behavior (14.0% versus 21.6%, p<0.001), pointing to a tension between agent autonomy and preserving users' decision authority that calls for further human-centered study. Dataset and code are available at https://github.com/PEACH-Research-Lab/AuthorizationBench .

**Comment:** This paper introduces AuthorizationBench, a benchmark for authorization alignment of LLM agents, measuring whether agents act within user authorization scopes. It relates to AI alignment (criterion 11) by studying alignment of agent behavior with user intentions and value alignment. It provides a new benchmark dataset and large-scale evaluation. This is a direct match to criterion 11 (AI alignment techniques).
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 26. [FALCA: Falsification-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28361) <a id="link26"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28361
**Authors:** Zijun Dou, Kun Luo, Chaofan Li, Shuqi Lu, Zheng Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28361v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon deep search requires LLM agents to iteratively decompose complex information needs, gather evidence from external sources, and maintain a reliable research state under multiple constraints. A central challenge is that early mistakes can be amplified over the search trajectory: agents may anchor on an incorrect candidate, collect partially supporting evidence, overlook contradictions, and accumulate errors that misguide subsequent reasoning. We propose FALCA, a falsification-aware framework that formulates deep search as a propose-falsify process. FALCA adopts a collaborative Searcher-Falsifier architecture, where the Searcher proposes candidate answers or sub-answers, while independent Falsifiers actively seek counterevidence, check unmet constraints, identify conflicts, and produce concise falsification reports for research-state revision. To strengthen this loop, we introduce falsification-aware training, combining SFT warmup on falsification trajectories with agentic RL based on parallel rollouts and hierarchical credit assignment over research stages, state transitions, and actions. Experiments on five search benchmarks show that FALCA improves long-horizon deep search performance over backbone and harness ablations, while reducing confirmation bias and error accumulation through more effective falsification and research-state revision.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a falsification-aware RL framework (FALCA) with hierarchical credit assignment to improve instruction-following and reasoning in LLM agents, a novel fine-tuning and training approach for instruction-following models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 27. [A Fisher-Geometric View of Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26525) <a id="link27"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26525
**Authors:** Liu Kaiyuan, Zhuang Ziyuan, Ma Haoxuan, Gu Yannian, Song Mingfei, Weng Rongxiang, Ye Jieping

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26525v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Consolidating specialized foundation models into a unified generalist is a central goal of post-training. Two approaches are in common use: model merging, which is fast but treated as a heuristic, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation, which matches distributions but demands heavy rollout compute and suffers from cross-task gradient conflict. We show that the two are one objective seen at different orders. Within a shared basin of homologous specialists, the multi-teacher on-policy distillation objective is, to second order, exactly the Fisher-merging objective. Its minimizer is therefore the Fisher merge, a classic model-merging method given by a closed-form combination of the specialists, so merging is optimal, to second order, as a closed-form stage of distillation. Building on this identification, we propose Residual-Governed Branch-and-Merge(RGBM), a two-phase consolidation procedure. Concretely, a full-parameter Fisher merge is intractable on foundation models, so Phase~1 restricts the merge to the task-vector subspace. Extending tangent-space analyses of merging, we show that the same coefficients that mix task vectors also mix implicit rewards, which lets a residual program on unlabelled prompts solve for them. The program minimizes the worst per-domain residual and reports its energy-normalized values as the domain residual spectrum. Phase~2 lets this spectrum govern what may be added: RGBM passivates domains with low normalized residuals, trains isolated branches only for domains above the spectral gap, and writes back the admitted updates with calibrated shrinkage. On two open-source consolidation suites, the Phase~1 merge alone matches or outperforms fully trained multi-teacher distillation, and RGBM improves on it further with zero extra joint-training steps, whereas every un-gated write-back, including continued joint distillation from the merge, falls below it.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new fine-tuning/consolidation method (Residual-Governed Branch-and-Merge) that improves multi-teacher on-policy distillation and model merging, both relevant to instruction-following and RLHF-like distillation methods.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 28. [Discuss More, Comment Less: Boosting Automated Research via Multi-Perspective Collaborative Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24050) <a id="link28"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24050
**Authors:** Junkai Zhou, Jiajia Wu, Zhaoyi Zhang, Shiting Guan, Chuanguang Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24050v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As automated research is increasingly adopted for writing papers, automated review has become a critical link between paper generation and quality control. However, existing methods largely rely on single-model feedback or simple review aggregation, failing to explicitly model expert responsibilities or collective deliberation in actual peer reviews, which limits effective cross-examination and reliable decision-making. Therefore, we formulate paper review as a computational modeling problem of peer review mechanisms and propose RoundtableReview, a deliberative multi-agent framework for enhancing automated research. RoundtableReview structures each paper into eight semantic slots and coordinates six academic and industry reviewers with complementary perspectives, retrieval sources, and specialized responsibilities. Through independent assessment, cross-examination, roundtable deliberation, dynamically weighted voting, and chair synthesis, the framework generates evidence-based and traceable feedback. Experiments on two automated research backbones show that the method improves paper quality in both backbones and outperforms baselines in automatic and human evaluations with fewer comments.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation by proposing a multi-agent deliberative review framework that models expert responsibilities and collective deliberation, improving automated research review quality. This is a fundamental rethink of evaluation mechanisms beyond simple aggregation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 29. [EDMA: Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27120) <a id="link29"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27120
**Authors:** Emanuele Mezzi, Gertjan Burghouts, Fabio Massacci, Mengyuan Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27120v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multimodal question answering (MMQA) is based on integrating heterogeneous data sources, selectively leveraging relevant modalities, and ignoring distractors. Existing approaches based on Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) improve performance by splitting the task into intermediate steps, but do not quantify how each modality contributes to reaching the final answer. We argue that quantifying the effect that each modality has on the answering process improves process traceability and final performance. Thus, we propose Entropy-Driven Multimodal Answering (EDMA), which uses logical entropy to formalise multimodal QA as an iterative process of entropy reduction and to quantify the contribution of each modality in distinguishing correct from incorrect candidate answers. This enables the construction of quantifiable answering trajectories, where each step is associated with a measurable entropy reduction. EDMA introduces (i) modality-conditioned partitioning to estimate unimodal entropy reduction, (ii) entropy-driven multimodal fusion to capture complementary information across modalities, and (iii) entropy-based selection of the answering trajectory that minimises the most logical entropy. Evaluated across standard MMQA benchmarks, EDMA outperforms competitive prompting baselines, with average gains of 12.8 F1 and 4.6 LLM-J points over Planning Prompting and 18.6 F1 and 8.3 LLM-J points over Chain-of-Thought. Moreover, EDMA outperforms self-consistency baselines and is competitive with the multi-agent system baseline. Moreover, we show that higher entropy correlates with more false positives and, through controlled trajectory selection, demonstrate that logical entropy serves as an effective, monotonic selector for correct reasoning paths, validating entropy level as a reliable proxy for performance.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended multimodal question answering by quantifying entropy reduction per modality and tracing answering trajectories, improving reasoning path selection and evaluation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 30. [EG-GRPO: Evidence-Guided Step-level Credit Assignment for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25269) <a id="link30"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25269
**Authors:** Sijia Luo, Mengjie Gao, Yuxiao He, Mengshu Sun, Cuiping Li, Hong Chen, Jing Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25269v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-based search agents demonstrate strong capabilities in long-horizon information seeking and reasoning, typically trained via reinforcement learning. However, existing approaches predominantly rely on trajectory-level outcome rewards, providing limited supervision for complex search and reasoning processes. Although step-level credit assignment offers a promising solution, obtaining reliable and task-grounded supervision for open-ended search remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Evidence-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a framework that derives step-level supervision from the evidence annotations available in synthetic deep search data. EG-GRPO identifies the first supporting citation for each required evidence claim and uses the newly acquired evidence to construct step-level process rewards. By representing the accumulated evidence as an evolving knowledge state, EG-GRPO further performs state-conditioned relative advantage estimation and combines the resulting step-level signal with the standard trajectory-level outcome advantage. Experiments on five challenging deep search benchmarks show that EG-GRPO generally outperforms standard GRPO and existing reward-enhanced and step-level RL baselines across different model scales. These results demonstrate that task-grounded evidence annotations can provide effective process supervision for credit assignment in deep search RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new RL method (EG-GRPO) with step-level credit assignment for reinforcement learning in LLM-based search agents, improving instruction-following via fine-grained reward signals.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7
