## 31. [Rubrics Carry the Standard: Shapley Attribution for Checklist-Based LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24971) <a id="link31"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24971
**Authors:** Saeid Asgari, Rui Ying, Sara Malvar, Rakshanda Agarwal, Bruce Sun, Claire Wang, Leonardo de Oliveira Nunes, Ranveer Chandra

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24971v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Checklist rubrics applied by large language model (LLM) judges have become a standard instrument for evaluating open-ended outputs and for rewarding them during training. Two assumptions surround this practice: that leniency or strictness is a property of the judge model, and that a rubric can be improved by asking a model to review it. We test both in a controlled grading task with planted flaws and on two human-labeled datasets, SummEdits and RAGTruth. Holding the judge and the outputs fixed, rubric content moves the pass rate of correct outputs from 2% to 100%, whereas changing the judge model or the rendering of the same checks moves it by a few points. Model self-review then drifts the rubric toward the reviser's own standard: it adds requirements the task never stated and deletes checks that human labels had taught, with three reviser models and on all three datasets. To measure and control this, we show that under all-must-pass aggregation the Shapley value of each check has an exact closed form, which yields per-check catch credit, false-fail blame and unique catches in linear time. Attribution shows that self-review deletes the checks that do unique work and keeps the checks that cause false fails. A change-control gate that combines these quantities with a provenance freeze on label-derived checks holds nearly every drifting revision chain at its starting quality, with identical decisions under three judge models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: develops a novel Shapley attribution method for checklist-based LLM judges, addressing rubric drift and evaluation reliability in open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 32. [A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972) <a id="link32"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24972
**Authors:** Yanbin Zhao, Zhipeng Chen, Guangsheng Bao, Guandong Zhang, Yan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24972v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmarking large language model (LLM)-generated text detectors commonly relies on aggregate detector-level metrics such as accuracy, F1 score, and AUC. Although useful for summarizing overall performance, these metrics provide limited insight into how benchmark composition shapes detector comparisons or which texts distinguish detectors with different response profiles. We propose a measurement-based benchmarking framework for jointly characterizing benchmark heterogeneity and detector behavior using multidimensional item response theory (IRT). By treating benchmark texts as measurement items and detectors as respondents, the framework estimates text-level discrimination and difficulty-location parameters alongside multidimensional detector abilities. We apply the framework to a benchmark of 32K human-written and machine-generated texts across four domains, evaluated by 14 detectors. The estimated IRT item-parameter space reveals distinct but overlapping human-machine distributions, with substantial variation across domains and text lengths. The human-machine label shows the strongest multivariate association with this space, while domain and text length further contribute to benchmark heterogeneity. Detector ability profiles reveal multidimensional and domain-dependent response patterns that are not fully reflected in aggregate performance rankings. As a practical application, clustering texts in the estimated IRT space yields a compact subset of 2,088 texts (6.5\% of the full 32K benchmark) that largely preserves the comparative detector-performance patterns observed on the full benchmark. Overall, the proposed framework provides a principled and interpretable methodology for benchmarking heterogeneous detection technologies while jointly characterizing the measurement properties of the texts used for their evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a fundamentally new measurement-based benchmarking framework for evaluating open-ended text generation detectors using multidimensional item response theory, which rethinks evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 33. [LEAPS: Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern Learning and State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25919) <a id="link33"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25919
**Authors:** Howard Yen, Nitya Mani, Alex Renda, Christopher S. Hardin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25919v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context language models (LCLMs) power agents that act over long trajectories, from improving a codebase to running research experiments to forecasters that condition on lengthy histories. These applications draw on three distinct long-context capabilities: recall, where the answer is a fact present in the context; state tracking, where the outcome can be computed by performing a series of transformations to a specified state; and pattern learning, where the goal is to make a prediction by sampling from a distribution inferred from many observations. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on the first capability. In this work, we introduce LEAPS (Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern learning and State tracking), a collection of 12 novel datasets, spanning agentic workflows, behavioral modeling, and forecasting of market and world events. By construction, LEAPS is resistant to data contamination, extends to millions of input tokens, and uses reliable metrics. We evaluate 30 API and open models from 8K to 512K input tokens. LEAPS distinguishes models that perform near perfectly on recall tasks, finding that such models' state tracking and pattern learning abilities can vary significantly and degrade much more dramatically with increasing input lengths--even for pattern learning, where more observations should make learning a theoretically easier task. Moreover, thinking models often underperform their base counterparts, exhausting their generation budget or oversimplifying the task. We hope LEAPS can guide the development of the next generation of LCLMs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: introduces LEAPS, a new paradigm for evaluating long-context language models focusing on recall, state tracking, and pattern learning with resistant-to-contamination datasets and reliable metrics, fundamentally rethinking evaluation of open-ended generation over long contexts.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 34. [From Stories to Features: Scaling Concept Vectors for AI Alignment & Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24556) <a id="link34"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24556
**Authors:** Dmitry Scherbakov, Oleg Chernikov, Victoria Khamash, Kirill Afendulev, Alexey Dontsov, Ivan Oseledets, Elena Tutubalina, Anton Korznikov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24556v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study feature vectors: directions in a language model's activation space that correspond to human-interpretable concepts, such as honesty or deception. Existing unsupervised methods like sparse autoencoders attempt to recover the whole feature space, often at the cost of feature quality and interpretability. We take a more pragmatic route: focusing on concepts relevant to AI safety and alignment, we generate synthetic concept-antagonist story pairs and use the difference of their mean model activations as a feature vector. We scale this approach to 1,036 alignment-relevant concepts using 2M synthetic stories across multiple languages and genres, which we publicly release. Across six models from three model families, our feature vectors reliably generalize to detecting their corresponding concepts in chat dialogues and web text. They form an interpretable geometry shared across models and can causally steer model behavior when injected into activations. We further use these features to analyze model representations during jailbreak attacks, mathematical and agentic reasoning. Our targeted feature construction thus offers a scalable route to studying concepts in language models, potentially extending to millions of features across arbitrary domains.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: Presents new methods for sparse autoencoders in neural network interpretability. Uses synthetic concept-antagonist story pairs to generate feature vectors for AI alignment and interpretability, scaling to over 1000 concepts.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 35. [FlashMap: Flow Map Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24365) <a id="link35"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24365
**Authors:** Jeff Shen, Zhejian Zhou, Ryan Lee, Jonathan May, Shirley Ho

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24365v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight drafter to propose a block of tokens that the target model verifies in one parallel pass. Autoregressive drafters generate this block one token at a time, whereas block diffusion drafters such as DFlash generate it in one pass, making drafting faster. However, these diffusion drafters predict each token independently, ignoring dependencies between tokens in the block. We introduce FlashMap, a continuous flow drafter that conditions a flow map language model on the target model's hidden states and generates the whole block in one forward pass while capturing inter-token dependencies. On Qwen3-8B, FlashMap is faster than a matched DFlash baseline on every benchmark, with 7% higher average speedup and up to 6.1x lossless acceleration.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. Introduces FlashMap, a continuous flow drafter for language models that improves speculative decoding speed and captures inter-token dependencies in diffusion-based drafting.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 36. [DecisionPivot: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Decision Models in LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28522) <a id="link36"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28522
**Authors:** Xialie Zhuang, Mingcong Lei, Zunhai Su, Ri Liu, Jianjin Li, Peixian Ma, Jia Pan, Ngai Wong, Guanhua Chen, Chuan-Wei Kuo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28522v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Decision models support routing, guardrails, and output verification in LLM systems. However, item accuracy alone cannot establish whether models make correct decisions across controlled variations or assign reliable confidence to their predictions. We introduce DecisionPivot, a diagnostic benchmark comprising 48 tasks and 40,033 items across seven decision roles. Its core track contains 7,800 items from 13 generated task families, organized into contrast groups with computed answers verified by independently implemented solvers. The complementary reference track contains 32,233 items from 35 public test sets, preserving their original evaluation metrics. We evaluate 40 decision models and 14 general models using documented, model-specific protocols, assessing item and contrast-group accuracy, probability ordering, calibration, and empirical selective coverage. Our results reveal substantial differences obscured by aggregate scores: two 27B models separated by only 2.6 points on the reference track achieve contrast-group accuracies of 70.1% and 44.2%. Across 2,400 binary contrast pairs, Perplexity Decider correctly ranks the positive member above the negative member in 92.3% of pairs but answers both members correctly in only 74.1%, exposing a gap between probability ordering and decision correctness. Models with similar item accuracy also differ in the reliability of their high-confidence predictions. On a separate 1,296-item subset, GLM-5.3 achieves 93.5\% contrast-group accuracy at a higher reasoning setting, compared with 81.5% at its lowest effort. By linking errors to pivotal details and difficulty levels, DecisionPivot provides fine-grained diagnostics of decision failures that aggregate evaluation alone cannot reveal.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes DecisionPivot, a new diagnostic benchmark for evaluating decision models in LLM systems, emphasizing contrast-group accuracy and reliability of confidence estimates, fundamentally rethinking evaluation of open-ended LLM decision-making.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 37. [Calibration-Free Early Stopping for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via E-processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25212) <a id="link37"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25212
**Authors:** Dong-Jae Lee, Gyojin Han, Junmo Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25212v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, motivating adaptive policies that spend tokens when they help and stop early otherwise. Existing rules halt when a confidence or stability signal crosses a threshold, but such thresholds rarely transfer across models and datasets, and validation-calibrated rules lose their guarantee under shifts in test-time horizon or signal dynamics. We reframe adaptive stopping as sequential evidence accumulation along the reasoning trajectory rather than threshold calibration on held-out data. Our framework runs two e-processes on a shared per-chunk signal: a commit stream tracks the signal's position relative to its slow trend, and an abstain stream tracks its signed velocity, making the two anti-correlated by construction. Under signal-level nulls on the per-chunk increments, Ville's inequality yields an anytime-valid signal-level false-alarm bound at user-specified significance levels, with no held-out calibration. Across various model--dataset combinations, the framework matches or exceeds calibration-based stopping on the token--accuracy Pareto front using zero calibration data, and the same significance level transfers unchanged across cells where calibration becomes infeasible.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a new paradigm for adaptive early stopping in chain-of-thought reasoning using e-processes with statistical guarantees, rethinking evaluation and stopping criteria without calibration.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 38. [ProbePO: Sparse Probes, Redistributed Credit for Critic-Free LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24515) <a id="link38"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24515
**Authors:** Diyi Hu, Ruizhong Qiu, Lin Qiu, Hanghang Tong, Hanqing Zeng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24515v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards assigns one terminal reward to an entire reasoning trajectory, obscuring how individual reasoning steps contributed to the outcome. GRPO broadcasts a group-normalized advantage to every response token, while finer-grained approaches often rely on dense auxiliary rollouts or branching schemes that can also alter the data used for policy updates. We cast the missing middle as budgeted measurement: with a limited rollout budget, which partial solutions should be evaluated, and how should their value estimates refine credit? ProbePO selects partial-solution states with a reward-free uncertainty score and estimates their expected verifier rewards under a capped continuation budget. ProbePO redistributes, rather than replaces, GRPO's trajectory-level advantage across tokens while preserving each trajectory's mean token-level advantage; probe continuations serve only as measurements. Across three DeepSeekMath and Qwen-family models spanning 1.5B-7B parameters, ProbePO consistently outperforms GRPO and achieves the highest accuracy among the four evaluated methods in 16 of 18 model-benchmark comparisons. Against dense-probing VinePPO on DeepSeekMath-7B, ProbePO achieves higher accuracy on all six benchmarks with 76% less end-to-end training time.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new reinforcement learning method (ProbePO) for critic-free LLM reinforcement learning that redistributes credit across tokens, improving instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 39. [Steered Not Stirred: Effective Reward Tilting using Surrogate Reward Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25502) <a id="link39"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25502
**Authors:** Alec Sargood, Olivia Gallup, Georg Maierhofer, Noam Ghenassia, Paul Duckworth, Krisztina Sinkovics, Avishek Joey Bose

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25502v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Continuous-time generative models, from diffusion models, flow matching, and now flow maps, owe their practical value to high-fidelity generation and steerability. In many applications, a ground-truth latent ``oracle'' reward $R^*$ is unavailable or expensive to evaluate, e.g., human judgments or experimental measurements. Although the oracle reward is not directly observable, it can often be approximated through multiple cheaper surrogates that, individually, provide incomplete characterizations but collectively reveal substantial information about the downstream task. In this paper, we present a method for composing such surrogates to steer a pretrained model toward the distribution tilted by an unavailable $R^*$. Given a small feedback set of oracle rankings or numerical rewards, we introduce Mixing And Reward Tilting for INference-time Improvement (MARTINI), which learns a surrogate composition for plug-and-play use with standard inference-time steering methods. Our theory characterizes surrogate information limits and relates population recovery error to ideal-target KL at small tilt strength. For preferences, the bound additionally accounts for an irreducible mismatch between the Plackett-Luce population target and the conditional mean. Experiments demonstrate improved oracle-tilt fidelity in language generation and ImageNet alignment, and improved joint pose accuracy and validity in protein-ligand cofolding, without inference-time oracle queries.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: advances in continuous-time diffusion language models with a novel method (MARTINI) for reward tilting using surrogate reward mixing to steer pretrained models at inference time.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 40. [Learning to Reason Efficiently with Interventional Step Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25876) <a id="link40"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25876
**Authors:** Guanda Li, Xuefeng Bai, Yulong Chen, Zhiyong Wang, Kehai Chen, Min Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25876v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models often produce lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) traces mixing useful intermediate steps with unnecessary ones. Recent methods explore step-level signals such as attention mass and step entropy to identify and suppress unnecessary steps. Despite their effectiveness, such signals reflect the model's behavior at a step rather than that step's contribution to the answer. To address this, we propose the interventional step credit (ISC), which takes a causal-intervention view and assesses step importance to the reasoning through targeted parameter-level interventions. Building on ISC, we further propose IGPO, which uses ISC to guide fine-grained advantage rescaling in reinforcement learning, reducing redundant steps while preserving the important ones. Experiments across two model scales and nine benchmarks show that IGPO achieves up to 62% average token reduction and better overall performance among compared methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Proposes a new method (Interventional Step Credit) to improve chain-of-thought reasoning efficiency and quality in large reasoning models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 41. [Prefix-Tuning for Arbitrary Output Sequences on Pretrained Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24123) <a id="link41"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24123
**Authors:** Peter Cho-Ho Lam, Ziyi Wang, Zirui Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24123v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prefix-tuning is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs) that steers model outputs by prepending task-specific prefix representations to the input sequence. Despite its practical success, the theoretical understanding of the expressiveness of prefixes remains incomplete. In particular, the following question remains open: Given a pretrained autoregressive Transformer, for any input-output sequence pair $(\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y})$, does there always exist a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ such that prepending $\mathcal{S}$ to $\mathtt{x}$ induces the model to generate the target output $\mathtt{y}$? In this work, we prove that under the assumption that $\mathtt{y}$ is in the range of the pretrained Transformer, such a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ exists and can be explicitly computed by solving multiple linear systems. We further show that this assumption is necessary, in the sense that such a prefix may not exist when the assumption fails, thereby providing a complete answer to the above question. Our analysis is constructive and the obtained theoretical results hold under fairly general assumptions on model architecture. Extensive experiments on GPT-2 and Qwen3-4B demonstrate that our algorithm produces prefixes with strong control over model outputs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Provides a theoretical analysis of prefix-tuning for pretrained transformers, proving existence and computability of prefixes that induce arbitrary outputs under certain assumptions. This is a new methodological insight into instruction-following fine-tuning methods and control of LLM outputs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 42. [Preserving Reasoning Diversity in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards via Adaptive Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24236) <a id="link42"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24236
**Authors:** Wen-Zhi Li, Sainyam Galhotra, Claire Cardie

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24236v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but often reduces the diversity of their reasoning behaviors. We study this phenomenon from the perspective of finite-sample learning dynamics and show that limited on-policy rollouts can progressively suppress semantically distinct but equally rewarded reasoning modes. Once a mode becomes sufficiently under-sampled, standard on-policy RLVR rarely recovers it. Motivated by this observation, we propose TAR, a Targeted Adaptive Replay framework that preserves diverse correct reasoning behaviors already present in the base policy. TAR constructs a compact memory bank of diverse base-model solutions and selectively replays them through an adaptive mechanism that activates only when stored behaviors are being suppressed during RLVR. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two Qwen2.5 model scales, TAR consistently achieves a better balance between reasoning performance and diversity preservation than existing diversity-preserving post-training methods, while substantially reducing the forgetting of correct base-model solutions. Code is available at https://github.com/wenzhilics/tar.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely. This paper proposes TAR, a targeted adaptive replay method to preserve reasoning diversity during Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which is a variant of RLHF-like instruction-following fine-tuning to improve reasoning behavior diversity in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 43. [Does Unlearning Survive Collaboration? Robust Unlearning for Shared-Model LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25731) <a id="link43"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25731
**Authors:** Hengsong Liu, Liangyu Teng, Jingnan Cai, Yuntian Shi, Jingyu Zhang, Yang Liu, Jing Liu, Liang Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25731v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM unlearning is essential for removing sensitive or unwanted knowledge while preserving useful capabilities. As LLM teams are increasingly used to solve complex problems, forgetting must remain effective during collaboration. Despite advances in forgetting effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness to relearning, team deployment remains underexplored. We find that collaboration weakens forgetting: final-answer evaluations miss recoverable evidence in team messages, including complementary clues supporting joint recovery. We establish theoretically and confirm empirically that controlling final answers or individual messages does not ensure low recovery from their combination. To address this challenge, we propose Aggregation-Conditioned Unlearning (ACU). ACU trains a shared model using reconstruction feedback from individual and pooled messages, prioritizes views with higher recovery risk, and uses replacement-based credit assignment while protecting direct and collaborative utility. TOFU evaluates trajectory-level recovery, while WMDP-bio tests native forgetting and retained utility. On TOFU, ACU lowers full-trajectory recovery from 41.8% to 19.3% in ROUGE-L recall and factual recovery from 34.2% to 18.7% versus output-supervised training, with comparable retained-task performance. These results demonstrate that ACU curbs information leakage in LLM teams and generalizes across team sizes and readers.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Presents a new method (Aggregation-Conditioned Unlearning) for robust unlearning in LLM teams, addressing recovery of forgotten information and information leakage in collaborative settings.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 44. [How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23946) <a id="link44"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23946
**Authors:** Bowei He, Yankai Chen, Xiaokun Zhang, Changjiang Han, Ye Yuan, Chong Li, Xue Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23946v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Pretraining scaling laws treat training data as a one-dimensional quantity: a token count. Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) inherits this scalar abstraction, but modern synthesis pipelines now generate \textit{tasks}, \textit{environments}, and \textit{trajectories} independently and at very different costs, turning data into a structured design space. This raises a question pretraining never had to answer: given a synthesis budget, \textit{which axis} should be scaled? We address this question end-to-end with three tightly coupled contributions. First, we decompose agentic data into three cardinality axes (task, environment, trajectory), plus a reward-density control variable, and fit per-axis scaling relations on different large language models (LLMs) across math+code, SWE, web, and UI/tool application domains. Across all model sizes, model families, domains, and RL algorithms (PPO and GRPO) we measured, the scaling exponents order as environment $>$ task $>$ trajectory. When environment diversity is decomposed into world and environment-structure diversity, its advantage is mainly driven by the former, while the latter is just comparable to the task diversity. Second, we use the fitted laws as a common ruler to price synthesis methods, capturing each method's efficiency as a per-axis discount factor and identifying critical synthetic-to-real ratios at which collapse begins; these ratios differ by an order of magnitude across axes. Third, we cast budget allocation as a constrained optimization under our laws and discounts, deriving a Chinchilla-style compute-optimal recipe and validating it at held-out scale. The empirical results demonstrate that following the recipe yields up to $1.7\times$ compute efficiency over the dominant ``scale-the-trajectories'' practice.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in agentic reinforcement learning with a novel decomposition of data axes and derives compute-optimal scaling recipes, providing empirical scaling exponents and optimization strategies.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 45. [TrajEvolve: Trajectory Evolution for Reinforcement Learning with Hindsight Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28060) <a id="link45"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28060
**Authors:** Yi Wen, Hao Chen, Wanyu Wang, Maolin Wang, Pengyue Jia, Derong Xu, Huize Tan, Yingyi Zhang, Wenlin Zhang, Weihong Luo, Xiku Du, Xiangyu Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28060v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The Reinforcement Learning (RL) paradigm has achieved remarkable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, when applied to long-horizon tasks, it faces severe challenges: i) Due to the high task complexity, the model struggles to sample enough successful trajectories, resulting in slow convergence. ii) Relying solely on sparse final rewards causes all steps to be updated indiscriminately, encouraging redundant step generation. When both the quantity and quality of successful trajectories become difficult to guarantee, the performance is severely constrained. This dilemma stems from the limitations: existing RL paradigms rely entirely on the reasoning capability of base models, lacking the ability to actively optimize trajectories. To address the above issues, we propose a novel trajectory evolution paradigm with hindsight credit assignment, termed TrajEvolve. TrajEvolve not only relies on the trajectory inferred by the model but also generates ``evolutionary trajectories'' by refining low-quality steps in historical trajectories, thereby exposing the policy to high-quality compositions that lie within the base model's support but are rarely sampled within practical training budgets. Furthermore, TrajEvolve reformulates step-level continuous credit assignment as a binary classification task, whether each step is necessary for task completion. This simplification not only reduces the learning difficulty but also enables step labels to be obtained through counterfactual verification, achieving precise identification of redundant steps. Extensive experiments on two datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new methodological improvement in reinforcement learning for instruction-following by introducing TrajEvolve, a trajectory evolution paradigm with hindsight credit assignment to improve RL-based fine-tuning of language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 46. [Learning to Couple: Context-Adaptive Joint Denoising for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25723) <a id="link46"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25723
**Authors:** Zilei Shao, Ian Li, Heng Zhao, Kewei Lian, Guy Van den Broeck, Zhe Zeng, Anji Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25723v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models accelerate generation by predicting multiple tokens in parallel, but factorized denoisers treat simultaneous predictions as conditionally independent. Decoding heuristics reduce the resulting errors by controlling which tokens are committed, but leave the underlying distribution factorized. Joint modeling addresses the source of the problem, yet existing methods attach separately learned layers to frozen denoisers, leaving the backbone unadapted to the joint objective and the coupling parameters fixed across contexts. We propose Codpula, a context-parameterized denoiser combining a Transformer with a tractable probabilistic inference layer. Given a partially masked input, the Transformer produces token potentials that shape per-position predictions and compact gates that modulate the layer's coupling parameters, yielding a context-dependent joint distribution while retaining exact conditional inference. We jointly optimize both components under a conditional denoising objective using a dual-flow multiplicative update that preserves valid probabilistic parameters. At inference, Codpula supports standard heuristics and uses conditional queries to adapt commitments to dependencies among proposed tokens without extra Transformer evaluations within a step. Codpula sets new state-of-the-art results across a broad suite of reasoning benchmarks while advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier in mathematical reasoning and code generation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. Proposes Codpula, a context-adaptive joint denoising method for diffusion language models improving joint token prediction and setting new SOTA on reasoning benchmarks.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 47. [No Matter Who You Are: Addressivity and Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25728) <a id="link47"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25728
**Authors:** Seungjong Sun, Hyunmin Jun, Minsik Yoon, Eungu Lee, Choongwon Kang, Young Seok Woo, Jang Hyun Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25728v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sycophancy, where an LLM becomes overly agreeable, deferential, or flattering toward the user, has been identified as a major failure mode. While post-training has been implicated as a cause, how it internally gives rise to sycophantic behavior remains insufficiently understood. Post-training transforms the model's output from text completion into utterances directed toward an addressee, thereby giving the output \textit{addressivity}. We hypothesize that the accompanying pressure to follow the addressee's instructions and align with their preferences links this addressivity to sycophancy. To test this, we use Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions from pre-trained and post-trained models and examine their relationship with sycophancy. Steering models with addressivity directions can amplify or suppress sycophancy, and this effect is observed only in post-trained models. Furthermore, comparing the internal mechanisms of both model types confirms that the association between addressivity and sycophancy is strengthened through post-training. Finally, through a controlled post-training experiment, we find that models are sycophantic not toward a specific addressee but toward any recognized addressee, suggesting that the presence of an addressee, rather than the addressee's identity, is what elicits sycophancy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: Uses Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions in large language models and studies their relationship with sycophancy, a novel interpretability approach applied to LLM internal mechanisms post-training.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 48. [FisherEdit: Fisher Information Guided Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25914) <a id="link48"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25914
**Authors:** Yizhou Yang, Jiahong Fu, Zhiwu Wang, Kailong Fan, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, Zhenan Sun, Renzhen Wang, Deyu Meng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25914v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sequential knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) often leads to a degradation of general capabilities due to cumulative parameter interference. In this paper, we revisit prominent projection-based methods like AlphaEdit and reveal a surprising mismatch: their robustness is primarily driven by update regularization rather than the intended null-space projection. Based on this insight, we propose FisherEdit, a theoretically grounded method built on Fisher geometry, which replaces coarse activation-space projections with importance-aware regularization in parameter space. It penalizes changes to sensitive parameters more strongly while retaining flexibility for new facts, and admits an efficient row-wise closed-form solution. Experiments on Llama-3, Qwen2.5, and Olmo-3 across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that FisherEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance while better preserving model stability and downstream task accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Advances in machine unlearning techniques for removing specific knowledge or behaviors from trained models. Proposes FisherEdit, a theoretically grounded knowledge editing method that preserves model stability and downstream accuracy.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 49. [Safety Kernel: Mechanistic Alignment for Cross-Lingual Defense in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24614) <a id="link49"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24614
**Authors:** Chengyu Xie, Fei Shen, Shuyi Miao, Yue Feng, Jian Yu, Pengyang Shao, Yijun Tian, Xiaoyu Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24614v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The safety capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are highly uneven across different languages, often allowing cross-lingual attacks to bypass safety guardrails. Existing alignment methods predominantly rely on external alignment signals and provide limited insight into underlying neuron-level mechanisms, making it difficult to provide a unified internal target for cross-lingual safety interventions. To address this, we analyze cross-lingual safety behavior at the neuron level and identify a compact set of safety-related neurons shared across languages, termed the safety kernel (SK). Specifically, we first utilize multilingual parallel safe and unsafe prompts to compute safety contrast with activation responsiveness (SCAR) scores, identifying language-wise safety-neuron candidates from internal activation patterns. We then intersect the resulting language-wise safety-neuron sets to obtain the SK, and validate its functional relevance to cross-lingual safety behavior through activation suppression and activation-state transfer interventions. Finally, building on the SK coordinates, we introduce SK-Masked LoRA, which constrains LoRA adaptation to the feed-forward network (FFN) output dimensions corresponding to the SK, thereby focusing safety-related changes on the cross-lingually shared neuron coordinates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SK-Masked LoRA substantially reduces attack success rates across multiple languages, including languages excluded from SK discovery, while intervening on only a small fraction of internal neuron coordinates and producing generally limited changes in the model's general capabilities.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Introduces mechanistic interpretability techniques identifying a 'safety kernel' of neurons related to cross-lingual safety behavior in LLMs, with causal interventions and targeted LoRA fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 50. [Parallel Language Models via Variational Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26693) <a id="link50"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26693
**Authors:** Felix Draxler, Robin C. Geyer, Daniel Geyfman, Farrin Marouf Sofian, Justus Will, Philipp Hildebrandt, Giosue Migliorini, Prakhar Srivastava, Gagan Sai Trinadh Duggaraju Peddiraju, Kushagra Pandey, Jorge da Silva Gon\c{c}alves, Elijah Tavares, Jan Niklas Groeneveld, Yang Meng, Julia E. Vogt, Stephan Mandt

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26693v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Autoregressive language models emit one token per forward pass. This causes high inference latency, in particular as models are scaled up for quality. In this paper, we introduce Parallel Language Models (PLMs), which generate mutually consistent tokens in one forward pass by routing dependencies through latent auxiliary variables. PLMs are trained using a variational bound that reduces to an autoregressive loss plus a parallel decoding loss. Empirically, PLMs are on a faster and better Pareto frontier than autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. Pretrained PLMs achieve near-autoregressive pretraining loss on model scales of 80M to 3B parameters, and speedup follows a power law in model size. When post-trained, our 1.3B model matches or exceeds an identically trained autoregressive model on math, coding, and instruction-following benchmarks, speeding up inference $2.3\text{-}5.7\times$. Together, we show that parallel generation can be trained from a single training objective at autoregressive quality. We will make our code available at https://github.com/mandt-lab/plm.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Parallel Language Models with variational decoding to speed up inference and achieve near-autoregressive quality, including improvements on instruction-following benchmarks. It relates closely to criterion 3 (diffusion language models) since it compares to discrete diffusion baselines and proposes a new generative modeling paradigm for language models. It also touches on instruction-following improvements but focuses on decoding methodology rather than RLHF fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 51. [Honeyed Words, Hidden Daggers: Benchmarking and Training LLMs to See Through Mock Politeness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26059) <a id="link51"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26059
**Authors:** Caixin Kang, Sitong Gong, Tianyu Yan, Kaipeng Zhang, Yoichi Sato, Yifei Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26059v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The Chinese idiom "kou mi fu jian" ("honeyed words, hidden daggers") names speech that is polite in form but hostile in function. We present ZhenhuanBench, a 1,592-item benchmark built from expert commentary on Empresses in the Palace, covering speaker intent, emotion-language mismatch, and per-listener uptake. Text-only evaluation covers 34 LLMs. GPT-6 Astra leads the leaderboard at 73.48% overall, while its listener-reaction score is 58.37%. The annotations also supply supervised traces and string-verifiable training rewards. With 338 gold traces, an 8B model outperforms teacher distillation on identical items. Subsequent RL saturates, and chance correction reduces the estimated recoverable performance gap by a factor of 7.5. Our CPR-GRPO loop (Compress-Probe-Replenish) uses this estimate to weight gold supervision, reaching 67.0 on public multiple-choice tasks and 74.5 under the training-matched hidden protocol. Without target-task training, CPR outperforms Base, SFT, and GRPO across six Chinese and English pragmatic and sarcasm evaluations. On three external datasets, training with CPR yields higher mean Macro-F1 than SFT and GRPO.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses new training methods including a CPR-GRPO loop that improves instruction-following and pragmatic understanding in LLMs, with supervised traces and RL training saturation analysis relevant to instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 52. [Learning When to Abort in Transformer Planning: A Mechanistic Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25300) <a id="link52"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25300
**Authors:** Shi Feng, Qimin Zhong, Siwei Wang, Yifei Shen, Shang-Hua Teng, Hao Liao, Wei Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25300v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Planning does not by itself imply the ability to recognise errors, but recognising errors can improve planning capacity. In this paper, we study this phenomenon in Transformer planning. To analyze the inner mechanism, we follow the approach of Wang et al. (2024) and abstract planning as path-finding in graphs. In this setting, we train the model to detect the error of invalid transitions, i.e., after the model generates a next node that is not adjacent with the current node, the model should then generate a special abort token x. For expressiveness, we construct a Transformer with feed-forward width $O(|V|)$ for any fixed graph with node set $V$. It can detect all possible invalid transitions, and select a valid next node if the prefix is valid. We then propose a worst-case lower bound on feed-forward width, which makes the detector's linear width near-optimal up to logarithmic factors. In trained models, we demonstrate that invalid-transition detection increases previous-node attention, and most of the valid--invalid separation locates in the feed-forward network's output. This accords with our theoretical insights. Finally, we use this error detection to improve the model's performance: at inference, generating the abort token x will directly cause an external procedure to discard the attempt and restart from the original prompt. Experiments show that error detection combined with retry improves model's planning reliability on graphs, Countdown, and mazes.

**Comment:** This paper studies mechanistic interpretability of Transformer planning models, focusing on error detection and abort mechanisms. It relates to mechanistic interpretability (criterion 8) by analyzing internal model mechanisms and proposing a theoretical lower bound. This is a direct match to criterion 8.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 53. [Distribution-Based Regularization for Reward Fine-Tuning Diffusion and Flow Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24897) <a id="link53"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24897
**Authors:** Romeo Passaro, Jeongjae Lee, Jong Chul Ye, Michael M. Bronstein, Yoshua Bengio, Alexander Tong, Danyal Rehman

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24897v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reward fine-tuning is an effective way to adapt pretrained diffusion and flow-matching models to downstream objectives, but aggressive optimization often leads to mode collapse and distributional drift. Existing methods attempt to control this drift with reverse-KL regularization, implemented as an $\ell_2$ penalty on the velocity field; however, this functions by indirectly constraining sampling dynamics rather than directly acting on the generated distribution. To address this, we propose DFR (Distributional Functional Rewards), which augments the task reward with a distribution-dependent, per-sample reward that acts explicitly on the generated distribution. Formally, we generalize the KL-regularized objective from expected rewards, which are linear in the model's distribution, to convex functionals of it. The first variation of this objective defines an effective, distribution-dependent reward that directly penalizes deviation from the pretrained distribution. This formulation yields additive per-sample rewards for the Fr\'echet distance (FD), maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), and DreamSim diversity, recovering several recently proposed rewards as special cases. Across various text-to-image fine-tuning tasks, DFR matches the peak reward of KL-tuned baselines while increasing diversity by up to 29% at equal or lower drift.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper proposes a new distribution-based regularization method for reward fine-tuning diffusion and flow models, improving stability and diversity in generative modeling, relevant to diffusion language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 54. [Truncate or Not? A Scaling Study of Looped Transformers with Truncated Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26030) <a id="link54"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26030
**Authors:** Xuyuan Xiong, Siyu Chen, Zihan Shao, Yue Su, Weiyang Jin, Ping Luo, Zhuoran Yang, Xihui Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26030v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped transformers reuse a shared block of layers to increase computational depth without increasing the number of trainable parameters. They are trained with backpropagation through time (BPTT). To save training compute, BPTT is often truncated to the last few loops, but truncation yields biased gradients that may weaken the benefit of looping. In this work, we study the value of truncation in looped transformer training in a systematic way: we jointly vary model size, training tokens, and forward and backward loop counts across nearly 600 training runs and fit a unified scaling law that captures the effects of looping and truncation through effective parameters. Despite the training-compute savings of truncation, our fitted law favors full BPTT when forward and backward loop counts are jointly optimized. In matched-compute runs, full BPTT through fewer loops consistently outperforms truncated settings of the same compute. The law further shows that at fixed model size, the compute-optimal loop count and training-token budget both grow with compute. When model size is also free, the law identifies two fully trained loops as the best looped setting. Experimentally, this setting achieves lower validation loss than the compute-optimal non-looped model with about 27% fewer non-embedding parameters and only 8% more training compute.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Presents a unified scaling law for looped transformers trained with truncated backpropagation through time, analyzing the trade-offs of truncation and looping in training compute and model size. This is a theoretical/conceptual study of scaling laws in transformer architectures relevant to language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 55. [Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25447) <a id="link55"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25447
**Authors:** Tomer Garber, Tom Tirer

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25447v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional context. As these models reach real-world deployment, machine unlearning becomes essential. This process aims to remove the influence of a designated "forget set" without retraining from scratch, so that sensitive, copyrighted, or restricted content can no longer be elicited while preserving retained capabilities. Masked Diffusion Unlearning (MDU) is the first publicly available unlearning method customized for these models: it tunes the model $p_\theta$ to match a frozen prompt-masked anchor $p_{\mathrm{anchor}}$ on the forget set by minimizing the anchor-last divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_\theta \Vert p_{\mathrm{anchor}})$. We identify two failure modes of MDU. First, random answer masking can leave key forget tokens visible to the anchor even when the question is fully masked. Second, at a memorized (near-one-hot) prediction, the anchor-last logit gradient vanishes while the anchor-first divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{anchor}} \Vert p_\theta)$ logit gradient does not. As a result, MDU training stalls on the tokens we most need to forget. Backed by theoretical and empirical analyses, we propose Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU): anchor-first KL divergence toward a frozen anchor whose entire answer span is masked. On TOFU and RWKU benchmarks using LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B, our EMDU matches or exceeds the forgetting of MDU and adapted AR baselines, while better preserving retain utility.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Proposes Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU), a new machine unlearning method for masked diffusion language models to remove specific knowledge while preserving utility.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 56. [LatLM: Line-Parallel Language Models with Deferred Commitment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23951) <a id="link56"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23951
**Authors:** Shaorong Zhang, Shiyuan Li, Longxuan Yu, Greg Ver Steeg

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23951v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Line-parallel language models advance multiple lines concurrently for substantial speedups. However, frameworks like the Line-Coupled Language Model (LCLM) enforce a fixed commitment schedule across lines. Because dependencies vary within text, a fixed schedule cannot hold back a line whose prerequisites remain uncomputed, forcing premature commitments and cascading reasoning errors. To address this limitation, we introduce the Lattice Language Model (LatLM), making commitment schedules an adaptive decoding-time choice. Text occupies a 2D lattice where eligible frontier tokens are scored each round; a policy commits confident candidates while deferring unready ones for extended context. One forward pass supervises entire sampled trajectories via 2D causal masking, while an append-only cache retains exact states. At 482M parameters, we establish two findings. (1) LatLM matches a data- and capacity-matched autoregressive model on held-out text when decoded sequentially, while its adaptive parallel decoder achieves near-autoregressive accuracy on independent-line tasks using 25--41% as many decoding rounds. (2) When cross-line dependencies cause LCLM's fixed schedule to fail, deferred commitment substantially improves accuracy while retaining parallelism where dependencies permit. On mixed dependency chains, LatLM achieves 96.5% accuracy versus LCLM's 78.5%, using fewer than half the decoding rounds of the autoregressive baseline.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models by proposing LatLM, a line-parallel language model with adaptive deferred commitment decoding, improving speed and accuracy.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 57. [On Reparameterizing the Score Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24188) <a id="link57"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24188
**Authors:** Mohammad Hossein Amani, Leonardo Bocchieri, Maxime Peyrard, Cl\'ement Hongler, Robert West

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24188v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Policy gradients for autoregressive sequence models differentiate each token's log-probability while holding the conditioning history fixed. This discards information about how earlier decisions shape later conditional distributions, creating a credit-assignment bottleneck when rewards are sparse. We introduce History-Reparameterized (HR) policy gradients, which replace the frozen prefix in the score function with a differentiable reparameterized or relaxed history, allowing gradients of downstream log-probabilities to propagate backward through the generated trajectory. The resulting history-pathwise term is generally biased when added directly, so we derive Optimal HR (OHR), a centered control-variate estimator with a closed-form coefficient that minimizes the trace of the gradient variance at the population level. The variance reduction is governed by temporal structure in the policy dynamics: random or weakly structured policies provide little benefit, while policies with learned sequential dependencies yield informative history-pathwise corrections. For discrete tokens, the choice of relaxation is crucial: embedding-level relaxation yields substantial variance reduction that grows with horizon, whereas straight-through Gumbel-Softmax yields essentially none in our diagnostics. We validate the method on a Gaussian linear Markov model, a sparse-reward T-maze with a recurrent policy, and an autoregressive Transformer model for small Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) instances.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new methodological improvement to policy gradient methods for autoregressive sequence models, addressing credit assignment in RLHF-like settings for instruction-following models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 58. [OPDArena: Understanding Learning and Forgetting in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25651) <a id="link58"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25651
**Authors:** Yuanze Xu, Tianzhu Ye, Hui Sun, Wenhui Wang, Li Dong, Furu Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25651v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is increasingly used for language model post-training, yet how teacher supervision shapes learning and forgetting remains unclear. We present OPDArena, a framework comparing supervised fine-tuning (SFT), reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), and OPD across five pretrained models and diverse teacher sources. It jointly evaluates mathematical learning, general capability retention, and predictive distribution changes. Teacher choice strongly shapes retention: students of RLVR-trained teachers improve on the target task while retaining prior capabilities, whereas students of SFT-trained teachers incur greater capability loss. Across post-training methods, out-of-domain (OOD) distribution change shows a more consistent positive association with forgetting than in-domain change. To understand how teacher choice affects these changes, we examine the additional information teachers provide relative to the student's pretrained model. Controlled teacher comparisons show that the task-specific preferences teachers acquire through post-training make student updates more concentrated in-domain.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses on-policy distillation (OPD) as a post-training method for language models, comparing it with supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). This relates to methodological improvements in instruction-following or RLHF-like fine-tuning steps to improve language models' capabilities and retention.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6
