## 59. [Transformers as Adaptive Kernel Learners across Tasks for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24441) <a id="link59"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24441
**Authors:** Zhaiming Shen, Alexander Cloninger, Rongjie Lai, Wenjing Liao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24441v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Transformers have emerged as a dominant architecture in modern machine learning, with in-context learning (ICL) enabling models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on prompts, without any parameter updates. To understand the attention mechanism in transformer blocks, a growing line of theoretical work interprets ICL through the lens of classical statistical estimators. Recent works have established a connection between the attention mechanism and kernel methods, while the kernel function is usually assumed to be fixed. In practice, the query and key matrices in the attention mechanism are trained, which allows the attention mechanism to serve as an adaptive kernel method. In this work, we demonstrate that transformers can act as adaptive anisotropic kernel learners. Specifically, we study ICL for low-rank linear and multi-index nonlinear regressions, and conduct numerical simulations to show that the learned attention scores closely resemble the oracle kernels for these function classes. We then develop a general framework to analyze the generalization error of ICL by connecting transformers to the oracle kernel estimator for each function class. Our theory gives an explicit and constructive implementation of the oracle kernel estimators for these function classes using a wide and shallow transformer architecture, illustrating how the prediction is carried out by the interaction between the query token and the context tokens. Our work complements existing studies by providing numerical and theoretical evidence of how the attention mechanism adapts to anisotropic kernels in both linear and nonlinear settings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws and theoretical understanding of transformers as adaptive kernel learners, providing theoretical and numerical evidence on attention as adaptive kernel methods relevant to in-context learning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 60. [Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24137) <a id="link60"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24137
**Authors:** Guangya Hao, Yiting Duan, Longyan Tan, Yunbo Long, Hao Wu, Ge Zheng, Alexandra Brintrup, Zhuokai Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24137v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new continual test-time learning method (ESRL) that improves adaptation and fine-tuning of large language models without external supervision, relevant to instruction-following and RLHF fine-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 61. [Representations in Motion: Layer-wise Rotation and Trajectory Divergence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25574) <a id="link61"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25574
**Authors:** Zhuonan Yang, Yik Siu Chan, Wannan Yang, Ellie Pavlick

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25574v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work suggests that activation dynamics--how representations change during inference--might be more revealing about language model behavior than static readouts from individual layers. In this work, we investigate the layer-wise evolution of representations of factuality, using true and false statements when the model is prompted to either lie or to be honest. We report a geometric phenomenon in which the factuality directions extracted from the lying and honest conditions appear to rotate away from each other across layers. We find that such layer-wise rotation also occurs in a task that requires the model to take different visual perspectives in the 3D world, suggesting that the rotation phenomenon might be indicative of manipulating conceptual spaces in general, rather than unique to factuality. We then investigate the "real time" transformation of conceptual representations on a trial-to-trial basis. We show that it is possible to make strong predictions about the model's target task and behavior from trajectories alone. In addition, diverging points in single-trial trajectories correspond to the onset of rotation in aggregated-trial analysis. Finally, we show that both rotation and divergent trajectories occur in more naturalistic settings in which models are not overtly prompted to "lie" but rather report false statements in the course of completing a different task.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability via analysis of layer-wise rotation and trajectory divergence in LLM representations related to factuality and conceptual manipulation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 62. [A Calibrated Instrument for Detecting Disposition Drift in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25735) <a id="link62"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25735
**Authors:** Jerry Kaplan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25735v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Before language models are released, they are tested for harmful traits such as willingness to carry out dangerous requests, deception, power seeking, and sycophancy. The safe behavior these tests certify may degrade as models are modified, by topical fine-tuning, new training data, or changes to settings such as the depth of their reasoning. Most work reports such drift as single summary rates scored by another AI model, without the error analysis needed to interpret them. We adapt a calibrated measurement methodology recently developed for assessing how inference optimizations affect the quality of model outputs. Applying it to several misalignment dimensions in two open-weight models, we first establish the instrument's precision, showing that shifts of a few percentage points can be reliably detected. We then induce drift deliberately by fine-tuning on correct and on subtly harmful medical and financial advice; harmful advice induces a far greater shift than correct advice. Most strikingly, a model that reasons before answering can conceal the shift almost entirely in its answers, even though a direct reading of its internal state shows that its disposition has moved. With reasoning switched off, the same model shifts steadily as the share of harmful advice in its training grows. These results demonstrate the importance of testing safety with reasoning turned both on and off.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 11: studies AI alignment by measuring disposition drift in language models under fine-tuning, with a calibrated instrument detecting subtle shifts in harmful behavior and showing reasoning can conceal misalignment.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 63. [The Certification Exponent: Instance-Optimal Sequential Certification of Policy Improvement from History Dependent Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24345) <a id="link63"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24345
**Authors:** Rui Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24345v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Before a new policy replaces a deployed one, we often want a certificate, computed from logged episodes, that it is better. We ask how many episodes such a certificate needs when the logging policy is known but history-dependent, and the error probability must stay below $\delta$ in every environment consistent with the logs. The answer is an exponent $K^\star$: the smallest KL divergence from the logged law to a law with the same logged action probabilities under which the target does not improve. No sound certifier stops in fewer than $\log(1/\delta)/K^\star$ expected episodes, at any $\delta$, and whenever $0<\infty$ a plug-in Kelly certifier attains this rate to first order as $\delta\to0$. Coverage, computed by one backward induction, decides whether certification is impossible ($K^\star=0$), possible at a finite rate, or possible in a constant number of episodes ($K^\star=\infty$). By convex duality, the optimal certificate bets on the importance-weighted advantage and also places fair bets on the logged actions, so doubly robust control variates act as hedges rather than estimators. As a consequence, no choice of off-policy estimator changes $K^\star$, a coverage ceiling explains the curse of memory, and structural assumptions can be priced in exponent units. A cushion certifier extends the approach to trees too large to enumerate. In experiments the plug-in's median stopping time is 1.13-1.43x the lower bound on memory tasks, 1.13-1.51x on Tiger ($H\le5$) and 1.17-1.64x on random trees. The best scalar betting baselines need up to 2.65x (memory) and 4.1x (Tiger) the plug-in's episodes; only on a few shallow random trees is a doubly robust bet faster, by at most 18%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Develops an instance-optimal sequential certification method for policy improvement with guarantees based on KL divergence, a novel statistical test framework relevant to detecting policy improvements and potentially contamination in logged data.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 64. [TokenGate: Adapting LLMs for Fast and Memory-Efficient Prefill with Learned Token Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26754) <a id="link64"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26754
**Authors:** Liana Patel, Rishabh Ranjan, Ion Stoica, Matei Zaharia, Carlos Guestrin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26754v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Expert human readers process large inputs (e.g., code repositories and long documents) by skimming to closely read only what matters. On the other hand, language models process all input tokens through each model layer, resulting in significant processing costs for long-context inputs. We introduce TokenGate, a small, drop-in component that extends existing trained models to enable end-to-end learnable token pruning over long-context inputs for fast and memory-efficient prefill. To enable end-to-end learning despite the non-differentiable top-K operator for token pruning, we introduce token-gated attention, a mechanism inspired by Mixture-of-Experts that re-weights attention logits using token importance scores. Across diverse tasks, TokenGate achieves $1.4$-$11.3\times$ faster time-to-first token (TTFT) and $1.5$-$3.4\times$ lower peak memory at no accuracy degradation for prefill in transformers. Compared to the best-performing prior methods for token-pruning, TokenGate gives up to $6.4\times$ faster prefill and $2\times$ lower peak memory. TokenGate natively extends both standard transformers (e.g., Qwen3) and hybrid-attention models (e.g., Qwen3.5) with simple lightweight adaptation comparable to standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning. We make our code available at: https://github.com/tokengate-models/TokenGate.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes TokenGate, a learnable token pruning method for LLMs that improves efficiency during prefill with a novel token-gated attention mechanism. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following efficiency and model adaptation for long-context inputs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 65. [Strength Controls Change How Interventions Compare Across Sparse Autoencoder Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26489) <a id="link65"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26489
**Authors:** Jinchen Zhang, Zheyuan Liu, Yihan Zhu, Meng Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26489v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Changing sparse autoencoder (SAE) codes can alter both intervention direction and magnitude, so comparing intervention effects requires a strength control specifying what is held equal. Norm matching and output calibration provide different controls, but whether interventions yield different effects across codes after strength matching remains unclear. We compare intervention effects under these controls for fixed feature groups in Gemma and Llama and observe: (1) Whole-patch scaling. With one coefficient per input, full-output Kullback-Leibler (KL) calibration leaves smaller mean effect gaps from the encoder-based reference intervention than total-norm matching for nonnegative least-squares (NNLS) refits. On new Llama inputs, total-norm matching loses 11 reference transfers, whereas full-output calibration preserves every transfer decision. (2) Positionwise scaling. On a small matched gender-agreement subset, positionwise norm matching leaves smaller observed mean gaps than full-output calibration. (3) Answer exclusion. Compared with unscaled NNLS, answer-excluded calibration reduces gaps in the measured subject-verb agreement comparisons but increases them for gender refits. Full-output KL calibration also reduces larger gaps for norm-matched constructed candidates, although matching coverage is incomplete in several settings. Since calibration uses the evaluated input's outputs, it serves as a diagnostic of residual disagreement.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 closely: The paper studies sparse autoencoder codes and compares intervention effects under different strength controls, applied to models including Llama. It advances understanding of sparse autoencoder interventions in neural network interpretability.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 66. [Global Convergence in Deep Networks via the Second Law of Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24775) <a id="link66"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24775
**Authors:** Matan Tsipory, Ofir Gaash, Itamar Harel, Nathan Srebro, Daniel Soudry

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24775v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing convergence analyses of finite-width overparameterized deep neural networks under standard parameterization often rely on parameters remaining close to their initialization and typically neglect the role of noise, despite empirical evidence that stochasticity can improve optimization and generalization. To bridge this gap, we study $L_2$-regularized continuous-time Langevin dynamics (CLD) with isotropic noise, for training feedforward networks with smooth activations. We introduce a change-of-measure inequality, inspired by the second law of thermodynamics, that enables control of key properties of the training dynamics under the time-evolving parameter distribution. Leveraging a sharpened lower bound on the minimum eigenvalue of the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) at initialization, we show that injected noise helps preserve NTK stability with high probability without confining the weights to a small neighborhood of their initialization. As a result, we derive a non-asymptotic upper bound on the expected training loss. Our analysis yields explicit conditions on width and noise temperature that guarantee convergence to a finite loss floor. Finally, we experimentally show the network Jacobian can change from initialization, departing the commonly analyzed lazy regime.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: provides a theoretical analysis of deep network convergence using a thermodynamics-inspired inequality and noise effects on NTK stability, contributing to understanding scaling and training dynamics.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 67. [Co-Evolving Policy and Reward for Code Generation: A Mechanistic Study of Single-Adapter Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26416) <a id="link67"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26416
**Authors:** Amarsaikhan Batjargal, Noriko Tomuro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26416v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is particularly well suited to code generation because generated programs can be objectively evaluated through execution. Execution also provides behavioral information that can be reused as additional supervision. The Synergistic Policy and Reward Co-Evolving Framework (SPARK), originally proposed for reasoning tasks, exploits this idea by recycling training rollouts and their evaluation results to train a model not only to generate solutions but also to evaluate and improve them. We investigate whether this policy-reward co-evolution approach transfers effectively to code generation and whether execution-grounded reflection provides an additional source of self-improvement. We compare SPARK-style rollout recycling with execution-only Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) using Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct. Our results do not support either hypothesis under the configuration tested. Although co-evolution improves training performance, these gains do not generalize to held-out problems. Compared at the same stage of training, rollout recycling produces roughly 34x greater KL divergence from the reference policy than execution-only GRPO. Over training, trained tokens per GRPO sequence fall from 185 to 86 as training pass rate increases, while held-out performance declines or fails to sustain early gains. We characterize these patterns as single-adapter policy drift: the shared trainable parameters increasingly depart from pretrained code-generation behavior without corresponding gains in generalization. The results show that higher training reward alone is not evidence of generalizable code self-improvement and highlight the importance of held-out evaluation and drift diagnostics.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper studies reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and policy-reward co-evolution for code generation, which relates to RLHF and instruction-following improvements. It analyzes policy drift and generalization issues in RLHF-like training, providing mechanistic insights into fine-tuning steps to improve instruction-following models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 68. [Cognitive Map in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25596) <a id="link68"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25596
**Authors:** Albert Qin, Jonathan Cohen, Gautam Reddy

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25596v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: It has long been proposed that animals construct cognitive maps to support flexible behavior in novel environments. We tested whether large language models form similar representations using relational reasoning tasks and causal "mosaic experiments" that manipulate internal activations in open-weight language models. Models beyond the ten-billion-parameter scale successfully inferred unstated relationships between entities and generalized to synthetic entity names. Across model and task families, models generated abstract representations of relational structure and combined them additively with representations of entity identity, binding entities to roles within that structure. Models stored observed relationships as pairwise links that they composed retrospectively when queried, rather than prospectively positioning each entity in a global coordinate system. Binding was mediated by a sparse, distributed set of key--value pairs; by perturbing these pairs, we implanted "false memories" that altered model behavior in predictable ways. Overall, our findings reveal an internal organization analogous to a cognitive map that explains how language models reason with limited information.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 8: Demonstrates mechanistic interpretability techniques by analyzing internal representations and sparse key-value bindings in large language models, revealing cognitive map-like structures explaining relational reasoning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 69. [Copying Under Repetition: From Prefix Matching to Position Hashing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26722) <a id="link69"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26722
**Authors:** Anxin Guo, Tianlong Huang, Chenxiao Yang, Zhiyuan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26722v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Frontier LLMs can copy random text almost flawlessly, yet they often overrun or truncate long blocks of repeated characters. In repeated strings, matching local content no longer reliably identifies where copying should continue or when it should stop. Nevertheless, we show that a two-layer transformer with $O(\log\log N)$-bit precision and $O(\log N/\log\log N)$ width can copy every string up to a chosen design horizon $N$ and terminate correctly under arbitrary repetition. We use RoPE-style rotations of attention values and outputs (VO-rotations) with small prime periods to encode distances from delimiter anchors modulo those primes. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, these periodic encodings jointly distinguish all required distances, allowing one retrieval rule to select either the next source token or the end marker. In contrast, we prove a width-independent impossibility result for copying under sequential finite-precision softmax accumulation. It applies to transformers whose only positional input is an additive attention bias that eventually becomes constant, including NoPE and a recursive implementation of ALiBi. Once an identical-token run exceeds the resulting saturation threshold, further repetitions leave the logits unchanged. If this threshold grows sublinearly with $N$, these models cannot both complete a long all-identical copy and stop correctly, a pattern resembling the termination errors of frontier LLMs. In synthetic copying experiments, baseline transformer models fail on long repeated strings even within the training range. Trained VO models, by contrast, generalize beyond the training lengths with near-perfect accuracy up to length $\approx 5005$, the selected primes' period product.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12 (partially) and 8 (somewhat): The paper analyzes transformer mechanisms for copying repeated sequences, proving impossibility results for certain positional encodings and proposing a novel VO-rotation method. This is a mechanistic interpretability and theoretical analysis of transformer behavior relevant to understanding internal model algorithms and reasoning about sequence generation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 70. [SWIFT: Self-guided Weak-to-strong dIFfusion models via Target extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25844) <a id="link70"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25844
**Authors:** Yanjin He, Cuifeng Shen, Wanzhou Lei, Maohua Li, Tao Lan, Kan Liu, Hanlin Tang, Qing Qu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25844v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training high-quality diffusion models is computationally expensive, and inference-time guidance often adds further sampling cost. In this work, we introduce Swift, a simple yet effective self-guided training method that uses a frozen weak denoiser to improve a stronger model without adding inference overhead. For each noisy input, Swift extrapolates the clean regression target away from the weak denoiser's prediction, amplifying components that remain underfit. The weak guide can be an early training checkpoint, requires no backpropagation during main-model training, and is discarded afterward. The resulting model retains the original sampler and supports both conditional and unconditional generation without label dropout or an unconditional branch. We explain this mechanism through a spectral analysis of finite-time population gradient flow in an RKHS. With an early-stopped weak guide, we prove that a nonempty range of extrapolation strengths yields strictly lower posterior-mean error than matched standard training, despite shifting the regression target away from the posterior mean. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-256 demonstrate improvements across model scales and representations. In one ImageNet-256 configuration, Swift surpasses the baseline's best FID using roughly half the total training compute, including guide preparation and training overhead. On RAEv2, it achieves FID 1.15 without inference-time guidance and 0.99 with guidance. These results establish target extrapolation as an effective approach to weak-to-strong diffusion training with efficient single-model inference.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new method (Swift) for training diffusion language models with improved efficiency and performance, including theoretical analysis and empirical results on image datasets (though not explicitly language models, the method is about diffusion models and generative modeling).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 71. [ACE-Compression: An Ability-Centric Evaluation of Compressed Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27913) <a id="link71"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27913
**Authors:** Jiahui Ning, Liang Chen, Yuchen Liang, Yi Zhang, Fenglei Fan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27913v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Model compression makes large language models deployable, but today's evaluation tells an incomplete story. Standard practice measures retained task performance and stops there. This misses a critical fact: compression perturbs the distributed knowledge embedded across billions of weights, and task scores alone cannot reveal which underlying abilities survive and which quietly collapse. To solve this question, we introduce ACE-Compression, an ability-centric evaluation framework. Instead of asking only "does the compressed model still answer correctly?", we ask "which of its core abilities remain intact?" Our framework probes four deployment-facing dimensions: procedural reasoning and constraint execution, knowledge grounding and truthfulness, social cognition and affective understanding, and persona fidelity and stylistic consistency. Across more than 35 compressed checkpoints from six open-weight LLMs-covering quantization and pruning-we find that compression redistributes ability profiles. Coarse, local, and surface-level signals often persist, while fine-grained, long-tail, and context-dependent abilities become fragile. Quantization consistently outperforms pruning in retention, but the practical meaning of that gap depends entirely on which ability an application requires. Based on our findings, deployers can choose compression methods by the ability profile their application needs, not just by a leaderboard average. And algorithm designers gain diagnostic signals to build compression methods that preserve what matters. We believe that ACE-Compression turns compression from a black-box accuracy drop into an interpretable intervention-one we can measure, understand, and ultimately improve.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 5: introduces ACE-Compression, an ability-centric evaluation framework for compressed LLMs that probes multiple core abilities beyond task accuracy, providing new diagnostic insights into real-world usage and safety properties of compressed language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 72. [Trackable Skill Library Lifecycle Management for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25126) <a id="link72"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25126
**Authors:** Xi Zhu, Yuanlin Duan, Changyi Yang, Kai Mei, Riyang Bao, Shichao Zhu, Dimitris N. Metaxas

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25126v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. Despite this progress, individual skills may follow distinct trajectories as the policy evolves, with some becoming redundant after the model internalizes their capabilities, others remaining valuable as external scaffolding, and still others turning harmful. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available at https://github.com/xizhu1022/TrackRL.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 1: proposes a new agentic RL framework (TrackRL) for skill lifecycle management in reinforcement learning with LLM agents, involving randomized controlled experiments to isolate skill contributions, which is a novel fine-tuning and evaluation methodology for instruction-following agents.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 73. [CAVA: Complementary-Label-Aware Data Valuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26734) <a id="link73"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26734
**Authors:** Tan-Ha Mai, Hsuan-Tien Lin, Vu Nguyen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26734v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Complementary-label learning (CLL) trains a classifier from labels that name a class an instance does not belong to. Such labels are cheap to collect from crowdworkers or vision-language models (VLMs), making them attractive for large-scale annotation. However, both sources can mistakenly assign the true class as the complementary label. Such annotation errors are particularly harmful in CLL because the model is explicitly trained to suppress the correct answer. Optimal transport (OT) data valuation identifies harmful samples by comparing training data with a clean validation set, but transferring it directly to CLL creates a semantic mismatch: label $c$ means not class $c$ in the training but class $c$ in the validation. We show that this makes the OT label groups noisy mixtures whose behavior depends on the noise rate. The transferred score is informative only at sufficiently high noise, becomes uninformative at the crossover rate $1/M$ for $M$ classes, and reverses below it, where practical CLL annotation typically operates. We propose CAVA, which introduces a complementary label cost and a label margin that isolates the contribution of each complementary label to the transport potential. The margin keeps a consistent direction across noise rates, detects diagonal noise with a parameter-free threshold of closed-form precision, and allows detected samples to be recycled as ordinary-label supervision. On synthetic and human-annotated benchmarks, CAVA separates diagonal noise almost perfectly where directly transferred OT valuation performs worse than random. With only a fraction of the clean validation data, it also improves every CLL objective we evaluate, showing that OT-based valuation becomes reliable once the semantics of complementary supervision are modeled explicitly.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: Proposes CAVA, a novel data valuation method for complementary-label learning that explicitly models label semantics to detect annotation noise, improving data-centric AI approaches for training robust classifiers.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 74. [Adaptive Decoding Methods for Token-Efficient Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25860) <a id="link74"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25860
**Authors:** Zhaonan Li, Zelei Cheng, Amritansh Mishra, Chia-Hsuan Lee, Mann Patel, Sambit Sahu, Ben Zhou, William M. Campbell

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25860v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rollout decoding affects both which trajectories are used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and how many completion tokens are spent collecting them. We study whether candidate-token support should remain fixed or widen during training in the context of rollout sampling efficiency. Our main schedule moves from top-K=10 to K=20 and then to full support as the attempted-token budget increases. In a 4B code-RLVR setting, the schedule has a higher interpolated 150M-token validation value than its matched fixed-K=10 continuation in all three runs, with a mean gain of $1.80{\pm}0.42$ points. The schedule also performs comparably to a separately trained fixed-K=20 baseline, the strongest fixed-support setting in our sweep. Follow-up experiments suggest that late widening to full support is the most promising variant, although the evidence remains noisy, and mixed-reward-group diagnostics do not clearly explain the gain. An exploratory single-run test replay yields identical point estimates for the schedule and fixed $K=10$. Overall, our results show that rollout support is an important control for sampling efficiency in RLVR and motivate further study of adaptive support schedules.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper studies adaptive decoding methods to improve token efficiency in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following and RLHF-like training.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 75. [Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25760) <a id="link75"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25760
**Authors:** Pedram Mouseli, Massieh Moayedi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25760v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. The state that can be decoded without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: The paper studies chain-of-thought reasoning and monitoring in LLM agents, analyzing how reasoning at each step affects actions and rule-following, which relates to reasoning transparency and chain-of-thought monitoring.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 76. [Sample-Efficient Reinforcement Learning with General Utilities via Neural Dual Critics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24880) <a id="link76"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24880
**Authors:** Ankur Naskar, Vaneet Aggarwal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24880v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with general utilities optimizes nonlinear functionals of policy-induced occupancy measures. We develop a natural actor-critic method for concave RLGU objectives admitting an exact variational dual representation, with explicit constructions for a broad class of separable occupancy utilities. Rather than estimating the occupancy measure and then forming the policy-gradient pseudo-reward, our method learns this pseudo-reward directly as a function of the state-action pair using a neural critic. The critic is trained by projected stochastic updates, while multilevel Monte Carlo combines outputs across training horizons to control its bias at logarithmic expected cost. Our analysis separates conditional actor bias from estimator second moments and accounts explicitly for dual optimization, critic approximation, and neural linearization errors. Under policy-transfer, dual-stability, and local critic-regularity conditions, the method achieves $\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})$ global convergence above policy, critic-class, and finite-width approximation errors using $\widetilde{O}(T)$ expected environment transitions and reference samples. For a fixed critic representation, this yields $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$ sampling complexity above the approximation floor, covering infinite state-action spaces and occupancy utilities beyond fixed finite vectors of returns.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new natural actor-critic method for reinforcement learning with general utilities, involving neural critics and policy-gradient improvements, relevant to RLHF methodological improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 77. [When Opposite Premises Translate Identically: A Source-Conditioned Diagnostic for Multilingual Commonsense Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24428) <a id="link77"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24428
**Authors:** Shuai Wang, Rui Hu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24428v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multilingual reasoning comparisons depend on translated premises preserving the relations in their English sources. Automatic translation scores can rank source-target pairs, but an acceptance decision alone cannot certify a counterfactual benchmark field. We test a source-contrast diagnostic for ACCORD premises: reverse the English polarity, translate both arms independently, count identical target outputs, and score each arm against its own source. Across 71 matched question identities at two containing-item depths, five target languages, and three translation checkpoints, Swahili has 63, 113, and 67 exact collisions among 710 pairs per checkpoint. At a human-reference-calibrated threshold, round-trip NLI passes both arms of 3 of the 113 NLLB-3.3B collisions, while LaBSE passes 78. Each collision proves at least one unfaithful translation, but does not identify its arm. These controlled measurements do not establish screening accuracy on spontaneous translation errors or a language-by-hop reasoning effect; the study has no native equivalence labels or answer-model evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2 (test set contamination). This paper proposes a source-conditioned diagnostic method to detect unfaithful translations and potential contamination or leakage in multilingual benchmarks by analyzing identical translations of opposite premises. It provides a novel statistical diagnostic for contamination or benchmark fidelity in multilingual language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 78. [Beyond Prediction: A Component-Level Study of Concept Recovery in In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23936) <a id="link78"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23936
**Authors:** Xufeng Liu, Fang Wan, Yi Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23936v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables language models to make accurate predictions from demonstrations, but prediction success alone does not reveal the extent to which the underlying concept has been recovered. Existing evaluations typically assess recovery at the level of the whole concept, making it difficult to identify which components are recovered and to separate model limitations from ambiguity in the demonstrations. We introduce a component-level framework for concept recovery that decomposes concepts into their defining components and uses controlled structural interventions to evaluate both component-level and whole-concept recovery under identifiable demonstrations. Across four instruction-tuned LLMs and three composition forms, we find that models often achieve high prediction accuracy despite poor whole-concept recovery, with systematic over-specification of relevant attributes and level constraints. Individual structural components are generally insufficient for whole-concept recovery, whereas jointly specifying complementary components, particularly relevant attributes and their level constraints, substantially improves recovery. However, this improvement is fragile: a single plausible but unnecessary level constraint can substantially disrupt recovery. Finally, joint supervision of recovery and implicit prediction substantially improves recovery while preserving prediction performance, with the learned capability transferring across composition forms. Overall, our component-level analysis provides a more diagnostic view of what models recover from demonstrations than the existing holistic view.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: provides a component-level analysis framework for concept recovery in instruction-tuned LLMs, offering new insights into instruction-following and implicit concept learning, which can inform fine-tuning and instruction-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 79. [Progress-Guided Preference Generation for Aligning Generative Control Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25692) <a id="link79"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25692
**Authors:** Tung M. Luu, Tri Ton, Duc M. Nguyen, Xuan Thanh Nguyen, Nguyen Gia Binh, Quang Tan Nguyen, Vu Duong, Daniel Sonntag, Duy M. H. Nguyen, Vien Anh Ngo, Chang D. Yoo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25692v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative policies have achieved strong performance in imitation learning tasks, but their success is often constrained by the limited availability of expert demonstrations. Although preference-based fine-tuning can leverage suboptimal rollouts to improve performance, existing approaches heavily rely on expensive human annotations. In this work, we introduce Progress-Guided Preference Generation (PGPG), an unsupervised preference alignment framework that eliminates the need for human-in-the-loop supervision. Our method leverages ordered coverage alignment to measure sequential progress relative to a small set of expert demonstrations, enabling the automatic extraction of pseudo-preference labels from autonomously collected rollouts. Furthermore, we introduce key enhancements to improve the reliability of these progress-guided preferences, enabling effective fine-tuning of pretrained generative policies without requiring manually designed reward functions. Extensive experiments across a suite of challenging long-horizon manipulation tasks demonstrate that PGPG outperforms standard supervised fine-tuning baselines. Moreover, our conceptually simple framework is broadly applicable across multiple classes of generative policies, demonstrating strong versatility when integrated with both diffusion and flow-matching policies.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: introduces a method (PGPG) applicable to fine-tuning generative policies including diffusion and flow-matching policies, showing improvements in generative control policies. Relevant to diffusion language models and generative modeling.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 80. [From Unverifiable Domain Text to Cross-Lingual RLVR via Code-Switched Task Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25399) <a id="link80"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25399
**Authors:** Cheonbok Park, Jihwan Kim, Shinyoung Joo, Joosung Lee, Hwiyeol Jo, Jeonghoon Kim, Jaegul Choo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25399v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) can improve reasoning in large language models, but specialized non-English domains expose a data mismatch. Raw English domain text is often available, while target-language RLVR tasks with verifiable rewards are scarce. We introduce code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR, a data-centric approach that derives target-language and code-switched RLVR task variants from otherwise unverifiable English domain text. Using fill-in-the-middle multiple-choice QA as a seed task format, we synthesize automatically verifiable tasks from domain passages and reshape them into cross-lingual variants. These variants preserve the same evidence-answer relation but change how English and target-language evidence are presented during RLVR. On Korean medical benchmarks and cybersecurity benchmarks across four target languages, code-switched training outperforms matched monolingual and bilingual-mixture baselines on average target-language performance while maintaining comparable English performance. Additional analyses provide evidence of better target-language input understanding and stronger cross-lingual alignment in both model outputs and internal representations. These results suggest that code-switched training helps target-language inputs access domain reasoning learned more strongly in English.

**Comment:** Proposes code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards), a data-centric approach to improve reasoning in LLMs. This relates to RLHF/instruction-following improvements (criterion 1) via verifiable reward signals and task synthesis.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 81. [PORA: Learning When More Reasoning Pays Off for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27119) <a id="link81"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27119
**Authors:** Bo Yan, Qi Zhang, Zhen Tan, Tianlong Chen, Song Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27119v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents perform tasks over multiple steps and revise their actions based on feedback. As the total reasoning budget is often limited and must support future steps, the agent must choose between short and long reasoning before each action. Longer reasoning may improve the final task outcome by preventing later errors, but it also uses budget capacity that may be needed for future decisions. This creates two challenges: estimating the downstream value of long reasoning and deciding when that value is worth using the remaining budget. We propose Paired Outcome-guided Reasoning Allocation (PORA), which learns this decision by comparing short and long reasoning from the same interaction state. During training, \method{} completes the task twice from each selected state, once with a short reasoning allowance and once with a long allowance, and compares their final quality and total processing to estimate when longer reasoning is valuable. During inference, a controller weighs this estimate against learned prices on remaining-budget pressure and chooses either short or long reasoning without running both alternatives. We evaluate PORA across application, memory, code, retail, and mathematics tasks, achieving strong results across all settings. On BFCL memory, it exceeds the strongest existing method by 17.4--32.9 percentage points. Within the same agent, it matches or exceeds always using long reasoning on code and mathematics with 50.6% fewer actor tokens and 31.2% fewer model requests, respectively.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Proposes a method (PORA) to allocate reasoning budget dynamically in LLM agents, improving reasoning efficiency and outcomes, which relates to reasoning capabilities.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 82. [Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768) <a id="link82"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25768
**Authors:** Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, Lingyao Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25768v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, specifically about truncated reasoning and decision-making models that improve reasoning efficiency and accuracy trade-offs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 83. [Do LMs Know What Variables Do? Interpreting Code Representations via Variable Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24118) <a id="link83"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24118
**Authors:** C\'elian Vasson, Daniil Ognev, Kentaro Inui, Benjamin Heinzerling

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24118v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Variable roles describe recurring behaviors such as accumulation, traversal, and retaining the best candidate encountered. We use Sajaniemi's independently defined taxonomy to study which role distinctions can be recovered from large language models, how sparse autoencoders (SAEs) retain those distinctions, and whether associated directions support control of generation. We introduce static annotation rules for all eleven roles and a corpus of 126,926 natural-code files across five languages. Across five model-SAE systems, residual probes outperform individual SAE features and tested lexical baselines, with modest losses after neutral target renaming. Combining SAE features closes much of the single-feature gap, but probes on SAE reconstructions are less sensitive to role-changing edits reviewed by LLMs. High paired ranking accuracy coexists with frequent false positives at fixed thresholds. During generation, interventions often change role annotations at the cost of correctness; changes accompanied by passing tests show no clear aggregate advantage over random controls. The benchmark separates role-related recoverability, SAE fidelity, and correctness-preserving control through matched prediction, source edits, and generation interventions.

**Comment:** This paper uses sparse autoencoders and probes to interpret code representations in LLMs, studying variable roles and control of generation. It relates to criterion 7 (sparse autoencoders for interpretability) and partially to criterion 8 (mechanistic interpretability).
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 84. [Statistical Learning Theory for Direct Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25856) <a id="link84"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25856
**Authors:** Joel Wills, Yuanfan Li, Yiming Ying, Ding-Xuan Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25856v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) learns from pairwise preferences through a logistic objective whose score-difference parameterization follows the Bradley--Terry model. We study how failure of this modeling assumption limits DPO, distinguishing structural misspecification from limited model capacity and finite-sample estimation. Our main contribution is a characterization of structural approximation error through the triangle-curl of the preference log-odds, which vanishes exactly when preferences admit a Bradley--Terry representation. We upper bound the achievable excess logistic risk by a score-class approximation term and a triangle-curl term, and establish a lower bound proportional to triangle-curl under additional boundedness assumptions. For ReLU score models, including clipped models, we derive explicit approximation rates showing that increasing capacity can eliminate the score-approximation term while leaving the structural obstruction unchanged. We further establish finite-sample bounds for clipped empirical risk minimizers that separate network approximation, structural misspecification, and estimation error. We place these results within a surrogate-risk framework by identifying contextual preference accuracy (CPA) as a natural measure of agreement between preference judgments and changes in pairwise probability ratios relative to the reference policy. Standard logistic consistency and calibration results connect DPO's objective to this policy property and transfer our excess-risk upper bounds to CPA, without treating CPA as a complete measure of generated-response quality.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: The paper studies Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a method related to learning from pairwise preferences, which is relevant to RLHF and instruction-following fine-tuning methods. It analyzes structural misspecification and approximation errors in preference learning, which is a methodological improvement in preference-based optimization relevant to instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 85. [RACE: RoPE-Aligned Attention Compression Based on Query-Key Activation Energies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28385) <a id="link85"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28385
**Authors:** Irene Tenison, Abdulrahman Alabdulkareem, Daniel Karl I. Weidele, Nima Dehmamy, Dan Gutfreund

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28385v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Head-dimension compression can reduce the memory required by the key-value (KV) cache, but existing methods often reconstruct full-dimensional representations during inference, adding significant decoding FLOPs overhead. We formalize the design considerations for converting pretrained attention into a natively reduced-dimensional form that reduces both KV-cache storage and attention computation using a calibration set. Specifically, we consider reductions that (1) apply a shared projection to queries and keys so that they remain in a common reduced feature space, (2) derive this projection jointly from query and key activations to better preserve their interaction, and (3) are compatible with the pretrained RoPE transformation. To obtain a reconstruction-free realization of this reduction, we restrict it to coordinate selection and theoretically show that RoPE compatibility then holds if and only if complete rotary pairs are retained. Building on this characterization, we introduce RACE, a post-training method that selects rotary pairs using their joint query-key activation energy. We theoretically show that this criterion minimizes a separable, calibration-dependent upper bound on post-RoPE query-key product approximation error. The resulting reductions are folded into the model weights and require neither runtime reconstruction nor specialized kernels. Across three model families and different compression levels, RACE outperforms structured pruning and remains competitive with reconstruction-based methods on language-modeling, zero-shot, and long-context tasks. At 50% KV-cache retention and 256K context, RACE reduces total inference FLOPs by 47.8%, yielding 1.79x higher throughput, 43.2% lower time-to-first-token (TTFT), and 28.1% lower allocated GPU memory in comparison to an uncompressed model.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes RACE, a method for compressing RoPE-aligned attention in language models by selecting rotary pairs based on query-key activation energies. This is a novel method improving efficiency of attention computation in LLMs, related to continuous diffusion-like transformations in language models (RoPE is a positional encoding related to continuous transformations). While not explicitly a diffusion LM paper, it involves continuous transformations and compression in LLMs, which is relevant to generative modeling and efficiency improvements.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 86. [When Can LLM Reasoning Reliably Reuse Intermediate Results?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27662) <a id="link86"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27662
**Authors:** Deyuan Liu, Zecheng Wang, Xuanyi Liu, Jungang Li, Yihao Hu, Mingjie Tang, Bryan Dai, Jie Fu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27662v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sampling complete solutions gives large language models more chances to solve a problem, but each attempt starts again. For tasks with several subproblems, all required answers must be correct in the same attempt. Retaining intermediate results can avoid repeating correct work, but errors in those results may affect later attempts. We study when this reuse is reliable and how much computation it requires. For local retries that stop when no candidate passes within the retry budget, we give conditions for correct completion and bound the average number of attempts. We also show how checking cost and the chance of passing a check affect total computation. Experiments across eight reasoning models examine composite mathematics and arithmetic. Across four DeepSeek models, average success on the second subproblem is 36.2 percent without first-subproblem context, 57.2 percent with its answer, and 58.3 percent with its reasoning and answer. These scores measure second-subproblem coverage and include continuations made when selection fails. Arithmetic experiments show that similar first-attempt accuracies can lead to very different coverage after repeated attempts. Code is available at https://github.com/SempraETY/Intermediate-State-Reuse.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, specifically studying reuse of intermediate results in multi-step reasoning and analyzing conditions for reliable reuse and computational cost.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 87. [When Do Repetition Loops Lock? Truncation Sampling, Bounded Penalties, and a Blind Spot in DRY](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24715) <a id="link87"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24715
**Authors:** Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24715v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models sometimes repeat verbatim until the generation limit. We study the exit rather than the entrance: given that a span has already been repeated $n$ times, when is escape still possible? Along the loop the context is deterministic, so loop survival under any sampler can be computed exactly from one forward pass. Pure sampling leaves a positive escape probability at every step; top-$p$ with threshold $\pi>1/2$ locks a position exactly when the continuation probability reaches $\pi$. A bounded penalty can leave a position locked; a penalty that outgrows the model's preference for the loop over unseen tokens guarantees escape under top-$p$. The same analysis predicts a blind spot in the widely deployed DRY sampler: its sequence breakers cap the penalty at $\lambda b^{g-1-a}$ for loops whose longest breaker-free run has $g$ tokens. On eleven open models, 66\% of locked units with $g=6$ stay locked under DRY and 1\% with $g=8$. In free generation with llama.cpp, all 109 loops that survive DRY have $g\le 7$. We propose DRY-C, which adds a term that grows exponentially with the number of verbatim recurrences, up to its look-back window. With it, none of 720 structured-output generations looped to the length limit, and task completion stayed close to DRY's.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4 closely: The paper fundamentally analyzes repetition loops and sampling methods in language model generation, revealing a blind spot in the DRY sampler and proposing DRY-C to prevent infinite loops. This is a new paradigm in understanding and evaluating open-ended text generation behavior and sampling dynamics.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 88. [Concerns in Benchmarking Jailbreaks: An Empirical Study of Attack Success Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26595) <a id="link88"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26595
**Authors:** Leon Tu Nguyen, Rohan Menon, Rafael Tappe Maestro, Eric Aubinais, Chih-Hong Cheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26595v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19: Empirical study on jailbreak attack success rates and evaluation procedures for language models, highlighting probabilistic suppression of unsafe behavior and recommending robust evaluation protocols for security of LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 89. [World Model Interpretability: From Decoding Representations to Understanding Latent Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25296) <a id="link89"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25296
**Authors:** Jize Wang, Yongcheng Jing, Xinyi Le, Cailian Chen, Xinping Guan, Dacheng Tao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25296v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Emerging Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are moving beyond representation learning towards latent world models that support prediction, planning, and control. Yet their latent worlds naturally lack a human-interpretable interface. In this Perspective, we argue that as JEPA latents become internal states for prediction and action, interpretability must move beyond what they encode to how latent variables interact, evolve, and causally drive prediction. To this end, we envision a four-stage research agenda, involving Ground, Structure, Verify, and Act, to guide this shift toward actionable latent-world understanding. Within our framework, existing JEPA interpretability efforts provide early evidence for Ground, while systematic analysis of latent structure, causal verification, and intervention remains largely unexplored. Overall, this Perspective aims to call for a broader latent-world interpretability agenda that shifts attention from decoding representations toward understanding, validating, and acting on latent worlds.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Discusses mechanistic interpretability of latent world models in joint-embedding predictive architectures, focusing on understanding latent variables' interactions and causal roles, relevant to mechanistic interpretability of neural networks.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 90. [Indistinguishable Agents: Training Agent Privacy as Twin-World Indistinguishability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25161) <a id="link90"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25161
**Authors:** Jiaming He, Guanyu Hou, Yi Yu, Xudong Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25161v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents search, shop and send messages for their users, and every action is seen by some party. Existing privacy training rewards agents for not mentioning protected items, yet an observer can infer a private fact from what the agent does. Since an observer learns a fact only if its view changes with the fact, we propose \methodfull (\method), which trains on twin worlds that differ only in the private fact. A frozen distinguisher scores each observer's view by its log-likelihood ratio for the true world, and paired GRPO trains the agent on task success minus this ratio. We prove that this objective controls the Bayes success of every observer on every channel. On shopping and messaging tasks \method removes the leakage that disclosure judges and query-level rewards miss, while the agent still completes its tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Presents a new method (Twin-World Indistinguishability) for training agents to remove private information leakage, effectively a form of machine unlearning or forgetting specific knowledge while preserving task performance.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 91. [How Fine-Tuning Objectives Shape Layer-Wise Information in LLM Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27711) <a id="link91"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27711
**Authors:** Kaiyang Wan, Forrest Sheng Bao, Amin Ahmad, Yuxia Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27711v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Under matched conditions, classifier-head fine-tuning (\textsc{cls}) paradigm consistently outperforms the LLM-as-Judge sequence-to-sequence (\textsc{s2s}) on binary hallucination detection. We trace this gap to the representations induced by the two objectives. Static analysis shows that \textsc{cls} learns a task-specific discriminative direction, pushing hidden states into a clear two-cluster geometry with stronger class separation and linearly decodable label information. By contrast, \textsc{s2s} relies on the pretrained language-modeling head to make decisions. This minimizes the pressure to reorganize representations, leaving the hidden states much closer to the pretrained backbone. Our analysis also reveals that the \textsc{cls} signal saturates before the final layer, after which upper layers form a plateau of statistically interchangeable and largely redundant representations. However, endpoint redundancy alone does not show whether these layers are dispensable during training. Further training-dynamics analysis reveals 1) a lower pretrained substrate, 2) a narrow upper-middle \textbf{InfoWindow} where task information emerges, and 3) a later-synchronizing upper plateau that mirrors this signal. This mechanism motivates us to remove layers above the InfoWindow. Truncating 31\% of the network leaves accuracy unchanged within seed noise on Qwen3-4B and 8B and cuts latency by $1.4\times$. The same depth fraction, fixed before any analysis of the target models, transfers to Llama-3.2-3B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, and the layer structure behind it reappears there and on QNLI and SST-2. We hope this helps guide efficient task-specific LLM design.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 8: provides detailed mechanistic interpretability insights into how fine-tuning objectives shape layer-wise information and representations in LLMs, including analysis of task-specific discriminative directions and layer redundancy.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 92. [MisleadQA: Diagnosing LLM Failures under Misleading Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24851) <a id="link92"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24851
**Authors:** Hui Wang, Bohao Chu, Qianli Wang, Shuning Zhang, Norbert Fuhr

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24851v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to answer questions from retrieved or user-provided context. However, provided context can be factually true yet out-of-scope, describing a different entity, time, or location from the one the question targets, and still lead models to a wrong answer. Prior work has mainly studied factually false or irrelevant context. It remains unclear whether such out-of-scope context misleads models as much as false context, and whether adding gold context prevents models from being misled by either. We introduce **MisleadQA**, a diagnostic dataset of multiple-choice questions with an abstention option. Each question pairs gold context (G) with true but out-of-scope context (S) and false but in-scope context (F) supporting the same wrong answer. We evaluate six open-weight LLMs. On questions they answer correctly without context, providing S alone leads them to switch to this wrong answer in 29.3% of cases, and providing F alone does so in 75.4%. Adding G nearly removes the effect of S, but F still misleads models in 21.0% of cases, especially when F comes first. A context-faithful instruction reduces these errors mainly through abstention and lowers accuracy even with G. These findings suggest that evaluations should check whether context applies to the question, not only whether it is true, and should report correct, misled, and abstention rates together. Data and code are available at [https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MisleadQA](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MisleadQA).

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: The paper introduces MisleadQA, a diagnostic dataset analyzing LLM failures under misleading context, providing new data on real-world LLM usage and safety properties.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 93. [Hidden Cartographer: Hidden State Guided Data Selection for Dual Budgeted Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25215) <a id="link93"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25215
**Authors:** Zixuan Qin, Qi Qin, Erbo Li, Ting Wei, Yifan Sun, Zhaoxin Fan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25215v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Given the high cost of expert annotation and limited fine-tuning resources, effective data selection from large unlabeled pools under strict budgets has become a crucial step in adapting LLMs to specialized domains. Existing label-dependent methods risk distorted selection when using pseudo-labels, while traditional active learning methods do not explicitly characterize instruction-tuning dynamics. To address these limitations, we propose Hidden Cartographer (HC), a two-stage framework that uses hidden states to guide annotation and exact training dynamics to select the final training subset. The first stage is Progressive Candidate Expansion, which utilizes the proxy to iteratively select top unlabeled candidates for expert annotation, merging them to recompute training dynamics and update the proxy until the annotation budget is exhausted; the second stage is Exact Cartography Pruning, which ranks these annotated candidates by their exact dynamics scores to isolate a compact subset under the final training budget. Experiments across the medical, legal, and financial domains demonstrate that HC achieves the highest average accuracy among all budgeted baselines, requiring annotation for only 20--30% of the original pool and fine-tuning on just 10--15%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Presents a novel data selection method (Hidden Cartographer) for instruction tuning under budget constraints, using hidden states and exact training dynamics to improve instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 94. [CasePlay: Self-Play Reinforcement Learning from Case Reports for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24952) <a id="link94"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24952
**Authors:** Hao Wang, Zihan Wang, Kai Wu, Jian Yao, Yiwen Ye, Yongcan Luo, Dapeng Wu, Jiale Chen, Haihua Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24952v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a promising way to improve the clinical reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), but current pipelines often rely on expert-labeled questions or costly synthetic-data generation. Meanwhile, the medical literature offers a wealth of high-quality unlabeled documents, including clinical case reports that trace the full clinical course from initial presentation to final treatment decisions. We study whether document-grounded self-play can convert these reports into effective RL training signals. We identify two challenges that limit existing self-play methods in this setting: knowledge-point fixation}, where the proposer repeatedly asks about a narrow set of salient clues in a source report, and difficulty-quality mismatch, where appropriately difficult questions may still be poorly formed or shallow, limiting their value for reasoner training. To address these challenges, we propose CasePlay, a self-play framework with two key components: (a) a Knowledge-Conditioned Proposer, which anchors question generation to case-specific knowledge points so that questions draw on a broader range of evidence from the source report; and (b) a Rubric-Judge Reasoner, which augments answer-accuracy feedback with rubric-based quality feedback on clue integration, reasoning depth, and option quality, guiding the proposer toward reasoning-intensive questions that better support reasoner training. Extensive experiments across seven medically related benchmarks demonstrate that CasePlay outperforms existing self-play baselines and improves the SFT warm-start model by 3.1 points on average, highlighted by a 6.3-point gain on LiveClin. This approach offers an effective path toward continually improving medical reasoning ability through self-play RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new self-play reinforcement learning framework (CasePlay) to improve instruction-following and reasoning in LLMs, with specific fine-tuning steps and feedback mechanisms.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6
