## 95. [Beyond the Teacher: Understanding and Preserving Distillation Overshoot in Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25034) <a id="link95"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25034
**Authors:** Jike Zhong, Ming Li, Yuxiang Lai, Xi Xiao, Sheng Zhang, Ritwick Chaudhry, Tianle Chen, Jingyu Xie, Zheda Mai, Shao-Yuan Lo, Konstantinos Psounis, Yuanyuan Lei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25034v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains agents on their own trajectories using supervision from a stronger teacher. We uncover a counterintuitive phenomenon in multi-turn agentic tasks: a smaller, initially weaker student can substantially outperform its teacher during training, only to regress as distillation continues. We term this phenomenon distillation overshoot. Through decision- and task-level analyses, we trace overshoot to two competing effects of OPD: repairing weak student decisions and eroding successful student choices that the teacher disfavors. Early in training, repair outpaces erosion, allowing the student to combine the teacher's corrections with its own strengths and surpass the teacher. As training continues, erosion overtakes repair, causing these gains to vanish. Motivated by this finding, we propose Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation (SPOD), which selectively masks conflicting teacher supervision on successful student choices and introduces a soft probability floor to counteract their erosion, while retaining teacher guidance elsewhere. SPOD requires only binary episode success and model probabilities already available during OPD. Across two model families and three agentic benchmarks, SPOD consistently outperforms ordinary OPD and evaluated variants, improving final success by up to 15.2 percentage points and surpassing the teacher by up to 20.0 points. Moreover, SPOD's final checkpoints outperform even ordinary OPD's best intermediate checkpoints in all six settings, demonstrating that beyond-teacher gains can be not only preserved but further extended. Code: https://github.com/Jike338/SPOD

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a novel fine-tuning method (Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation) to improve instruction-following in agentic on-policy distillation, addressing preservation of gains beyond teacher performance.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 96. [CALM: Consistency-Aware Logit Masking in Decoding Space for Mitigating Overthinking of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25908) <a id="link96"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25908
**Authors:** Shengjing Liu, Feiyang Li, Yuxuan Yang, Yile Wang, Hui Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25908v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) have achieved strong performance on challenging mathematical and scientific tasks through long chain-of-thought reasoning, but they often suffer from overthinking with unnecessary reasoning steps. Existing truncation-based methods typically detect consecutive trial-answer agreement and force early exit, yet such local stopping signals do not necessarily indicate final-answer stability and can disrupt useful subsequent reflection or verification, potentially leading to performance degradation. We propose consistency-aware logit masking (CALM), a training-free decoding intervention for mitigating overthinking in LRMs. CALM first monitors trial answers at step boundaries and then applies logit masking to action transition point (ATP) tokens such as "Wait", "Alternatively", and "Hmm", suppressing redundant transitional reasoning while allowing the model to continue its thought process more naturally. Experiments across four advanced LRMs and five benchmarks show that CALM reduces token consumption by 18.6%-25.0% compared with full CoT reasoning while maintaining performance, and can even improve accuracy by 0.07%-1.48% by reducing redundant reasoning. LLM-as-a-Judge results also demonstrate that CALM provides more coherent and concise results.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: The paper proposes CALM, a decoding intervention to mitigate overthinking in large reasoning models, improving chain-of-thought reasoning efficiency and accuracy. This relates to advances in reasoning capabilities and controlling reasoning steps in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 97. [MaD-RSI: Model-as-Data for Recursive Self-Improvement of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28201) <a id="link97"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28201
**Authors:** Yu Cui, Hong He, Ruiqing Yue, Sicheng Pan, Zhuoyu Sun, Yifei Liu, Ting Li, Wenzhuo Zhu, Baohan Huang, Haibin Zhang, Cong Zuo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28201v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) of agent harnesses has emerged as an effective approach to enhancing the capabilities of large language model (LLM) agents. By searching the harness space for a specific LLM on a given dataset, RSI can discover a harness tailored to the capabilities and behavioral characteristics of that model. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper proposes a new method (MaD-RSI) for recursive self-improvement of agent harnesses across multiple LLMs, which relates to instruction-following improvements and optimization of harnesses for better instruction adherence/generalization. This is a novel fine-tuning or optimization step to improve instruction-following across models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 98. [The World as Viewed by 136 Countries: An Open, Curated, Annotated, and Multilingual Dataset of 118 Million State Media Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24990) <a id="link98"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24990
**Authors:** Bryan Li, Samar Haider, Grace Kim, Fiona Luo, Jinqi Luo, Yuan Yuan, Duncan Watts, Ren\'e Vidal, Chris Callison-Burch

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24990v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: State media outlets often project their governments' narratives to target both domestic and foreign audiences. Because this content is plentiful and free to access, it appears widely in the web corpora that large language models (LLMs) are trained on and that retrieval systems draw from. However, the prevalence of state media in these corpora and its potential influence on LLM outputs remain largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce WorldStateMedia, an open dataset of 118 million state media articles from 136 countries in 114 languages. We extract these articles from existing pretraining corpora and annotate each outlet with level of state control from State Media Monitor. Using WorldStateMedia to audit these corpora, we find that, in English web crawls, the share of state media pages from editorially controlled (vs. independent) outlets rose from 39% in 2013 to 93% in 2025. In several low-resource languages, state media make up a substantial share of all web data, such as 17.8% for Uyghur and 7.8% for Belarusian. To characterize the differing narratives of news outlets, we compare Russian and Ukrainian state media in English and in their native languages, revealing asymmetric topical focus and opposing framings of contested issues such as Crimea. Furthermore, in a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) experiment on territorial disputes, retrieving a country's state media shifts model answers toward that country's claims, most strongly for English-language (foreign-targeted) media. We release WorldStateMedia to support multilingual, cross-national insights on state media at https://huggingface.co/collections/manestay/worldstatemedia

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a large multilingual dataset of state media articles to study real-world usage and influence of language model pretraining data.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 99. [Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree for Inference-time Search Under Sparse Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24185) <a id="link99"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24185
**Authors:** Binglin Ji, Anindya Sarkar, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24185v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tree-based samplers offer a compelling alternative for scalable inference-time search, bypassing the per-step reward and gradient dependencies that introduce systematic bias into SMC-style frameworks. Despite this advantage, contemporary tree samplers fail to achieve broad exploration. Their reliance on learned, DDPM-like transitions inherently couples neighbor states in a trajectory, thereby restricting child-parent feature divergence in the tree sampler. Consequently, these samplers suffer from restricted coverage of the target marginal distribution, severely hindering the exploration critical for efficient search under sparse reward. To overcome these limitations, we introduce a simple yet effective Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree (BSIT) sampler that decouples child node generation from explicit parent dependencies. By expanding the degrees of freedom during child node sampling, BSIT encourages diverse trajectory rollouts and unlocks broader path-space diversity-the exact catalyst needed for efficient exploration. Crucially, we show that BSIT's improved exploration accelerates convergence to the target distribution while achieving broader distributional coverage than samplers that rely on DDPM-like transitions for child-node generation, all without incurring additional function evaluations or reward feedback, thereby providing a highly efficient basis for rare target search. Extensive experiments across diverse search settings, including rare-species discovery and target-aligned molecule generation tasks, demonstrate BSIT's superior search efficiency over the baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Introduces a new tree-based sampler (BSIT) that improves exploration and convergence in inference-time search under sparse reward, relevant to generative modeling with diffusion-like transitions in language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 100. [Encoder-Free Multimodal LLMs Learn Implicit Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24239) <a id="link100"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24239
**Authors:** David Harwath, Desh Raj, Xavier Velez, Sandra Arcos-Holzinger, Willie Jeng, Karen Livescu, Leibny Paola Garcia-Perera, Sanjeev Khudanpur

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24239v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We empirically analyze two recently proposed encoder-free multimodal LLMs, Gemma-4 12B and Inkling-Small. Unlike previous multimodal LLMs, these models do not use modality-specific encoders to extract tokenized embeddings representing audio and image input; instead, they directly feed windows of samples, pixels, or spectral features to the Transformer language model. We analyze whether the early layers of these models learn to function similarly to dedicated audio and visual encoders. We find that these early layers do in fact act as ``implicit encoders,'' encoding audio and visual inputs in a way that is largely invariant to their cross-modal context. Furthermore, when performing multimodal reasoning tasks, we show that independently encoding the modalities throughout these early model layers achieves the same accuracy as jointly encoding them from the first layer onwards. Finally, we use layer-wise probing to analyze the salient information captured by the implicit encoders and find their behavior to be similar to several popular encoder models.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 7: The paper analyzes implicit encoders learned by encoder-free multimodal LLMs, which relates to sparse feature extraction and understanding internal representations in multimodal LLMs. While not explicitly about sparse autoencoders, it touches on implicit encoding mechanisms relevant to interpretability.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 101. [Deep Fusion Memory: A Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25100) <a id="link101"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25100
**Authors:** Rubin Wei, Siyuan Sun, Hao Sun, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Shi, Jiarui Wang, Junming Zhang, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25100v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. A causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires $32\times$ fewer retrieval queries and $2048\times$ fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a new parametric memory architecture (Deep Fusion Memory) for large language models, which can be viewed as a novel generative modeling improvement related to diffusion-like memory fusion in LLMs. Although not explicitly a diffusion LM, the method involves continuous fusion of memory into hidden states, which is relevant to generative modeling advances in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 102. [UpLoop: Upcycling Large Language Models with Gated Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25004) <a id="link102"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25004
**Authors:** Tieyuan Chen, Huabin Liu, Yingtao Zhang, Shijie Li, Tengyu Pan, Weiyao Lin, Jianguo Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25004v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped large language models trade parameter storage for recurrent computation, but most are trained from scratch. In practice, pretraining with looped depth is expensive, making post-training upcycling of a pretrained non-looped LLM an attractive alternative. We introduce \textbf{UpLoop}, a post-training recipe based on \emph{layer-wise} recurrence. Instead of looping a whole model or a middle block, UpLoop loops each selected layer locally, preserving the pretrained depth hierarchy and avoiding feature-space mismatch. To make loop execution useful, we add hidden-wise gates on the attention and MLP residual branches, so later loops act as progressive refinements rather than redundant rewrites. Empirically, UpLoop breaks the commonly observed saturation at two loops ($R=2$) in prior looped models. Starting from Qwen3-4B Base and applying SFT, UpLoop improves the average accuracy across 20 benchmarks by 6.17\% at $R=3$. We use $R=3$ as the main setting for the best accuracy--cost trade-off, while both native and extrapolated $R=4$ yield further gains.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1 as it proposes a novel post-training method (UpLoop) to improve large language models by looping layers with gated refinements, which can be seen as a fine-tuning or architectural improvement to enhance instruction-following or general performance.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 103. [Probabilistic Limit Order Book Forecasting with Conditional Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25495) <a id="link103"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25495
**Authors:** Junyu Chen, Franklin Liu, Moshu Xu, Lijian Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25495v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modeling limit order book (LOB) dynamics is central to market forecasting and execution-strategy evaluation. While diffusion models achieve strong empirical performance, existing LOB snapshot models typically lack a learned recurrent encoder and formal statistical guarantees. We introduce LOBGrad, a conditional DDPM framework for probabilistic LOB forecasting that combines an autoregressive RNN encoder with a diffusion-based generator. In particular, we establish an excess-risk oracle inequality for the one-step DDPM noise-prediction objective under exponential mixing and regularity assumptions. We further show that, under contractive dynamics, the dependence on the conditioning-window length can be reduced to a dependence on the latent-state dimension. Experiments on BTC/USDT, GOOGL, and META show that LOBGrad improves on its encoder-free diffusion ablation across all reported queue metrics and obtains competitive results against other baselines. It also reproduces the key stylized facts of LOB microstructure, and yields reasonably calibrated queue-based predictive intervals. A stylized downstream task illustrates the potential value of the generated forecasts.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. Introduces a conditional DDPM framework for limit order book forecasting with theoretical guarantees and an RNN encoder, applied to financial time series but involving diffusion language modeling techniques.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 104. [Constructive Universal Approximation by Infinite-Dimensional Neural Networks via Gaussian-Weighted Ridgelet Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24293) <a id="link104"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24293
**Authors:** Sho Sonoda, Luca Galimberti, Yuanchao Xu, Isao Ishikawa, Masahiro Ikeda

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24293v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Neural networks whose inputs are functions, as in operator learning, are usually shown to be universal by existence arguments, either by reduction to finite-dimensional networks or by Stone--Weierstrass-type density; these prove that approximants exist but say little about how the parameters depend on the target. We take a constructive route. We extend the ridgelet transform, an integral transform related to the Fourier, Radon, and wavelet transforms, to continuous maps on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, replacing the Lebesgue measure that is unavailable in infinite dimensions by Gaussian weights with a homogeneous mixture on the direction space. The resulting transform has a Plancherel identity and reconstruction formulas incorporating the frame operator, the correction induced by the weights, and it assigns to each target an explicit coefficient measure, one for every admissible analysis filter. From these representations we obtain uniform approximation on compact sets for all continuous non-polynomial activations of polynomial growth, including ReLU, and, by Monte Carlo sampling of the coefficient, finite-width networks with a dimension-free $O(N^{-1/2})$ error in the width $N$ for globally Lipschitz activations and coefficients with finite variation and second moments. Explicit examples include Gaussian targets and neural-operator layers. A companion Lean~4 formalization provides machine-checked proofs, with its correspondence to the manuscript documented at https://shosonoda.github.io/lean-operator-ridgelet/ .

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6 (Studies scaling laws) partially in spirit, as it provides a constructive universal approximation theory for infinite-dimensional neural networks with explicit error rates and a novel Gaussian-weighted ridgelet transform. While not explicitly about scaling laws in language models, it advances theoretical understanding of neural network approximation in infinite dimensions, which is relevant to statistical machine learning theory.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 105. [When PTQ Calibration Helps or Hurts: Dose-Driven Collapse and Validation-Gated Abstention in Quantized Text Embedders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27397) <a id="link105"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27397
**Authors:** Hyojung Han, Ryangkyung Kang, Youngeun Kim, Seung-Hun Jeon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27397v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Learned post-training quantization (PTQ) calibration fits quantizer parameters on a small calibration set, and for a text embedder the natural supervision is retrieval relevance. We ask two questions that are usually conflated: how far to calibrate, and whether to calibrate at all. Across three embedders (e5-base-v2, BGE-M3, EmbeddingGemma-300m) at 4-bit weight quantization, 161 calibration runs, the outcome of a fixed procedure ranges from a gain to catastrophic degradation, and the driver is the dose of the update, measured as the functional displacement D_func of the quantized model from its uncalibrated start. On EmbeddingGemma the same learning-rate grid gives +1.47 to -71.53 points of in-domain NDCG@10 change; a label-free cosine objective collapses at the same dose (-69.52 vs -71.63), so the collapse is a property of learnable calibration rather than of retrieval supervision. Objective sensitivity is heterogeneous: on e5, ranking calibration is substantially more robust than cosine at matched displacement (+14.16 +/- 4.34 pp, 95% t-CI [+3.37, +24.96], three paired slates), whereas on BGE-M3 the ordering is directionally opposite but unresolved ([-5.18, +2.38]). Dose is the main axis but not a sufficient statistic: equal D_func reached along different paths differs by up to 1.61 pp. We therefore treat calibration as an optional intervention. A pre-registered rule that accepts a checkpoint only when a per-query paired confidence interval on a 25% calibration-validation split clears the uncalibrated baseline was run on 6 independent slates and five arms. It never selected a checkpoint below its slate's baseline (0 harmful selections in 30 decisions), retained 8 of 11 beneficial updates, and paid a mean oracle regret of 1.52 pp with one abstention that forfeited 11.13 pp, a safety-opportunity trade-off, not a free improvement. We report what remains open, including label efficiency.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a validation-gated abstention method for post-training quantization calibration of text embedders, which is a new paradigm for evaluating model calibration interventions and safety trade-offs in open-ended settings.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 106. [Runtime Control for AI Agents: Closing the Evaluation--Execution Gap in Operational Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27480) <a id="link106"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27480
**Authors:** Jayesh Bhadane, Ramachandra Yerramsetti, Rajesh Gupta, Sumit Kumar

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27480v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents increasingly participate in operational workflows where approvals, binds, and payments create commitments. An approval valid when issued may no longer authorize execution after relevant facts, evidence, policy, or authority change. We call this the evaluation--execution gap. We specify a state-bound runtime assurance layer that separates probabilistic measurement from deterministic adjudication, binds an execution allowance to the decision-relevant basis of an approval, invalidates that allowance after relevant logged changes, and rechecks admissibility at dispatch. This distinguishes the continued validity of a prior approval from a current-state policy check. We describe eight failure modes, an assurance model, a reference architecture, and prospective interventions with retrospective remediation. In a scripted 42-case replay, the state-bound configuration identifies 15 later-invalidated approvals that a deliberately non-re-evaluating point-in-time comparator does not revisit; it holds 14 affected actions before dispatch and compensates one landed effect. These results illustrate behavior under the stipulated policies and event orders, not superiority over dispatch-time enforcement designs or production effectiveness. Under explicit interception, ingestion, dependency, and serialization assumptions, the model establishes dispatch-time validity within the registered execution surface.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Proposes a runtime control framework to close the evaluation-execution gap in AI agents, specifying a state-bound runtime assurance layer for deployment-time oversight and control of approvals and policy adherence.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 107. [Write Once, Check Many: Reusable Ledgers for Post-Hoc Policy Compliance Auditing of Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24483) <a id="link107"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24483
**Authors:** Ashwin Nagarajan, Gursimran Panesar, Alicia Yang, Mona T. Diab, Virginia Smith, Aashiq Muhamed

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24483v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Auditing LLM agents against policies is critical to ensure safety but requires rereading long trajectories once per policy. Can we instead record each trajectory once and reuse that record for policies not known in advance? We propose write once, check many, a framework in which a writer model converts each agent trajectory into a policy-independent ledger of the agent's actions and outcomes. A reader then checks each new policy against the ledger alone, without access to the original trajectory. On 900 trajectories from SHADE-Arena, $\tau$-bench, and $\tau^2$-bench, we show that a 12B reader of narrative ledgers can either match or in fact achieve higher AUC than the same 12B model reading full trajectories. On SHADE-Arena policies that combine several facts, our approach outperforms a 31B model reading the trajectory (.777 vs. .713 AUC). When checking eight SHADE-Arena policies separately, ledgers use about $5\times$ fewer tokens, including the cost of writing them. A deeper analysis shows that rule-based extraction performs better on some single-action policies, and ledgers can miss evidence in long tool outputs and message contents. Our results suggest that reusable, policy-independent records can make retrospective agent auditing more efficient and effective, and highlight important considerations in ledger design.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents new methods for AI control, oversight, or monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment via reusable ledgers for post-hoc policy compliance auditing of agent trajectories, improving efficiency and effectiveness of retrospective auditing.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 108. [Recoverability and Verifier Strength: Two Measured Preconditions for Unattended Agent Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27465) <a id="link108"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27465
**Authors:** De Wang, Peng Qi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27465v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agent platforms are starting to improve their own agents: they read a deployed agent's execution traces, derive a change, have an automated verifier approve it, and release it with no human author. How much of this loop can safely run without a human? We propose two quantities a platform can measure. Verifier strength $\sigma$ is how well the available verifier -- often an LLM judge -- agrees with a trusted reference verifier; it limits how much a step can improve the agent. Recoverability is how cheaply a bad change can be undone: whether undoing it needs a new verifier approval, and how much of the old behavior a rollback restores. A step should run unattended only if both are adequate. On our platform this rule allows prompt, tool and skill edits to run unattended and keeps weight updates under human sign-off.   Our main contribution is these two definitions and how to measure them. We built a platform that applies the rule and tested it in two ways: on the platform's own approval gate, and in controlled experiments on the $\tau^2$-bench agent benchmark. On verifier strength, the platform's LLM judge sometimes gave the same score no matter what it was shown (9 of 37 settings) and reported no error, so a verifier can look healthy while approving anything; its strength must be measured, and two simple tests catch the failure. On recoverability, restoring a saved copy brought the agent back to its original behavior, but a regenerated undo did not, so only changes whose old version is kept can be reversed without a human; and while a change was deployed, it altered the outcome of 17 percent of tasks beyond normal run-to-run variation, which no undo repairs. Together these support the rule: a change can run unattended only when its old version is kept and its verifier's strength has been measured.

**Comment:** This paper proposes measurable quantities (verifier strength and recoverability) for unattended agent self-improvement, focusing on automated verification and rollback in agent platforms. It relates to AI control and oversight (criterion 13) but does not directly address RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 109. [miniTE: Diagnosing and Bridging the Execution Gap in Native NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23928) <a id="link109"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23928
**Authors:** Zhen Li, Mingfa Feng, Yiming Zhang, Yang Yu, Changhai Zhou, Pengxiang Li, Dongkuan Wu, Congkai Xie, Hongxia Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23928v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Native NVFP4 promises to accelerate reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models (LLMs), but achieving stable learning across separate training and sampling engines remains challenging. We show that matching model weights and numerical formats is insufficient to ensure consistent policy evaluation, highlighting the importance of cross-engine numerical execution. We introduce miniTE, a lightweight framework that combines execution diagnostics, cross-engine numerical alignment, and efficient native W4A4 (4-bit weights and activations) GEMM computation for LLM RL. It improves numerical execution consistency without modifying the RL objective, while reducing implementation overhead to realize the benefits of native low-bit computation. Long-horizon RL experiments on Qwen3-30B-A3B demonstrate stable training and benchmark performance close to BF16, outperforming naive Transformer Engine (TE) NVFP4 in evaluation quality. On single NVIDIA B300 GPU node, the optimized learner reaches 33,540 tokens/s, yielding 1.39x and 1.47x the throughput of BF16 and TE NVFP4, respectively, for complete learner updates. We released the framework, kernels, diagnostics, and training configurations to make native low-bit RL easier.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses reinforcement learning improvements for LLMs with native low-bit computation and stable training, relevant to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps for language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 110. [Where Are Language-Specific Features Represented in Multilingual LLMs? Everywhere](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25505) <a id="link110"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25505
**Authors:** Mikaela Martins, Jonathan Dunn

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25505v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Previous work has argued that multilingual LLMs encode language-specific features most strongly in their initial and final layers, implying that intermediate layers support language-agnostic representations. This paper reconsiders this finding by performing four different experiments: three that undertake a fine-grained analysis of representations of Portuguese and Spanish, two closely related languages, and one large-scale language identification experiment that covers over 200 languages. The results of these experiments challenge the strict separation between language-specific and language-agnostic layers in multilingual LLMs, using Llama 3.2 1B as a test case. By analyzing and manipulating contextual sentence embeddings, we demonstrate that language identity remains strongly recoverable throughout the model rather than being restricted to initial and final layers. Moreover, we find that the model tends to classify ambiguous strings as closer to Spanish than to Portuguese, revealing a skew in model behavior that differs from human judgments.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 8 (mechanistic interpretability). This paper analyzes where language-specific features are represented in multilingual LLMs, challenging prior assumptions about layer specialization. It uses representation analysis and manipulation to reveal language identity is distributed throughout the model, which is a mechanistic interpretability insight.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

## 111. [Aligning Cultural Behaviors in Arabic Language Models via Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25362) <a id="link111"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25362
**Authors:** Junhong Liang, Daniil Orel, Saeed Almheiri, Bilal Elbouardi, Younes Samih, Abed Alhakim Freihat, Zhuohan Xie, Momina Ahsan, Muhammad Dehan Al Kautsar, Sarfraz Ahmad, Omar El Herraoui, Mohamed Anwar, Preslav Nakov, Fajri Koto

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25362v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Arabic LLMs have improved rapidly, but aligning them with country-specific cultural behavioral preferences remains underexplored. Previous work has focused primarily on instruction-tuning for factual knowledge, overlooking nuanced cultural behaviors. We propose ArabPref, which to our knowledge is the first large-scale pan-Arab preference data set for the alignment of cultural behavior conditioned on the country. Our contribution includes two resources in English and Modern Standard Arabic: a culturally grounded preference dataset for training and evaluation, and a multiple-choice benchmark designed to assess culturally aligned behavior across nations. All test data are validated by native speakers to ensure authenticity. To optimize cultural alignment, we experiment with post-training methods across multilingual and Arabic-centric language models, evaluating performance on both generation tasks and our cultural multiple-choice benchmark. By releasing an evaluation benchmark, we provide a foundation for systematically studying and improving culturally aligned behavior in language models.

**Comment:** This paper presents a new large-scale culturally grounded preference dataset and benchmark for instruction-following alignment in Arabic LLMs, focusing on cultural behavior alignment. It relates to criterion 1 (instruction-following improvements via preference data and alignment).
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

## 112. [DeGMatch: Target-Aware Reasoning Data Selection via Deconfounded Gradient Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24963) <a id="link112"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24963
**Authors:** Changho Lee, Janghoon Han, Rajdeep Mukherjee, Yeda Song, Lening Nick Cui, Xinya Du, Honglak Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24963v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning post-training on long trajectories has substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but scaling such training to large datasets is increasingly expensive. We study target-aware reasoning data selection, where a small reference set specifies a target capability and guides the selection of training trajectories for specialization. We find that existing target-aware methods can exhibit limited target discrimination on long reasoning trajectories, while dominant population-level structure in candidate gradients can obscure finer target-dependent variation during gradient matching. To address these limitations, we introduce Deconfounded Gradient Matching (DeGMatch), which first constructs a target-relevance gate and then reranks candidates using model-specific gradients. DeGMatch centers gradients using the candidate-population mean and removes leading principal directions before target matching, reducing the influence of dominant population-level structure and improving target discrimination. Across mathematics, science, and code, DeGMatch outperforms strong heuristic, target-aware, and reasoning-specific data selection baselines, while remaining effective across selection budgets, model families, and scales. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/CHLee0801/degmatch.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: Proposes a new data selection method (Deconfounded Gradient Matching) for reasoning data selection that improves target-aware reasoning data selection by removing confounding population-level gradient structure. This is a data-centric AI approach focusing on data selection for reasoning capabilities in LLMs, which is relevant to improving instruction-following or reasoning fine-tuning data curation.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 113. [What "Form'' of Uncertainty Does Conformal Prediction Quantify?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24510) <a id="link113"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24510
**Authors:** Soroush H. Zargarbashi, Mohammad Sadegh Akhondzadeh, Rebekka Burkholz, Aleksandar Bojchevski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24510v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) is a post-hoc calibration method that returns prediction sets with distribution-free coverage guarantees, and the size of the output set is often interpreted as a notion of uncertainty. We show that the difference in set size between full and split CP is directly connected to Bayesian posterior variance, which is the epistemic uncertainty of the model. Essentially, this difference in set size is an aggregation of two opposite effects: (1) better model accuracy and therefore smaller set size achieved by training over extra calibration points, and (2) the "formability" of the model at test point towards every possible label. The latter effect vanishes with stronger models or more calibration data (as epistemic uncertainty is known for that behavior). It turns out that the model's formability itself (without the quantization effect of prediction sets, and through estimating the posterior variance) is a useful tool to quantify the epistemic uncertainty. In our empirical evaluation it performs on par with the state of the art in many setups.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Presents a new statistical insight connecting conformal prediction set sizes to Bayesian posterior variance as a measure of epistemic uncertainty, which relates to uncertainty quantification and potentially test set contamination detection methods for language models.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 114. [PRECOCIAL: Pretraining with Code Curriculums via Iterative Search for Accelerated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24970) <a id="link114"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24970
**Authors:** Edward Gu, Ali Payani, Kevin Ellis

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24970v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fully synthetic pretraining data generated by symbolic programs can bootstrap downstream training on naturalistic tasks, such as language modeling, but the generating programs have so far been designed by hand. We introduce PRECOCIAL, a method for automatically optimizing programs that generate synthetic pretraining data. Starting from an initial generator program, an LLM iteratively rewrites the program, and each rewrite is scored by pretraining a randomly-initialized transformer on its data and fine-tuning it on the downstream task. Because every score requires training, PRECOCIAL keeps a single network for the entire search, continuing its pretraining for each accepted program instead of training a new network per candidate, making it significantly more efficient. On four language and code datasets, at a fixed fine-tuning budget, optimized programs reach lower perplexity than hand-designed curricula, published procedural generators, random program mutation and the program the LLM writes in the first iteration of the search, and reach the same perplexity as the hand-designed curricula with 9-33% fewer fine-tuning steps. PRECOCIAL also ends with lower perplexity than FunSearch and AlphaEvolve, which train a new network for every candidate, at the same number of evaluations and a lower cost. We also observe that an optimized program generator from one domain transfers to datasets in other domains. Additionally, optimized programs allow for better downstream performance on four different reasoning and problem-solving tasks.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 18: Proposes a data-centric method (PRECOCIAL) for optimizing synthetic pretraining data generation programs to improve downstream language model training efficiency and performance.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 115. [NADS: Navigator-Guided Data Selection for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25751) <a id="link115"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25751
**Authors:** Jianhao Zhang, Ou Wu, Yi Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25751v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle in single-stage fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) on a new task, especially when the original pretraining data are unavailable. Synthetic data offers a practical mitigation path, but candidate pools are often noisy and redundant, making effective selection essential. Existing methods often rely on indirect proxy signals whose alignment with actual training benefit can be limited, while utility--diversity selection over large candidate pools remains computationally demanding. To address these challenges, we propose NAvigator-guided Data Selection (NADS), a fine-tuning framework for LLMs. NADS first follows the target fine-tuning recipe without preservation regularization to obtain a navigator model, whose deviation from the pretrained model exposes recipe-induced capability drift. It then uses the predictive divergence between the navigator and the pretrained model to score forgetting-aware utility for each synthetic candidate, and constructs a constraint set through an efficient utility--diversity selection strategy. Finally, NADS fine-tunes on the new-task data while distilling pretrained behavior on the selected constraints to preserve general capabilities. Experiments show that NADS delivers a stronger balance between new-task performance and general capability preservation, while reducing utility--diversity selection overhead. Code at: https://github.com/zjh-vinky/NADS.

**Comment:** This paper proposes NADS, a fine-tuning framework that mitigates catastrophic forgetting by selecting synthetic data based on predictive divergence between a navigator and pretrained model. It is about fine-tuning methodology and preserving pretrained capabilities but does not explicitly mention RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning. It is somewhat related to fine-tuning improvements but not a direct match to criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 116. [Where and Why Search-Augmented LLMs Fail When Reasoning over Rapidly Evolving Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25323) <a id="link116"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25323
**Authors:** Tianyi Hu, Bhavyajeet Singh, Niket Tandon, Akhil Arora

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25323v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As the world changes, new information is published while older records remain online. This complicates reasoning for large language models (LLMs), which must retrieve evidence and resolve conflicts to determine which information remains valid. Existing evaluations treat information seeking as a black box, revealing little about where and why LLMs fail. To address this gap, we introduce TREK, a suite of three components: TREKTRACE is a plug-and-play framework that attributes failures to specific stages of retrieval and reasoning. We apply it to TREKBENCH-WILD, a meta-benchmark of real-world questions that require complex reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge. Surprisingly, the main bottleneck is post-retrieval reasoning rather than retrieval: frontier models are often misled by conflicting evidence even after retrieving all the information needed to answer. To study this bottleneck, we build TREKBENCH-CTRL, a controlled benchmark with distractors spanning temporality, relevance, and trustworthiness, and show that benchmarks dominated by outdated distractors substantially underestimate task difficulty. Together, TREK provides a testbed for diagnosing and improving reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge.

**Comment:** This paper introduces TREK, a framework to diagnose failures in search-augmented LLM reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge. It focuses on evaluation and failure attribution in reasoning pipelines, which relates to new paradigms for evaluating open-ended text generation (criterion 4). However, it is more about reasoning failure analysis than fundamentally rethinking evaluation paradigms accounting for subjectivity or adversaries.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 117. [Safety Offset Learning: When Benign Fine-Tuning Misaligns LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25611) <a id="link117"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25611
**Authors:** Jesson Wang, Zhanhao Hu, Minghao Lin, Yuchen Wu, Aylin Caliskan, Mengyuan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25611v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Safety risks from fine-tuning are commonly characterized by the harmfulness of the training data:harmful fine-tuning is considered dangerous, while benign fine-tuning is often assumed to preservealignment. We argue that this view misses a more fundamental factor-the behavioral transition inducedby supervision. Harmful fine-tuning and safety-degrading benign fine-tuning can both be understoodas learning the same refusal-to-compliance transition, but on different data distributions. We studywhether such a transition learned on benign inputs can generalize to harmful requests. We constructbenign examples that an aligned model initially tends to refuse and pair them with their originalharmless responses, creating a discrepancy between the model's initial refusal and the supervisedanswer. Fine-tuning on these examples increases harmful compliance, but only when two conditionshold together: the discrepancy is large enough, and it cannot be resolved through a narrow surface-levelshortcut. With the induced discrepancy matched, varying only whether it is localized changes harmfulcompliance by an order of magnitude; raising the discrepancy does not help while a shortcut remainsavailable, and removing the shortcut does not help while the discrepancy is small. Building on thisobservation, we introduce SAFEOFF, a dataset of benign instructions rewritten to raise the alignedmodel's refusal likelihood and paired with their original answers. Across three aligned LLMs and threesafety benchmarks, SAFEOFF substantially weakens safety alignment despite containing no explicitharmful supervision, suggesting that fine-tuning safety depends not only on what data a model sees, butalso on which behavioral boundary the supervision moves.

**Comment:** Related to safety and alignment in fine-tuning LLMs, studying behavioral transitions during fine-tuning that affect refusal compliance. Relevant to alignment (criterion 11) but not directly about RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 118. [Shaping the Document, Not the Model: Optimized Suffixes for Context Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26236) <a id="link118"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26236
**Authors:** Gia-Bao Dinh Ho, Sekh Mainul Islam, Masahiro Kaneko, Timothy Baldwin, Isabelle Augenstein

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26236v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When a user supplies a document and wants the answer drawn from it, a language model is expected to follow it even when it contradicts its parametric knowledge. Prior work has focused on making the model rely on context by steering activations, contrasting logits during decoding, or instructing it in plain language. We ask whether the document alone, with no instruction written into it and no access to the model at inference time, is enough. We optimize a document suffix by discrete token search, starting from the answer to a single question and extending the objective to a set of passages, to multi-hop questions that follow from the planted fact, and to later turns of a conversation. On counterfactual passages that contradict parametric knowledge of the model, the suffix flips nearly all items the model resists across three instruction-tuned model families, in most settings outperforms the decoding baseline and the strongest instruction we could write, and matches activation steering. Controls show that the suffix largely acts through the passage rather than injecting the answer, and a single suffix per model transfers to unseen passages. Each extended objective carries the effect to its target, so the objective, not only the document, sets how far the effect reaches.

**Comment:** This paper proposes optimizing document suffixes to steer language model outputs without modifying the model or instructions, effectively controlling context following. While it relates to instruction-following behavior, it does not propose new fine-tuning methods like RLHF or instruction tuning datasets, nor does it address test contamination or evaluation paradigms fundamentally.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 119. [DeltaTrace: Efficient Signed Attribution for Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25877) <a id="link119"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25877
**Authors:** Chen Liang, Daoping Wu, Donghua Yang, Zhiyu Liang, Ying Zhang, Shuguang Xiong, Hongzhi Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25877v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study how words in the context support or oppose a language model's response and present DeltaTrace, a novel attribution framework that explains the effect of input tokens on a fixed model response. DeltaTrace measures how the response's log-probability changes when the context tokens being explained are masked. To distribute this difference among source tokens, it constructs operator-specific local rules for finite changes and composes them backward through the computational graph using effective secant slopes. Each token is assigned a signed attribution as the inner product of its embedding displacement and the corresponding backward-propagated slope. Local conservation makes these contributions sum to the measured score difference and preserves their meaning when aggregated over sentences or passages. DeltaTrace supports both attention and linear-attention models, with auxiliary memory growing linearly with sequence length at fixed model dimensions and chunk size. On the evaluated attention model, DeltaTrace achieves the lowest deletion-faithfulness scores (RISE and MAS) on most retrieval and reasoning tasks and the highest needle-in-a-haystack Recall, exceeding FlashTrace by 5.67-24.33 percentage points. On the evaluated hybrid attention and linear-attention model, DeltaTrace lowers RISE on 9 tasks and MAS on 12 relative to FlashTrace.

**Comment:** This paper proposes DeltaTrace, a novel signed attribution method for reasoning language models that explains token contributions to model responses. While it relates to interpretability, it does not directly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is relevant to mechanistic interpretability (criterion 8) but not explicitly stated as such.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 120. [Atoms, Not Knowledge: What Production-Scale Addressed-Memory Writes Deposit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25317) <a id="link120"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25317
**Authors:** Wei Gao, Cheng Tian, Duan Yang, Jianqing Yin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25317v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deterministic hash-addressed conditional memory permits local post-hoc knowledge injection. Extending arXiv:2610.21553's bounded-feasibility audit rejecting under-specified generality claims with repaired probes and training-dose/capacity axes, we give, to our knowledge, the first production-scale write-content verdict. We test 124M-7B self-trained/publicly pretrained beds, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next-FP8 (176.95B; 320,001,536 FP8 rows), and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. Write coverage requires a sufficiently trained backbone prior, full-width readout, trainable gates; undertrained-bed intervention confirms a threshold, not a slope. At fixed budget, coverage falls from 98% (50 facts) to 12.7% (1,000); table size is not binding. Two-hop composition does not exceed the independent single-hop baseline (26% observed vs 25% expected; 15% lexical-distortion floor). Synergy remains absent across dose/load in two independent bed families. A 28-percentage-point in-context gap (given vs recalled intermediates) localizes failure to retrieval-for-reasoning, not gate access. Writes are independently retrievable, precisely addressable, reversible atoms, not composable into reasoning. A layered protocol, the first, to our knowledge, to cover compositionality in behavioral memory evaluation, separates baseline, synergy and distortion floor. Any addressed/key-value memory must report coverage, synergy ratio and distortion rate before deployment claims. Composition must be system-orchestrated; multi-constraint queries should explicitly chain written atoms. In-context controls show given intermediate entities are exploited at twice the implicit rate. Production load fits exponential interference ($R^2=0.98$); its mechanism, between budget dilution and write interference, remains open. All numbers are recomputed from archived raw logs/JSON; a fresh 52-target production rerun gives 52/52, exactly matching archived baselines.

**Comment:** This paper studies production-scale addressed-memory writes in large models, analyzing coverage, synergy, and compositionality in memory retrieval. It provides a novel behavioral memory evaluation protocol. While it relates to language model internals and memory, it does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability mechanistic techniques explicitly.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 121. [Alliance of Stochastic Routing and Reinforcement Fusion for Multi-Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24054) <a id="link121"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24054
**Authors:** Selim Furkan Tekin, Ling Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24054v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This paper studies dynamic model routing under uncertainty in multimodal settings, where a system must select and potentially compose multiple models to answer a query. Standard reinforcement learning approaches for routing often suffer from premature convergence to suboptimal models due to noisy rewards and insufficient exploration. We propose a unified vision-language framework (VL-Scout) for efficient multimodal and multi-step reasoning with turn-based reinforcement fusion. First, VL-Scout combines Bayesian priors, policy gradient optimization, and sequential decision-making to enable structured exploration and adaptive inference. A context-dependent Dirichlet belief model allows for epistemic uncertainty over model quality and is updated online based on observed rewards and context-specific evidence, enabling Thompson-style exploration while serving as a prior for regularization. Second, a routing policy is trained via a KL-regularized REINFORCE objective by maximizing a reward-weighted evidence lower bound, where the policy acts as an amortized posterior, and the Dirichlet belief serves as a prior. Third, we introduce a fusion policy trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) or Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which operates over intermediate model rationales and selects between two actions: CONTINUE or ANSWER, terminating with a generated output. We evaluate VL-Scout on four representative multi-modal benchmarks (MMMU, MMMU-Pro, OKVQA, and RealWorldQA), compared against 11 representative routing and policy optimization baselines. Our method provides consistent performance improvements while reducing premature policy collapse. These results highlight the importance of integrating uncertainty-aware priors with both amortized policy learning and sequential fusion for robust and efficient model selection in discrete action spaces

**Comment:** Partially matches criterion 1: proposes a reinforcement learning framework (VL-Scout) with Bayesian priors and policy gradient optimization for multi-model routing and reasoning. It involves reinforcement fusion and uncertainty-aware priors, which relate to methodological improvements in RL-based model selection and reasoning. However, it is focused on multimodal routing rather than instruction-following or RLHF for language models specifically.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 122. [A "feature ODE" describing the learning behavior of shallow MLPs on simple functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25652) <a id="link122"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25652
**Authors:** Joseph Turnbull, Berkan Ottlik, Jamie Simon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25652v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study the gradient flow training dynamics of two-layer multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) on anisotropic Gaussian data. Even in this simple setting, solving the dynamics in full is notoriously difficult, so we try something different. We propose a "feature ODE" that might be intuitively expected to describe the learning dynamics of a shallow MLP. We find that MLPs trained to learn simple target functions (low-order Hermite polynomials and staircase functions) actually roughly follow the loss trajectories from this feature ODE. This agreement is a surprise because of the size of the simplification and the fact that it does not come from a rigorous chain of approximations. We discuss this surprise and speculate on its reasons and uses.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws or theoretical explanations of learning dynamics in neural networks, here via a 'feature ODE' describing shallow MLP training dynamics on simple functions.

**Novelty:** 6

## 123. [Accelerated Drift of Scientific Prose in the LLM Era: Evidence from an Interrupted Time-Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26008) <a id="link123"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26008
**Authors:** Hanna Hubarava, Benjamin V. Ineichen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26008v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Every scientific text is embedded in the writing norms of its time: it reflects earlier publications while also shaping the texts that follow. The growing use of LLM-based writing tools introduces a new factor in this evolving system, but the linguistic changes coinciding with their emergence remain insufficiently understood. We investigate whether the trajectories of scientific-preprint writing changed in rate or direction after conversational LLMs became publicly accessible, using the release of ChatGPT in November 2022 as the observational breakpoint. We compare pre- and post-2022 trajectories across vocabulary, punctuation, readability, and syntax in abstracts from arXiv's artificial intelligence, statistics, and quantitative biology categories and from medRxiv. Using interrupted time-series regression, we find substantial post-2022 changes across multiple feature families and research domains, including increases in average word length, reduced formula-based readability, and declines in maximum dependency-tree depth and sentence-level depth variation. Individual features follow different dynamics: some lexical markers accelerate persistently, while others rise and then recede. Our findings show that isolated marker frequencies capture only part of the shift: changes in the rate, direction, and structure of linguistic trajectories provide complementary evidence of broader post-2022 change in scientific-preprint writing. More broadly, these findings motivate monitoring scientific writing as a multidimensional, evolving system rather than through a fixed set of LLM-associated markers.

**Comment:** Analyzes linguistic changes in scientific prose post-ChatGPT release using interrupted time-series analysis. Related to real-world usage and impact of LLMs (criterion 5) but not about instruction-following improvements, contamination, or generative modeling advances.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 124. [Evolving Self-tuning Benchmarks for Long-Horizon Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27307) <a id="link124"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27307
**Authors:** Hyunji Alex Nam, Michael Dennis, Natasha Jaques, Minsuk Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27307v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: For AI assistants to move beyond simple chat interfaces and reliably assist users with applications like Gmail, Venmo, and Slack, we need to improve their tool-use capabilities. However, existing tool use benchmarks are still limited and difficult to scale because they rely on human supervision or are too domain-specific. To make data generation scalable, we propose, TURTLE (**T**ool **U**se **R**elated **T**asks with **L**ong-Horizon **E**volution), an Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approach to evolve existing tasks using a proposer, a solver, and a feedback generator large language model agents. The proposer is rewarded for difficult yet solvable tasks based on the solver's empirical performance, and the feedback generator summarizes the solver's execution traces for the proposer to exploit. TURTLE produces evolving, self-tuning test suites adaptable to different environment requirements, from supporting user simulators and dense document retrieval to interacting across multiple apps. Empirical results on AppWorld, $\tau$-Bench, and AutomationBench using Gemma 4 31B-it show that the evolved tasks target the desired difficulty range (e.g., a 165\% increase in the percentage of hard tasks on AppWorld), increase task diversity (in terms of reasoning and simultaneous tool interactions), and elicit a broader range of failure modes than the original tasks.

**Comment:** This paper proposes TURTLE, an unsupervised environment design approach to evolve self-tuning benchmarks for long-horizon tool use by LLM agents. It focuses on evolving test suites and task difficulty for tool use capabilities. While it involves benchmark evolution and evaluation, it does not fundamentally rethink open-ended text generation evaluation (criterion 4), nor does it address RLHF or instruction-following improvements (1), test contamination (2), or diffusion LMs (3). It is somewhat relevant to evaluation and generative modeling but not a direct match.

**Novelty:** 6

## 125. [Humans Correct Their Judgments Through Debate, but Weaker AI Models Do Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28539) <a id="link125"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28539
**Authors:** Maria Victoria Carro, Denise Alejandra Mester, Facundo Nieto, Oscar Agustin Stanchi, Guido Ernesto Bergman, Giovanni Franco Gabriel Marrafini, Trinidad Borrell, Guido Freire, Joaquin Salvador Machulsky, Mario Alejandro Leiva, Ana Lopez, Nicolas Spinelli, Luca Nicolas Forziati Gangi, Eitan Sprejer, Federico Barrera Lemarchand, Gerardo Simari, Maria Vanina Martinez

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28539v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: How can we effectively oversee AI systems that surpass human intelligence? AI Safety via Debate proposes that two AI systems argue opposing positions and a judge, either a human or a weaker model, decides which is correct. By facilitating supervisors in providing high-quality training signals, debate aims to encourage truthful and safe behavior from AI systems whose capabilities exceed our own. However, human judgment is not neutral: people often rely on biases which advanced AI systems may learn to exploit. Drawing on insights from collective intelligence and social cognition, this paper investigates whether debate can guide human and LLM supervisors toward the truth despite their prior beliefs. We evaluate several protocol configurations. We also explore setups inspired by the wisdom of the crowd such as the number and diversity of judges, as well as interaction mechanisms including deliberation and voting. Overall, we find that debate increases accuracy relative to prior beliefs for human judges, whereas the consultancy baseline, in which a single expert argues for one answer, does not. For weaker language models, however, the results are mixed: debate provides no comparable gains in most settings and can even reduce accuracy. Among multi-judge protocols, weighted voting produces the highest accuracy for human supervisors, whereas for LLMs none of them significantly improves group accuracy, even as the number of judges increases. Notably, the behavior of minority judges in non-diverse deliberation protocols suggests that, under symmetric settings, incorrect positions could be more persuasive than correct ones. These findings support debate as a viable path toward scalable human oversight, but cast doubt on its benefits for weaker AI supervisors, raising concerns about fully automated training and control pipelines in high-stakes settings.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 13: Studies AI oversight via debate protocols to improve human and AI supervisor accuracy in judging AI outputs. This is about AI control and oversight during deployment, focusing on debate as a scalable human oversight method. However, it does not propose new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following, nor new evaluation paradigms or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

## 126. [HCFM: Helmholtz Component Flow Matching for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25059) <a id="link126"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25059
**Authors:** Shyam Sundar Saravanan, Tie Luo, Sanjay Madria

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25059v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flow Matching (FM) is an efficient simulation-free approach for training flow-based generative models, making it a promising foundation for scalable time-series anomaly detection (TSAD). However, standard FM learns a monolithic vector field in which nominal transport and local density-changing behavior are entangled. This creates a central challenge for TSAD; local expansion and contraction carry crucial anomaly-sensitive information, yet isolating this signal requires expensive Jacobian-trace estimation over the entire field. We propose \emph{Helmholtz Component Flow Matching} (HCFM), an unsupervised TSAD framework that parameterizes the vector field prediction network with separate transport, compression, and residual heads. The transport head is constrained to be divergence-free, so divergence estimation is restricted to the compression head, while a lightweight residual head absorbs the remaining mismatch. This structured parameterization improves scalability and detection performance by separating volume-preserving nominal transport from density-changing compression, yielding cleaner anomaly signals from distinct deviations of nominal behavior. Experiments on large-scale univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks show that HCFM consistently improves detection accuracy while maintaining efficient inference compared with 16 baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/TrustAIoT/HCFM__time-series-anomaly/

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes Helmholtz Component Flow Matching (HCFM), a novel flow matching method for time series anomaly detection. Although not directly about language models, it advances flow matching generative modeling which is related to diffusion modeling techniques. However, no explicit mention of language models or diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 127. [Pair-Aware Optimization: Coordinating Exploration and Refinement for Jailbreaking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24823) <a id="link127"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24823
**Authors:** Juanyang Xu, Zheng Wang, Xingyu Zhao, Siddartha Khastgir, Andi Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24823v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 19: studies jailbreaking attacks on LLM agents with a novel hybrid optimization method, but focuses on attack methods rather than defenses or securing LLMs.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 128. [Can This LLM Annotate My Data? Estimating Zero-Shot LLM Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24250) <a id="link128"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24250
**Authors:** Tong Lin, Grant Schoenebeck, Sabina Tomkins

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24250v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in annotation tasks with varying success. While several studies have demonstrated the opportunities and shortcomings of LLMs for annotation, a critical question remains unanswered: is it possible to predict how well they will perform on a new dataset? To answer this and related questions, we construct two task families which induce 461 text classification datasets. We find that it is possible to predict LLM performance - to a certain degree - and that contrary to other work which highlights the usefulness of LLM verbalizations of self-confidence, internal log-probabilities are the most predictive signal.

## 129. [P2N: Reusing Deep Representations for Greater Effective Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25076) <a id="link129"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25076
**Authors:** Yiqin Hao, Boyi Zeng, Zitong Wang, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, Yifan Liu, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25076v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Mathematical problem solving and autonomous agents are increasingly important applications of language models, demanding stronger reasoning capabilities. Computational depth is a key resource for such reasoning, but adding model layers increases both training and inference costs, while test-time scaling incurs substantial additional inference computation. We introduce P2N (Previous to Next), which reuses the previous token's deep representations, already available during generation, to increase effective computational depth with negligible decoding overhead. Specifically, P2N adds the previous token's deep hidden state to the current token's shallow representation, allowing subsequent layers to build on previously completed deep computation. This recurrence extends effective computational depth across tokens, while introducing only a single vector addition per decoding step. We train the recurrence using parallel Jacobi updates restricted to a middle-layer core. Experiments with Qwen-style models show that, at 1.5B parameters, P2N improves GSM8K accuracy by over 10 percentage points under both zero-shot and few-shot prompting, with nearly unchanged decoding latency. The benefits also extend to language modeling and average performance on general downstream benchmarks. on general downstream benchmarks.

**Comment:** Related to improving reasoning capabilities of language models by reusing deep representations to increase effective computational depth, but does not explicitly match criteria on RLHF, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 130. [When Does Better Acceptance Pay? A Controlled Study of Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24993) <a id="link130"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24993
**Authors:** Yongchao Long, Chunrui Zheng, Yuxuan Zhou, Guangkun Nie, Tian-Chi Ma, Chao Pang, Shuohuan Wang, Yuchen Ding, Xianbin Wen, Dianhai Yu, Junyuan Shang, Yuxi Zhou, Shenda Hong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24993v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The throughput gains of speculative decoding depend on the trade-off between acceptance length and execution latency. We ask when additional acceptance is worth the cost of obtaining it. We establish a comparison protocol spanning training, inference, and evaluation, remeasuring released checkpoints and retraining six drafter recipes on shared target-generated data across ten benchmarks and two target models. Alignment changes the picture: gaps that appear large in published comparisons shrink substantially under shared training and inference conditions. This narrowing motivates two deeper questions: how is acceptance improved at different costs, and when do these gains improve throughput? We find that acceptance can be improved through nearly free objective and context changes or more expensive token-to-token conditioning. DCore illustrates the former route as a strong baseline with little additional measured drafting overhead. The payoff is conditional: task uncertainty and serving conditions affect acceptance returns, execution costs, and the preferred drafter and budget. These findings inform future drafter design and deployment choices.

**Comment:** This paper studies speculative decoding throughput gains and acceptance trade-offs, analyzing training, inference, and evaluation protocols. It focuses on decoding efficiency and drafter design rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or contamination detection. It does not match any specific criteria but is relevant to generative modeling and decoding strategies.

## 131. [Claims of emergence in AI should be grounded in information decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28479) <a id="link131"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28479
**Authors:** Christoph Riedl, Fernando E. Rosas

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28479v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When a language model develops capabilities not traceable to its individual components, when a multi-agent AI system outperforms each agent on its own, or when a human-AI pair succeeds where either fails in isolation, AI researchers describe it as a case of ``synergy'' or ``emergence''. However, these are usually informal assessments not supported by a theoretical framework, making these claims impossible to compare or even meaningfully dispute. Here we argue that \emph{information decomposition can serve as a unifying formal framework to operationalize assessments about synergy in AI systems}. Information decomposition provides a way to distinguish qualitatively different \emph{types} of information, including unique information available from individual components, redundant information shared across multiple components, and synergistic information accessible only from parts taken together. This gives claims of emergence a precise, falsifiable criterion: a system's behavior is emergent only if it carries synergistic information about task-relevant outcomes. We apply this lens to attention heads, autonomous agents, and human-AI teams, showing how it organizes existing knowledge across scales and substrates, while also generating cross-level conjectures that siloed fields would miss.

**Comment:** This paper proposes using information decomposition as a formal framework to operationalize and falsify claims of emergence and synergy in AI systems, including language models. While it is a conceptual/theoretical contribution, it does not directly address any of the listed criteria such as RLHF improvements, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is more foundational for understanding emergence rather than specific to the criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 7

## 132. [A Statistical Viewpoint on Adam, Shampoo and Muon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24839) <a id="link132"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24839
**Authors:** Tetiana Parshakova, Robert M. Gower, Paulo Orenstein

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24839v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We provide new statistical insights into designing stochastic optimization methods. By modeling the gradient via a normal distribution, we argue that the stochastic gradient should be whitened using its covariance. We give an online method for estimating the mean and variance of the gradients based on regularized likelihood or, equivalently, through a maximum a posteriori estimate. We then show how to use structured approximations to the covariance within this same framework, which includes diagonal and Kronecker-factored approximations. The diagonal approximation results in a method that is very closely related to Adam and performs as well in practice. The Kronecker approximation results in the new MN method, which is a variant of Shampoo and KL-Shampoo. We also recover Muon by applying this same statistical framework to the gradient direction rather than the full gradient. By combining this with a whitening step, we arrive at MN-Muon, a novel preconditioned version of Muon. Our experiments on GPT-2-type models at Chinchilla scale indicate that MND, the diagonal implementation of MN, outperforms both the Distributed Shampoo and the KL-Shampoo implementations in terms of stability, final validation loss and time taken. MND-Muon, the diagonal implementation of MN-Muon, improves on Muon in 21 of 24 paired GPT-small runs and achieves a lower best validation loss on GPT-medium, where it remains stable over the six tested learning rates. However, we found that NorMuon was comparable to MND-Muon and achieved a slightly lower validation loss on GPT-medium and at GPT-small batch 512 on FineWeb.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. Provides a statistical viewpoint on stochastic optimization methods like Adam and Shampoo, improving optimizer design for training language models but not specifically about RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion models, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6
