## 133. [Value-Rich, Differently Organized: Measuring Value Expression and Organization in AI-Generated Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28604) <a id="link133"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28604
**Authors:** Maoyuan Li, Haoyuan Li, Zhongsheng Wang, Jiamou Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28604v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Current evaluation of LLM-generated debate largely focuses on argumentative quality, leaving the value structure of generated arguments underexamined. We introduce a diagnostic metric framework for comparing how values are expressed and organized in human-reference and AI-generated debates. The framework separates value expression, the range of recognizable values a debate invokes, from value organization, how those values are distributed and sustained across turns. Grounded in Schwartz's Basic-10 value theory, it represents debates as multi-level value distributions over argumentative units, utterances, stances, and full debates, enabling metrics for value coverage, alignment, diffusion, and cross-turn dynamics. Across multiple generation systems, AI-generated debates are broadly value-expressive, often activating as many or more value dimensions than matched human references, but they organize these values differently. Their utterances are more locally value-diffuse, and source-blind judges distinguish value clarity from value focus. These findings show that debate evaluation should consider not only value presence, but also value organization. Code and supplementary materials are available at https://github.com/Maoyuan-li/Value-Expression-and-Organization.

**Comment:** Focuses on evaluation of value expression and organization in AI-generated debates, proposing new metrics for value coverage and alignment. Relevant to evaluation paradigms (criterion 4) but more about value structure than open-ended text generation evaluation.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 134. [Latent State Utilization in Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24315) <a id="link134"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24315
**Authors:** Zilei Shao, Anji Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24315v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) are generative models that support efficient probabilistic inference. Adding latent states to a PC increases its representational capacity, but yields diminishing gains in likelihood. We use mutual information to examine how the dependence represented by a trained PC changes with latent size. Experiments with Hidden Chow-Liu Trees (HCLTs) on ImageNet32 show that learned dependence grows slowly and remains well below both the latent-state upper bound and estimates of dependence in the data. To understand how learning uses these states, we examine how strongly each sample favors particular latent states and how broadly states are used across the dataset. Tracking these measurements during HCLT training distinguishes rapid early posterior concentration from the more gradual stabilization of sample-dependent state preferences. Early measurements also guide initialization selection in HCLTs and hidden Markov models (HMMs), as well as tree selection in HCLTs. The resulting choices improve the ultimate performance across the tested latent sizes, providing a practical benefit from our study of latent-state utilization.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 18 (data-centric AI) and statistical machine learning theory. The paper studies latent state utilization in probabilistic circuits, analyzing mutual information and dependence, and provides practical insights for initialization and model selection. However, it does not address RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or instruction-following.

## 135. [Uniform convergence at optimal rate and scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24336) <a id="link135"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24336
**Authors:** Noga Alon, Steve Hanneke, Shay Moran, Tom Waknine

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24336v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Uniform convergence asks how many random samples suffice for empirical averages to approximate expectations simultaneously over an entire class of functions. It is a central concept in probability and a foundation of statistical learning. For binary-valued classes, the Vapnik--Chervonenkis theorem characterizes uniform convergence by finiteness of the VC dimension. For dimension $d$, the sharp worst-case sample complexity for additive error $\varepsilon$ is of order $d/\varepsilon^2$. For real-valued functions, the analogous combinatorial parameter is the fat-shattering dimension, which depends on an approximation scale $\gamma$. The challenge is to achieve error $\gamma+\varepsilon$ with order $d/\varepsilon^2$ samples, matching the sample bound for binary classes, where $d$ is now the fat-shattering dimension at scale $\gamma$. Previous results achieved either the optimal scale or the optimal sample complexity, but not both. Our main result closes this gap, establishing the sharp sample complexity at the optimal scale. Along the way, we use several tools that may be of independent interest. The first concerns $\varepsilon$-nets with margin. A classical $\varepsilon$-net intersects every set of mass at least $\varepsilon$ in a prescribed family; we allow the net to intersect a slightly enlarged version of each such set. Remarkably, this relaxation permits bounds independent of the ambient dimension in natural geometric settings. Second, we derive improved covering bounds in both discrete and real-valued settings: we extend Haussler's covering lemma to partial concept classes and use this extension to obtain $\ell_2$ covers at the optimal scale for real-valued function classes. Third, we obtain a chaining bound at the optimal scale by aligning the covers so that successive approximations become increasingly close to one another.

**Comment:** This paper provides a theoretical advance in uniform convergence sample complexity for real-valued function classes, which is foundational to statistical learning theory but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or language model evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 7

## 136. [Nash-MOPD: Optimizing Task Trade-offs in Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26124) <a id="link136"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26124
**Authors:** Siyuan Gan, Bo Yang, Linjian Meng, Boyan Wang, Xiaolong Qi, Jing Huo, Yang Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26124v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) aggregates the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers into a single student model. However, all domains in MOPD share the student's finite budget of optimization steps and rollouts. Fixed uses constant domain sampling ratios based on corpus sizes; by contrast, D$^3$-MOPD adjusts the ratios using the teacher-student reverse-KL divergence and its recent decrease. However, favoring large recent decreases can direct more training toward domains that already improve quickly, while domains that improve more slowly may receive too few examples. To address inflexible allocation and the risk of giving insufficient training to domains that improve more slowly, we propose Nash-MOPD, an adaptive domain scheduler based on a local minimax game using existing reverse-KL statistics. The equilibrium maximizes the smallest predicted proportional reduction under the local model and gives more prompts to domains that need more examples for comparable improvement. Consequently, Nash-MOPD responds to changing learning progress and supports domains that improve more slowly without extra teacher queries or rollouts. At step 100, Nash-MOPD exceeds both baselines' final mathematics and code checkpoint scores on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and reaches Fixed's on Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, it leads eight of nine reported metrics and is best or tied best on all six mathematics and code accuracy metrics, respectively.

**Comment:** Discusses multi-teacher on-policy distillation with an adaptive domain scheduler optimizing task trade-offs. While related to distillation and training strategies, it does not explicitly mention RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or contamination detection. Not a direct match to criteria.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 137. [MemBubble: Benchmarking How Agent Memory Shapes LLM Response Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25127) <a id="link137"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25127
**Authors:** Jiajun Tan, Fei Sun, Xinyu Ma, Shixuan Zhang, Du Su, Qi Cao, Daiting Shi, Huawei Shen, Xueqi Cheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25127v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Memory serves as the core component of LLM-based agents, enabling personalized responses via extracting and using user preferences from past interactions. However, its use may reduce response diversity, thereby narrowing the range of information available to users. Existing evaluations rely largely on synthetic interactions and cover few memory systems, leaving the effects of memory on response diversity insufficiently evaluated in real-world scenarios. We introduce MemBubble, a benchmark of 1000 instances built on preferences extracted from real user-assistant conversations. To measure the extent to which memory concentrates response distributions, we construct test queries in scenarios distinct from user preferences while preserving topical relevance through shared preference categories. Across multiple LLMs and memory mechanisms, entropy decreases by 34.7% on average compared with directly providing the full interaction history, highlighting the important role of memory processing in diversity loss. We also explore simple user-side interventions and changes in information availability but find that these do not effectively close the diversity gap. Our findings highlight the need to consider preserving diversity and user access to information alongside improvements in personalization when designing and using agent memory systems.

**Comment:** Related to real-world usage and safety properties of LLMs (criterion 5) by analyzing how agent memory affects response diversity in real user data. However, it does not focus on instruction-following improvements or contamination or generative modeling advances.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

## 138. [Beyond 4 bits: Data-Centric Post-training Quantization and Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28537) <a id="link138"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28537
**Authors:** Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker, Riccardo Grazzi, James Hensman, Pashmina Cameron

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28537v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses the weights of a trained model with limited compute and data. Down to 4 bits it is near-lossless, but at 2 bits scalar PTQ configurations fall to chance-level accuracy. We study three choices usually treated as implementation details that matter little at 4 bits but decide 2-bit quality: the quantization representation, the calibration data, and the recovery fine-tuning data, across six dense and mixture-of experts models (ranging from $3.8$ to $32$B parameters). Representation is the largest factor and vector codebooks restore usable models where scalar PTQ pipelines collapse. Most of the remaining loss in accuracy is on \emph{generative} tasks, and the amount of performance drop is model-dependent. Furthermore, at a short recovery budget, calibration and recovery data are coupled. The best recovery data depends on the calibration data, so the two must be chosen jointly. The resulting recipe involves a vector codebook representation, a small task-aligned calibration mixture, and an inexpensive codebook-only recovery stage. Including codebooks, it uses about $2.3$ effective bits per weight and retains $89$ to $97\%$ of full-precision question-answering (QA) accuracy where scalar PTQ is at chance, and, including recovery, uses $1.4$ to $30\times$ less compute than the strongest quantization-aware-training (QAT) baseline.

**Comment:** This paper studies post-training quantization methods for large language models, focusing on data-centric calibration and recovery fine-tuning to preserve generative task performance at very low bit widths. While it involves generative language models and fine-tuning steps, it does not specifically address RLHF or instruction-following improvements (criterion 1), test set contamination (2), diffusion LMs (3), or other listed criteria. It is somewhat relevant to generative modeling but not to the specific criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 139. [Reasoning with Context Management and External Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24187) <a id="link139"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24187
**Authors:** Man-Ling Sung, William Hartmann, Jiangying Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24187v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning models maintain information about the status of their reasoning process through in-context memory. Complex problems require larger context windows and memory requirements. In this scenario, context management without caching risks discarding useful information for reasoning. We propose RCMEM, an approach that extends context management with an external memory to maintain additional information without burdening the reasoning model with additional context. RCMEM significantly reduces computation time on problems that require repeated computation of intermediate steps, while achieving better performance on complex problems. We demonstrate our approach on a few mathematics problems using model sizes ranging from 0.4 to 25 million parameters, and GPT-2.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes an external memory mechanism to improve reasoning with context management in language models, but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or new evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 140. [CoEvo-RSI: When Harnesses Evolve, Which Models Should Follow?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25078) <a id="link140"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25078
**Authors:** Jiaqi Su, Guibin Zhang, Jie-Jing Shao, Cong Pang, Tiankuo Yao, Ivor Tsang, Haiyan Yin, Shuicheng Yan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25078v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Model--harness co-evolution has emerged as a prominent paradigm for recursive self-improving (RSI) agents, jointly evolving their execution scaffolds and intrinsic capabilities. We study the persistent yet often overlooked brain--body mismatch problem in this paradigm, where an evolving harness (body) demands behaviors its model (brain) has not learned or restricts capabilities it already possesses. We introduce CoEvo-RSI, a RSI framework that advances model--harness co-evolution through agent-driven, bidirectional historical inheritance. Drawing on a shared evolutionary archive and execution feedback, a meta-agent coordinates harness development from historical designs with model checkpoint selection or merging and subsequent adaptation under the selected harness, aligning inherited capabilities with evolving execution requirements to reduce brain--body mismatch. Using only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on its own rejection-sampled trajectories, CoEvo-RSI makes Qwen3.5-9B competitive with larger models across diverse vertical applications, including multi-agent failure attribution, role-aware text-to-SQL, and smart-home instruction following. It surpasses DeepSeek-V4-Pro on TraceElephant (+14.16 agent accuracy) and GLM-5.3 on HomeBench VM (+4.90 success rate), underscoring the need for brain--body alignment and the potential of bidirectional historical inheritance to advance agent co-evolution.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Discusses supervised fine-tuning and model-harness co-evolution for instruction following and agent improvement, but focus is on recursive self-improving agents rather than direct RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

## 141. [Near-Optimal Last-Iterate Convergence of SGD in the Smooth Interpolation Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25469) <a id="link141"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25469
**Authors:** Matan Schliserman, Tomer Koren

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25469v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study the last iterate of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) with a constant stepsize on smooth convex objectives in the low-noise and interpolation regimes, where the variance $\sigma_\star^2$ of the stochastic gradients at the optimum is small or zero. We establish that after $T$ steps of SGD on $\beta$-smooth convex losses with any constant stepsize $0<\eta<2/\beta$, the last iterate attains expected excess risk $\widetilde O\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta(2-\beta\eta)^3T}+\tfrac{\eta}{(2-\beta\eta)^4}\sigma_\star^2\bigr)$, which gives the rate $\smash{\widetilde O(1/T)}$ for every constant stepsize in the interpolation regime ($\sigma_\star=0$), matching the $\Omega(1/T)$ lower bound up to logarithmic factors and improving upon the $O(1/\sqrt T)$ rate of Attia et al. (2026) for $\eta=1/\beta$. Our analysis is based on a new technique, which compares the second moments of the stochastic gradients along the SGD trajectory across only logarithmically many time scales. For quadratic losses in the interpolation regime, an adaptation of this technique gives an $O(1/T)$ rate for every constant stepsize, extending the recent optimal rate for the randomized Kaczmarz method of Derezinski and Dong (2026) to stepsizes above the greedy stepsize with an arguably simpler proof.

**Comment:** Theoretical analysis of SGD convergence rates in interpolation regime; relevant to statistical ML but not related to RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, or language model-specific phenomena.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 142. [Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676) <a id="link142"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26676
**Authors:** Qian Xie, Wenyue Hua, Yueli He, Sripad Karne, Armaan Agrawal, Nikos Pagonas, Eugene Wu, Kostis Kaffes, Tianyi Peng, Nairen Cao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26676v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a cost-efficient search method for assigning LLMs to roles in agentic workflows, focusing on cost-quality tradeoffs rather than RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 143. [How Much Do Activation Monitors Benefit from Original Model Computations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24613) <a id="link143"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24613
**Authors:** James Oldfield, Shawn Im, Jay J Culligan, Philip Torr, Sharon Li, Adel Bibi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24613v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: One powerful way to safeguard AI systems is to monitor their internal computations--where signs of undesirable behavior might be more easily detectable than from models' inputs and outputs alone. However, recent studies begin to question the premise that activations provide a unique window into model behavior. In this paper, we study how much of activation monitors' success depends on access to the original behavior-generating computations. Within a fixed choice of target model and monitoring architecture, we compare how well monitors trained and evaluated on trajectories that differ in computational context can predict the behavior of the original execution, after the transcript is changed in a controlled manner (e.g., its exact text, or chat formatting). We first find that much performance can be recovered without access to the original behavior-generating trace (in up to 4 models across 2 counterfactual monitoring tasks), but a mild yet consistent advantage to native-trained monitors remains, outperforming strongest controls by up to 0.014 AUROC. Interestingly, we find monitors trained with native access have notable uplift on original examples whose behavioral label changes under paraphrasing. We further find this a useful lens to expose limitations of baselines that use simple forms of privileged information (i.e., answer logit margins). While our results show the advantage of native monitor access is modest in aggregate, they highlight the importance of evaluations that identify if and when original model computations provide additional predictive value.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies activation monitors for AI systems, which relates to interpretability but does not explicitly mention sparse autoencoders or mechanistic interpretability techniques. It is more about monitoring and predictive value of activations.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

## 144. [Trust Your Targets: Is Target Propagation Block-Diagonal Gauss-Newton?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26283) <a id="link144"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26283
**Authors:** Giuseppe Castiglione, Christopher Buckley, Ivor Simpson

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26283v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Target propagation (TP) is a backpropagation alternative in which local targets carry the global learning signal. Prior works claim that TP performs Gauss-Newton (GN) optimisation on these targets, but the correspondence has only been established under unrealistic assumptions where hitting them exactly is trivial. Practical variants instead descend on a least-squares problem via a single gradient step. In this paper, we rederive TP from a trust region under an implicit, previously unexamined norm, and introduce CGTP (Conjugate Gradient Target Propagation), which uses conjugate gradients to solve the layer-local subproblems in parallel. A single adaptive parameter jointly controls this trust region and a matching bound on the weight update; KFAC emerges exactly as the special case where the per-sample Jacobians are replaced by their batch mean, giving its factored damping a variational grounding. On MNIST and Fashion-MNIST MLPs, CGTP matches the accuracy of ADAM and KFAC while needing orders of magnitude fewer outer iterations than ADAM (about 10x fewer backward-pass-equivalents overall), while remaining stable across depth.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies target propagation as an alternative to backpropagation and introduces CGTP, which is a novel optimization method but unrelated to RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability of language models.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 145. [T-MUON: Tensorizing Muon via t-SVD for Large Language Model Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24938) <a id="link145"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24938
**Authors:** Dongyang Li, Mingsong Yan, Zheng Zhang, Sui Tang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24938v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a strong optimizer for large language model pretraining by exploiting matrix structure through polar orthogonalization. However, it processes each parameter matrix independently, whereas recent tensor-aware extensions of Muon jointly update groups of eligible matrix parameters through matrix unfoldings. In contrast, we propose T-MUON, a t-SVD-based generalization that groups structurally related Transformer gradients into third-order tensors and defines the corresponding Muon-style update directly through the $M$-transform. We show that the exact T-MUON update is a steepest-descent direction under the $\star_M$-spectral norm and establish a convergence guarantee. Across GPT-2-style and LLaMA-style models from 51M to 1.3B parameters trained on FineWeb and FineWeb-Edu, head-wise T-MUON with the identity transform consistently achieves lower validation perplexity than AdamW, Muon, and variants using DFT, DCT, or DST transforms. These gains persist in additional experiments with longer context lengths and larger token budgets, although the preferred tensorization mode varies across settings.

**Comment:** Relevant to general optimization methods for LLM pretraining but does not match any specific criterion related to RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 146. [RESIN: Multi-Chain Reasoning with Entity-Structured Indexing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26782) <a id="link146"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26782
**Authors:** Xuanyu Meng, Jash Rajesh Parekh, Jiawei Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26782v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-chain reasoning is a challenging task not only because the multi-chain evidence is dispersed, but also because different parts of reasoning may require distinct, interacting chains of support. Flat retrieval can flood a model with loosely related text, whereas early selection of a single reasoning path can discard evidence that becomes useful only after other facts are considered. We introduce RESIN (Reasoning with Entity Structured Indexing), which uses structure to locate evidence while keeping the original source as the basis for reasoning. RESIN decomposes a question into focused one-hop needs, retrieves a small set of source-grounded units for each hop, and places only this relevant evidence in a compact reasoning buffer. The buffer preserves alternative supports until final composition, making intermediate decisions explicit without forcing a brittle path. On the full HolisQA benchmark, RESIN achieves 82.92 context recall and 76.70 answer relevancy over all questions, with higher reported CR and AR across five domains and higher F1 in four domains than the published baselines. Ablations show that both typed evidence diversity and hop-wise decomposition are essential, supporting a clear conclusion: Precise evidence organization and delayed commitment make multi-chain reasoning effective and auditable. Our code is available at https://github.com/RamonMeng/RESIN.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any criteria; focuses on multi-chain reasoning with entity-structured indexing for retrieval, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 147. [Improving the Expressive Power of Hybrid Sequence Models via Multi-Hop Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25250) <a id="link147"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25250
**Authors:** Yam Eitan, Gilad Yehudai, Haggai Maron

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25250v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Hybrid architectures offer a promising approach to efficient language modeling by combining a small number of full-attention layers with a larger number of efficient sequence layers, such as sparse attention and recurrent layers. While full-attention layers maintain a KV cache that grows linearly with sequence length, efficient layers typically rely on separate, fixed-size states rather than accessing this richer existing memory. Recent work has begun exploring ways to allow efficient layers access to the KV cache, but the theoretical benefits of such access, and the extent to which existing approaches can realize them remain poorly understood. We investigate these questions by analyzing the expressive power of individual hybrid blocks on function composition, a well-studied task in sequence-model expressivity associated with multi-hop reasoning. We first establish that both conventional hybrid blocks and those with top-$\kappa$ KV-cache access require $\Omega(\sqrt{n})$ capacity to compute even two-function composition over a domain of size $n$. To overcome this limitation, we introduce the \emph{Multi-hop Retrieval Attention} (MuRA) hybrid block, a simple architecture that enables multi-hop retrieval from a shared KV cache while preserving asymptotic computational and memory efficiency. We prove that a single fixed size $m$-hop MuRA block can compute $m$-function composition, whereas computing $(m+1)$-function composition with the same retrieval depth requires polynomial model size, establishing a strict expressivity hierarchy in retrieval depth. Synthetic experiments support this hierarchy, showing that increasing retrieval depth enables learning progressively deeper compositions, substantially outperforming existing hybrid architectures. Together, our results identify multi-hop retrieval as a promising mechanism for improving the expressive power of efficient hybrid architectures.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion; focuses on hybrid sequence models and multi-hop retrieval expressivity rather than instruction-following, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 148. [What Matters for NVFP4 Training? A Comparative Study of Low-Precision Pretraining Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24343) <a id="link148"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24343
**Authors:** Anjulie Agrusa, Andrei Panferov, Elizabeth Wei, Keith Wyss, Paul Gibbons, Erik Schultheis, Jinhang Choi, Frank Sun, Tijmen Blankevoort, Dan Alistarh

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24343v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training large language models directly in 4-bit floating-point (FP) formats promises substantial improvements in throughput and energy efficiency. While some recipe design choices have been validated at scale, many promising approaches remain untested beyond small models and short token horizons, leaving open the question of which trends will hold. We present a systematic comparison of recent NVFP4 recipes at medium model scale, evaluating dense 8B and 30B-A3B MoE models trained for up to 1T tokens, with a focus on which ingredients are necessary for accuracy recovery. We propose a final recipe grounded in the principles behind established stable NVFP4 training at scale, incorporating state-of-the-art techniques such as unbiased gradient estimation with lower quantization error than stochastic rounding. To the best of our knowledge, this is the strongest FP4 training result demonstrated at this scale to date in loss gap to BF16. Through ablation studies, we find that: (i) each technique in the optimized recipe measurably improves loss trajectory, (ii) selective high-precision layers are necessary for recovering accuracy at scale, (iii) not all tensors in the backward pass benefit equally from de-biasing, leaving room to apply complementary error-reduction techniques to the remaining tensors.

**Comment:** This paper studies low-precision pretraining recipes for large language models, focusing on training stability and accuracy recovery in 4-bit floating-point formats. It is about training methodology but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, or evaluation paradigms. It is relevant to generative modeling and training efficiency but not to the specific criteria.

## 149. [E8-QAT: Efficient 2-Bit Quantization-Aware Training with Implicit Lattice Codebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27699) <a id="link149"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27699
**Authors:** Xinyu Lei, Hong Huang, Jiebin Zhang, Hongyu Li, Jinlong Song, Decheng Wu, Guanghua Yu, Jianchen Zhu, Sujian Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27699v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Low-bit quantization reduces the memory footprint of large language models, but preserving accuracy with efficient training remains challenging. Scalar quantization encodes weights independently, while explicit-codebook vector quantization introduces costly search during training. We propose E8-QAT, a 2-bit quantization-aware training framework that uses the $E_8$ lattice as an implicit eight-dimensional codebook. It jointly quantizes weight groups through arithmetic projection without storing or searching an explicit codebook during the training forward pass. A 2-bit core encoding and sparse extension preserve the selected lattice points exactly, adding less than 0.1\% to core index storage. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, E8-QAT matches the performance of the full-precision model in terms of the average score across the four tasks. E8-QAT improves over the state-of-the-art baseline by 2.03\% on IFEval and by 1.51\% on LiveCodeBench, under the same data, schedule, and bit width. The 2-bit representation reduces peak memory by 80\% and supports a $4\times$ larger batch. At each representation's largest feasible batch, E8-QAT reaches $1.97\times$ the decoding throughput of BF16.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new efficient 2-bit quantization-aware training method for large language models, improving training efficiency and memory usage. While it relates to training large language models, it does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is more about quantization methods for efficient training.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 150. [Power-Law Correlations Without Apparent Temperature-Tuned Criticality in Fixed-Tree Hierarchical Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24334) <a id="link150"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24334
**Authors:** Marcel K\"uhn, Bernd Rosenow

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24334v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sampling temperature can substantially reshape autoregressive generation, but it is unclear when temperature changes in long-range correlations reflect a genuine phase transition rather than a smooth change in statistics. We study this question in a controlled analogue of temperature sampling: a finite-depth probabilistic context-free grammar on a fixed hierarchical tree, where exact next-token conditionals are computed by belief propagation and then rescaled by a softmax temperature before autoregressive sampling. We measure two-point mutual information between tokens as a function of separation. Across the tested temperatures and grammar ensembles, mutual information remains well described by a power-law envelope. Temperature changes the correlation magnitude and fitted exponent, but within the accessible system sizes we find no long-distance plateau characteristic of an ordered phase and no clear crossover to short-range exponential decay. An approximate mapping to an Ising model on a tree explains how exponential decay in tree distance becomes a power law in token distance, allowing power laws to persist without tuning to a special decoding temperature. This controlled baseline clarifies which temperature effects can arise from hierarchical structure alone, before adding training, approximation error, or neural architecture.

**Comment:** This paper studies power-law correlations in hierarchical language models under temperature scaling, using a probabilistic context-free grammar and belief propagation. It provides theoretical insights into correlation structures but does not focus on scaling laws of neural networks (criterion 6) or RLHF, contamination, diffusion, or evaluation paradigms. It is a theoretical study of sampling temperature effects in hierarchical models.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 151. [Dancing Diffusion: Learning Continuous Programs for Image Generation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28003) <a id="link151"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28003
**Authors:** Cui Enhe, Jinyue Yang, Bojian Yin, Guoqi Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28003v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion models generate samples through a multi-step denoising trajectory, yet condition every step on the same instruction, so the trajectory cannot be addressed at the level of individual operations. We introduce Dancing Diffusion, which assigns each span of timesteps a dedicated continuous condition block and thereby turns conditioning into a latent program. A compiler maps text features into this program, and a fixed pretrained generator executes its blocks over successive sampling intervals. The trajectory can then dance, keeping its executed history while its continuation is redirected. A shared prefix is executed once and reused, and a later operation is rewritten while content fixed by earlier operations is preserved. The compiler is trained through the images it produces, with visual feedback on both the requested change and the retained content. On SANA, reusing an executed prefix preserves markedly more of the scene than regenerating from noise at a similar strength of edit, learned conditions preserve more background than native prompt switching from the same saved state, and the learned program improves generation quality over native SANA. Later branches weaken the edit, which leaves semantic control over individual operations limited. By converting a single-instruction trajectory into a program of addressable operations, Dancing Diffusion lays a foundation for structured intermediate computation in diffusion models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a significant advance in diffusion language models by introducing Dancing Diffusion, a method that learns continuous programs for generation/editing trajectories, enabling structured intermediate computation in diffusion models for images. However, it focuses on image generation, not language models, so relevance is limited.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 152. [Mixture-of-Memories: Low-Overhead Language Modeling with Modular Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25425) <a id="link152"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25425
**Authors:** Yifan Lu, Rubin Wei, Ziyun Zou, Junhong Liang, Haolin Yang, Xinlin Zhuang, Abhijit Das, Adinath Madhavrao Dukre, Shadab Khan, Zhouhan Lin, Imran Razzak

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25425v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models store substantial factual knowledge in their parameters, yet this knowledge is difficult to control, update, and reliably elicit at inference time. Existing memory-augmented approaches typically rely on a single auxiliary mechanism, such as retrieval, parameter updates, or neural memory, and therefore apply the same form of correction to tokens with very different knowledge demands. We propose Mixture-of-Memories (MoM), a memory-augmented language modeling framework that separates routed neural memory from writable factual memory. MoM combines a frozen base LM with two neural memory experts: a latent memory that provides low-latency hidden-state corrections and a lexical memory that provides a token-space knowledge pathway. A lightweight Budget Router sets per-token mixture weights from uncertainty and disagreement features, replacing the fixed interpolation of prior memory modules. For post-hoc updates, an external explicit memory stays outside the router, because a router trained before facts are written learns to down-weight it; new facts are appended without retraining any component and used only when an answer-level gate accepts the retrieved fact. Experiments across language modeling, factual QA, update analysis, and efficiency show that MoM improves both predictive accuracy and updateability while retaining low inference overhead.

**Comment:** Relevant to general language modeling improvements but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. Focuses on modular memory injection for factual knowledge.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 153. [Consistency Training Suppresses Cue Following Without Teaching Cue Selectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25650) <a id="link153"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25650
**Authors:** Prakhar Gupta, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Zhijing Jin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25650v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models often change answers because of a cue, a hint such as "I believe the answer is B" that gives no valid evidence. Consistency training (CT) trains a model to answer the same with and without the cue. Four CT methods apply this idea to output tokens (BCT), residual activations (ACT), attention patterns (AttCT) and sampled-answer rates (RMCT). Prior work suggests some learn different solutions. We train all four on Gemma-2-2B-it and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct with the same data. We test predictions written in advance and inspect the models with a known circuit that carries cues to the answer. All four make the trained cue less effective, and their fixes share three properties. First, correct cues lose about as much influence as wrong ones. Second, the fix is usually large for cues before the answer options, as in training, and much smaller after them. Llama RMCT depends least on position. Third, the circuit heads still carry most remaining cue-induced errors. Making BCT targets depend on the cue's correctness mostly weakens the fix. CT thus weakens the trained cue without teaching models when a cue is right. Evaluations with only wrong cues at the trained position miss the first two properties.

**Comment:** Somewhat related to criterion 1: studies consistency training methods to suppress cue following in language models, which is related to instruction-following robustness, but does not propose new fine-tuning methods for RLHF or instruction tuning explicitly.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 154. [Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119) <a id="link154"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25119
**Authors:** Yiming Xu, Qian Yang, Zihan Chen, Yuefei Wu, Wenhan Zeng, Bin Shi, Bo Dong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25119v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: discusses memory valuation and continual adaptation in LLM agents, which relates to instruction-following improvements and agent self-evolution, but does not explicitly mention RLHF or instruction-tuning methods. More about memory mechanisms than fine-tuning or instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 155. [MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26825) <a id="link155"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26825
**Authors:** Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jingsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26825v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

**Comment:** Focuses on memory-native foundation models with internalized memory mechanisms and training pipelines. Does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. More about model architecture and memory capabilities.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 156. [Dual-Slot: From Serial Loops to Parallel Slots in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25086) <a id="link156"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25086
**Authors:** Yiqin Hao, Zitong Wang, Boyi Zeng, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, Yifan Liu, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25086v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped language models increase computational depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offering a way to improve model quality without adding parameters. However, sequential dependencies between loop iterations increase autoregressive decoding latency. We introduce Dual-Slot, an architecture that reorganizes serial looped computation into parallel latent and prediction slots. Rather than waiting for the current token's latent computation to finish, the prediction slot combines the previous token's deep latent state with the current representation, while the latent slot produces a state for the next decoding step. This shifts the latent dependency across adjacent tokens, replacing two sequential module invocations with a single paired invocation while preserving standard autoregressive generation. To preserve token-level parallelism during training, we use a small number of Jacobi iterations to refine latent states across the sequence in parallel. Experiments on Qwen-style dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) models show consistent improvements in language modeling and average downstream accuracy. On the 1B dense model, Dual-Slot reduces Pile validation perplexity from 7.25 to 7.00 and improves average five-shot accuracy across nine task configurations by 2.26 percentage points. After supervised fine-tuning, it improves zero-shot GSM8K accuracy by 5.83 and 3.49 percentage points for the 1B dense and A0.6B MoE models, respectively. Across the evaluated batch sizes, Dual-Slot retains 93.1-96.5% of vanilla decoding throughput for the dense model and 98.1-99.6% for the MoE model.

**Comment:** Related to language model architecture improvements for looped transformers, but does not explicitly address instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. Mostly an architectural efficiency improvement, not matching specific criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 157. [Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26834) <a id="link157"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26834
**Authors:** Anamaria-Roberta Hartl, Levente Z\'olyomi, Michael List, G\"unter Klambauer, Sebastian B\"ock, Sepp Hochreiter

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26834v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to generative modeling and language models via a novel linear attention mechanism (KronLSTM) with stable memory dynamics, but does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 158. [Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26286) <a id="link158"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26286
**Authors:** Chenhao Zhou, Jiyu Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26286v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. Existing robust reinforcement-learning theory does not yet provide such an operator-learning perspective beyond specialized multi-agent settings. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. Small-scale numerical experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return-gap trade-off predicted by the framework.

**Comment:** Related to reinforcement learning but focuses on robust zero-sum Markov games with function approximation, not specifically on RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 159. [LatComm: Reusable Context Compression for Latent Communication between Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26840) <a id="link159"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26840
**Authors:** Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tan Yu, Weijie Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26840v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Systems that combine several large language models (LLMs) often use one context for many questions. When the context is passed between models as text, the receiving model processes all of its tokens again for every question. Avoiding this cost requires a context representation that is compact, reusable across questions, and readable by a different LLM. Existing compression and latent communication methods each provide only some of these properties. We introduce LatComm, a Latent Communication interface that provides all three by separating encoding from reading: the context is encoded once without the question, and the receiver selects what to read for each question. A frozen context encoder LLM compresses the context into a fixed-size packet and sends it to a different frozen receiver LLM, which reads the packet through cross-attention, with gates computed from its prompt. On 12 multiple-choice question-answering datasets, LatComm improves average accuracy over single-vector baselines by up to 10.2 points. On QuALITY, it reduces the receiver's input by 57x and answers the questions of an article 3x faster.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling and efficiency in LLM workflows but does not match any specific criteria such as RLHF improvements, contamination detection, diffusion models, or evaluation paradigms. Focuses on latent communication and context compression between LLMs.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 160. [Training Negatives Still Matter After Calibrating Answerability Readouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26461) <a id="link160"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26461
**Authors:** Jinchen Zhang, Zheyuan Liu, Yihan Zhu, Meng Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26461v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Answerability readouts predict whether a passage supplies an answer, but whether calibration can compensate for removing negative passages during training remains unclear. We present a controlled study of how training-negative construction affects calibrated prediction, ranking, and downstream answer retention. We compare native training, which retains negative passages, with empty training, which removes them, holding questions, labels, positive examples, and model weights fixed. Both are calibrated on the same labeled native examples. On SQuAD 2.0 and RepLiQA, native-trained logistic readouts at selected depths achieve lower Brier loss for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Gemma-2-9B-it, with or without output scores. Native fusion also outperforms calibrated output alone. Post-hoc AUROC analysis shows better ranking under native training, beyond what threshold adjustment alone can explain. On the official SQuAD 2.0 development set, native readout policies improve F1 over empty policies but retain more answers to unanswerable questions. The advantage varies by estimator and depth, while RepLiQA's text shortcuts limit claims about contextual understanding. Unfinished generations prevent complete answer-level evaluation, although worst-case bounds preserve the native-empty F1 gains. Our findings show that training negatives affect ranking and downstream decisions even after common calibration, without establishing a universal advantage for native training.

**Comment:** This paper analyzes the effect of training negatives on calibrated answerability readouts in language models, studying calibration and ranking performance. It involves calibration and evaluation of language model outputs but does not propose new methods for RLHF or instruction-following (1), test contamination (2), diffusion LMs (3), or new evaluation paradigms (4). It is somewhat relevant to statistical ML and language model evaluation but not a direct match to criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 161. [AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950) <a id="link161"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25950
**Authors:** Rui Wang, Zishen Lai, Jiayu Liu, Cheng Qian, Jeonghwan Kim, Heng Wang, Xuejun Zhang, Jiateng Liu, Bingxuan Li, Shujin Wu, Qing Zong, Tianshi Zheng, Chunyang Li, Qihan Lin, Chen Li, Dadi Guo, Xiusi Chen, Yangqiu Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25950v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper introduces a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments for LLM agents, focusing on environment understanding and adaptation rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 162. [An Empirical Study of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26370) <a id="link162"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26370
**Authors:** Minglei Li, Peng Ye, Haonan He, Jingqi Ye, Qingyu Yang, Tao Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26370v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods are critical for adapting large models under resource-constrained settings. However, as mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures, which offer efficient inference at massive parameter scales, gain increasing prevalence in the community, a critical gap arising from the lack of systematic recipes for applying PEFT to MoE models. To narrow this gap, we conduct a large-scale systematic study that investigates a set of essential design recipes, including which modules should be adapted, which granularity should be used to adapt MoE modules, which PEFT method to use, and to what extent should the load-balancing objective be used, across three MoE backbones on downstream tasks and general knowledge tasks. Our experiments yield several key insights. First, adapting MoE experts alone achieves the highest downstream performance with competitive general capability retention under a comparable parameter budget. Second, native expert-weight updates offer a better downstream--retention trade-off than adapters that bypass experts or entire MoE modules. Third, LoRA and DoRA together define the strongest operating region for high downstream accuracy among the evaluated PEFT methods. Fourth, no nonzero load-balancing coefficient Pareto-dominates the zero baseline. We finally evaluate a weight-level redesign of MoE$^2$-LoRA on Qwen3. Moving its module-level bypass pool into native expert weights improves all four upstream endpoints by 2.4 to 17.9 points with 17% fewer trainable parameters and a 3.2-point average downstream cost.

**Comment:** Related to fine-tuning large models but focuses on parameter-efficient fine-tuning for mixture-of-experts architectures, not specifically on RLHF or instruction-following improvements. Does not explicitly mention RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 163. [DIVE: Benchmarking Full-Duplex Interaction with Visual Engagement in Continuous Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28405) <a id="link163"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28405
**Authors:** Yuhang Chen, Guangyue Liu, Shilong Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28405v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially improved spoken interaction and video understanding. Existing online and streaming benchmarks primarily assess causal multimodal perception through timestamped question answering, whereas real-world interaction requires models to continuously identify task-relevant multimodal cues and decide whether, when, and how to respond. This leaves open how well current models can sustain real-time interaction throughout an evolving multimodal stream. To address this gap, we introduce DIVE, a benchmark for evaluating full-duplex interaction with visual engagement under continuous and realistic multimodal streams. It organizes six tasks into three complementary interaction scenarios. (1) Proactive Intervention evaluates whether models can identify emerging visual or semantic cues and intervene at the appropriate moment. (2) Continuous Engagement evaluates sustained participation through continuous visual tracking and visually grounded query interaction. (3) Interaction Coordination evaluates how models coordinate listening and speaking under overlapping and multi-party interactions. DIVE contains 525 audio/video samples with fine-grained human annotations and precise temporal metadata. Its evaluation protocol separates response quality, participation decisions, and response timing. We evaluate representative real-time models using response quality, window response rates, and reaction latency. Experiments show that leading real-time models achieve sub-second average response latency, yet their average window response rates remain at or below 53.77%. Response quality varies substantially across models and tasks, with adapting ongoing responses to newly arriving information remaining particularly challenging. We hope DIVE facilitates progress toward more natural and capable real-time multimodal interactive systems.

**Comment:** This paper introduces a benchmark for continuous multimodal interaction with visual engagement, focusing on real-time multimodal LLMs. It does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 164. [Representations Have Trajectories: Characterizing Temporal Representation Dynamics in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24571) <a id="link164"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24571
**Authors:** Shivaprasad B. Gowda, Sparsh Mittal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24571v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work has shown that deep neural networks exhibit an implicit low-rank simplicity bias. In this paper, we investigate the questions that naturally arise from this observation. What are the temporal consequences of low-rank simplicity bias during optimization? How does representation geometry evolve throughout optimization rather than only at convergence? Do different neural architectures exhibit distinct geometric optimization behaviors? To answer these questions, we conduct a large-scale empirical study tracking representational geometry throughout training. We find that neural networks follow three recurring geometric trajectories: persistent high-rank, collapse-and-recovery, and persistent low-rank. We further identify Destructive Representation Collapse (DRC) as a pathological regime in which representations collapse to rank-one and fail to recover, leading to a sustained loss of representational capacity. While collapse-and-recovery is consistently associated with successful optimization in controlled settings, experiments on real-world models show that the relationship between geometric trajectories and optimization outcomes is configuration dependent. Overall, our findings suggest that representation geometry evolves dynamically during training and provide a new perspective on how deep neural networks learn.

**Comment:** This paper studies temporal dynamics of representation geometry during training in neural networks, focusing on low-rank trajectories and representation collapse. It does not match any specific criterion related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability of LLMs.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 165. [Cognitively Decoupled Meta-Induction for Out-of-Distribution Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25749) <a id="link165"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25749
**Authors:** Yuqian Wang, Yuanzheng Wang, Chaojun Xiao, Ke Xu, Bingxiang He, Jinghan Tan, Zijun Chen, Lu Chen, Yi-Xin Huo, Maosong Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25749v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) tackles downstream tasks conditioned on demonstrations. This paradigm relies heavily on neighbor-based matching between queries and demonstrations, limiting out-of-distribution (OOD) performance. For better generalization, rule-based inductive reasoning derives task-level regularities, which we term task-level inductive representations (TIR), before directly answering. However, existing methods lack generalizable inductive ability across diverse tasks. Inspired by meta-learning, we propose Cognitive-Decoupling meta-Induction (CoDI) for TIR-based inductive reasoning on general tasks. CoDI comprises a Cognitive-Decoupling Framework for query-blind TIR induction, and a Dual-Loop Meta-Inductive Learning method for cross-task inducer optimization. Empirically, CoDI achieves substantial performance gains on five ID and OOD benchmarks, and preserves OOD gains without explicit induction at inference. Code is available at https://github.com/Yuqian-Wang2277/codi-meta-induction.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on meta-inductive reasoning and out-of-distribution generalization in in-context learning, which is related to language models but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or other listed criteria.

## 166. [An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291) <a id="link166"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24291
**Authors:** Nian Ran, Tianhe Jia, Zhengyang Mu, Yue Wang, Sebastian Brulin, Markus Olhofer, Mauricio A. Alvarez, Yang Liu, Richard Allmendinger

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24291v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. Yet rapid initial gains often give way to prolonged plateaus despite substantial remaining evaluation budgets. We investigate \emph{principle-level search collapse}, where an optimizer's search becomes confined to a narrow set of design principles, even though it may continue to generate distinct candidates and achieve small improvements. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies optimization behavior of LLM optimizers but does not focus on RLHF, instruction tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability. It is more about optimizer search dynamics in general.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 167. [Weighted State Souping for Efficient Long-Context Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25621) <a id="link167"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25621
**Authors:** William L. Tong, Aryo Lotfi, Emmanuel Abbe, Josh Susskind, Cengiz Pehlevan, Eran Malach

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25621v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: State space models (SSMs) combine fast inference and fixed state size with competitive language-modeling performance. Recently, state souping has been proposed as a procedure whereby an SSM independently encodes chunks of tokens like documents, tool definitions, or memory records. The resulting state encodings may be cached and recombined to cheaply construct a context for downstream queries. However, both state souping and vanilla SSMs deteriorate on long, crowded contexts, limiting their utility for long-horizon domains. To address this issue, we propose weighted state souping, a simple method that learns query-dependent weights for composing cached states. The resulting models improve long-context performance substantially across both single- and multi-hop document question answering, needle-in-a-haystack retrieval, variable tracking, and tool use. Our method can be further adapted into an efficient sparse variant, yielding near-constant encoding cost with respect to context length while preserving (and sometimes improving) performance. We also present simple theoretical arguments that illustrate why souping works and when it may fail, and discuss informally the advantages of weighted souping. Weighted state souping thus combines reusable context representations with selective retrieval, offering an efficient approach to language modeling over large collections of documents, tools, and other forms of structured context that increasingly dominate modern agentic workloads.

**Comment:** Related to language modeling and efficient long-context retrieval but does not match specific criteria such as RLHF improvements, contamination detection, diffusion models, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 168. [Trajectory Replay: Continuous-Time Mixture Transport for Lifelong Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26749) <a id="link168"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26749
**Authors:** Alessandro Palma, Charles Jones, Tristan Cinquin, Fabian J. Theis, Frederik Wenkel

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26749v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Adapting pre-trained continuous-time generative models to sequentially incoming data without forgetting earlier tasks is a central challenge in lifelong learning. A leading strategy, generative replay, uses a generative model trained on historical tasks to augment its own continual update with synthetic samples from experience. However, it requires full trajectory simulations at every training step, which accumulate discretization errors and can degrade the replayed historical distribution. Here, we propose trajectory replay, which bypasses sample generation by training an updated velocity field to match both a frozen historical velocity along its trajectories and the incoming task's velocity field, weighted by a stability-plasticity coefficient. Through the lens of continuous-time transport, we show the resulting optimum averages the two task velocities by their respective densities and generates the mixture of their probability paths. Combined with mean velocity distillation, trajectory replay accesses historical trajectories using only a few network evaluations per continual update. Empirically, it outperforms generative replay and distillation baselines on class-incremental image generation. In two biological case studies, trajectory replay extends generative perturbation models to new contexts and experimental batches across two data modalities while preserving treatment-effect prediction.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling and continual learning but does not match any specific criterion about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

Wellens, Yeming Wen, Mary Williamson, Yuncheng Wu, Fei Xia, Tian Xie, Anlu Xing, David Xu, Jacob Xu, Puxin Xu, Zheng Xu, Shen Yan, Megan Yang, Yining Yang, Nick Yannopoulos, Kevin Yao, Michihiro Zhang, Shengjia Zhao, Chao Zhou, Claire Zhou, Shuyan Zhou, Yanjun Zhou, Yaxuan Zhou, Jiacheng Zhu, Rosalie Zhu, Yun Zhu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24628v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. We release the model under the Apache 2.0 license to foster open-source development of capable, safe, local AI agents.

## 170. [REM: What to Store, When to Recall, What to Forget in Experience Memory for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27377) <a id="link170"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27377
**Authors:** Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaohan Chen, Xiaojing Yu, Cris Liu, Bingqing Song, Bin Xu, Suleiman A. Khan, You-Lin Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27377v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents now autonomously complete long-horizon tasks, such as repository-level coding, through hundreds of think-act steps, yet the experience each run produces is rarely reused on the next. Prior memory systems concentrate on retrieval, finding the most relevant past trajectory. Retrieval cannot observe the running agent, so it cannot decide whether the agent needs an experience, at which point, or for how long. We introduce REM (Reusable Experience Memory), a framework that manages the memory lifecycle from inside the agent's think-act loop through three explicit decisions. What to store: each completed trajectory is consolidated into a phase-structured document, two orders of magnitude smaller than the trajectory. When to recall: the document is injected at the start of the task; the costlier implementation record is withheld until an in-loop gate detects the agent struggling. What to forget: the record is evicted after a single exposure. On repository-level bug fixing (SWE-ContextBench Lite), REM retrieves the related trajectory more accurately than every state-of-the-art memory system (68.7% vs. 55.6% match@1) and improves, in every replicate, end-task resolution (27.6% vs. 24.9%) at 17% lower cost. Injecting the same experience as a summary or raw trajectory, even when oracle-retrieved, does not. Ablating any of the three decisions reduces resolution. Altogether, these results demonstrate that the bottleneck in experience reuse is not retrieval fidelity but how experience is stored and governed across its lifecycle, opening a control-theoretic perspective on agent memory.

**Comment:** Does not match any specific criterion. Proposes a memory management framework for LLM agents but not related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 171. [A Simple and General Approach to top-$k$ Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26407) <a id="link171"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26407
**Authors:** Motti Goldberger, Nils Rudi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26407v2 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This paper considers the Bayesian top-$k$ identification problem, wherein a decision maker is tasked with selecting $k$ items from a pool of items of unknown quality. To assess the items, the decision maker adaptively allocates a fixed sampling budget, where each sample of an item yields a noisy observation of its quality, with the objective of minimizing the expected opportunity cost of the selected set. We propose an approach that allocates each batch of samples by minimizing a surrogate objective derived from an upper bound on the expected opportunity cost. The approach is general: samples can be allocated in batches, the selection size $k$ is arbitrary, and it applies to many sampling distributions. It is also simple and intuitive. We prove that the method's posterior expected opportunity cost decays at an exponential rate of at least half the best rate achievable by any sampling rule. In simulations across a wide range of settings, the method is competitive with, and often improves upon, the best existing algorithm in each setting.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a Bayesian top-k selection method, which is a statistical machine learning contribution but unrelated to RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5
