## 172. [On Learning Expand-and-Sparsify Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26470) <a id="link172"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26470
**Authors:** Alireza Asghari, Parikshit Ram, Kaushik Sinha

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26470v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Expand-and-sparsify ($\texttt{EaS}$) representations lift an input to a much higher dimension through a linear map and keep only the $k$ largest coordinates. Existing theory builds that map at random and needs the expanded dimension to grow exponentially in the input dimension, which puts the construction out of practical reach. We ask what is gained by learning the map by gradient descent instead, and how that gain depends on how wide the expansion is. We sweep $73$ OpenML classification tasks and $48$ regression tasks under a protocol that holds the datasets, expanded dimensions, sparsity levels and splits fixed across every method, and we summarise each comparison by a median over datasets and a Wilcoxon signed rank test. We report four findings. First, learning the expansion helps most when the expansion is narrowest, and its advantage over a random map decays to nothing by the widest expansions we run, so a learned $\texttt{EaS}$ layer can be made small. Second, with the map learned, a top-$k$ representation that keeps at most a tenth of its coordinates is indistinguishable from a dense $\texttt{ReLU}$ layer of the same expanded dimension at every expansion factor we test, while GELU and SiLU are not. Third, when the representation is forced to be binary the sparse code is the better of the two, and the reason is that binarization costs it nothing while it costs the dense layer accuracy. Fourth, how sparse the representation can be is governed by how wide it is: the sparsity level that is free rises as the expansion grows, so there is no fixed limit on how sparse it can be. The regression tasks reproduce the first finding and qualify the second and third.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. Studies expand-and-sparsify representations and their learning dynamics, but not specifically sparse autoencoders for interpretability or other listed criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 173. [VaDar: Implicit Goal Conditioning via Value Disaggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26629) <a id="link173"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26629
**Authors:** Shashwat Saxena, Mehul Goel, Sreyas Venkataraman, Sarvesh Patil, Max Simchowitz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26629v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon tasks are important for modern AI and robotics, yet pose a challenge for reinforcement learning. Historically, a popular approach has been to decompose a task into subgoals, and learn a subgoal-conditioned policy to execute each subtask in sequence.   We introduce an alternative called Value Disaggregation (VaDar), which trains a single subgoal-independent policy to maximize a linear combination of per-subgoal critic values. Because subgoal information is needed only at training-time, VaDar removes the need for possibly costly or cumbersome subgoal-selection during policy deployment. Moreover, through careful experiments, we show that VaDar (1) is always competitive with, and frequently outperforms, natural baselines like goal-conditioning and (2) succeeds on tasks with non-sequential goal structure and multiple optimization objectives. In particular, VaDar is the first algorithm to solve challenging, long-horizon tasks in the RoboCasa suite, even when starting from a policy pretrained from behavior cloning with near-zero initial task success. Taken together, VaDar's successes suggest that subgoal information benefits actor-critic reinforcement learning by enhancing the efficacy of critic learning, whereas policy goal-conditioning is often unnecessary.

**Comment:** Related to reinforcement learning methods but focuses on subgoal decomposition and value disaggregation for long-horizon tasks, not on RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 174. [TEX: Test-Time Scaling Testing Agents via Execution-based Cross-Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27290) <a id="link174"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27290
**Authors:** Akash Gokul, Srijan Bansal, Yingbo Zhou, Semih Yavuz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27290v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Test-case generation is critical but remains challenging for language models. Existing test-time scaling methods either isolate candidates or aggregate traces, which do not scale to long-horizon tasks. We present TEX, a collaborative test-time scaling framework based on execution-driven cross-validation. TEX parallelizes agents to generate tests and patches, cross-executing each test against every patch to form a $K \times K$ feedback matrix. Agents use their matrix slices for refinement, enabling objective validation without verbose trace sharing. On SWT-Bench Verified, TEX achieves 86.4%, establishing a new state-of-the-art and improving +9.0% over single-agent baselines. Moreover, even random selection from TEX outputs (84.4%) surpasses the previous SOTA (84.0%), showing collaborative execution feedback improves solution quality, not just best-case samples. Our results highlight execution-grounded feedback as an effective scaling mechanism for long-horizon agentic tasks.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Proposes a test-time scaling framework for agentic test generation using execution-based cross-validation, but does not address RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 3

## 175. [More Memory, Worse Agents: Error Reproduction and Anti-Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27406) <a id="link175"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27406
**Authors:** Ruhan Wang, Kishan Panaganti, Dongruo Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27406v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents can improve across tasks without updating model parameters by storing experience from their own executions in external memory and reusing it to guide later decisions. A central design question is whether to retain only observed trajectories and outcomes or also the rules, summaries, and skills inferred from them. Retaining these abstractions allows agents to reuse strategies without repeatedly deriving them from raw trajectories. However, errors in stored abstractions can propagate to subsequent decisions through retrieval and reuse. They can also shape execution trajectories, which then become the basis for new abstractions that reproduce the original errors. We term this feedback mechanism error reproduction: errors are regenerated through a cycle of abstraction, execution, and re-abstraction, extending their persistence beyond direct reuse of the original faulty knowledge. We propose Anti-Persistence, a memory design that accumulates trajectories and outcomes while reconstructing task-specific guidance on demand. An adaptive selector uses prior outcomes to decide whether and how to synthesize guidance, which is discarded after execution. This prevents its direct reuse as a stored object, although indirect feedback through retained trajectories remains possible. Across different benchmarks and backbones, Anti-Persistence achieves higher overall held-out success and the lowest average LLM-call cost among the evaluated memory designs. These results support separating experience accumulation from abstraction persistence in memorybased self-improvement.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any specific criterion; discusses memory design in LLM agents and error reproduction, but not about RLHF, contamination, diffusion models, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 176. [Training with Ciphers to Enhance In-Context Terminology Learning For Translation from Rare Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24593) <a id="link176"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24593
**Authors:** Deevyanshu Malu, Deeptanshu Malu, Sunita Sarawagi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24593v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs with in-context examples (IC-Train) lets translation models adapt to new parallel data at deployment, but it is unclear whether they actually learn the translations of rare terms from those examples. We formalize this capability as in-context term learning (ICTL). We show that standard IC-Train develops this capability only weakly, and propose RareCipherFT, which strengthens it without any lexical resources. RareCipherFT pairs each training target with in-context examples that share a rare term with it. It then replaces that term with a character-shuffled cipher in both the context and the reference, so the term can only be recovered by copying from context. The ciphered fraction of training data is annealed to preserve translation quality. Across seven translation directions between English and Tamil, Assamese, Mizo, Nepali and Hausa, and four models from 1B to 7B parameters, RareCipherFT raises term recall over standard IC-Train by 5-15 absolute points without lowering COMET. Probes using synonyms, nonce words and controls for shared surface context show that these gains come from linking source terms to their in-context translations rather than from shortcuts.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria; focuses on fine-tuning for in-context terminology learning in translation, not on RLHF or instruction-following improvements or contamination.

## 177. [Meta Expert: Cross-Layer Expert Sharing for Storage- and Retrieval-Efficient MoE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27598) <a id="link177"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27598
**Authors:** Ermo Hua, Xiangyu Hong, Baiting Wu, Che Jiang, Haozheng Hou, Yicheng Gu, Hanjing Wang, Qirui Zeng, Ming Zhang, Ning Ding, Qipeng Guo, Youbang Sun, Biqing Qi, Bowen Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27598v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As model parameters scale up, sparsely activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches have become essential for model scaling. However, mainstream models currently adopt hierarchical architectures, where experts in different layers, although topologically independent, exhibit substantial similarity and redundancy in their stored content. Compared to the hierarchically arranged \textbf{Local Expert} in existing mainstream models, we hypothesize that inter-layer shared \textbf{Meta Expert} hold greater potential. To this end, we conduct a multi-dimensional empirical comparison between Local and Meta Experts, and find that models incorporating Meta Experts exhibit lower parameter redundancy, together with lower cross-entropy loss and higher downstream task accuracy. With the activated quantity and the expert granularity held fixed, \textbf{our model matches the performance of Vanilla MoE using only 70--80\% of the experts}, substantially improving the model's scaling potential. Beyond removing inter-layer redundancy among experts and improving information storage efficiency, our theoretical analysis further shows that, compared with Local Experts, Meta Experts provide hidden states with more flexible residual pathways, thereby improving information retrieval efficiency.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures with cross-layer expert sharing to improve storage and retrieval efficiency, which is relevant to model scaling but not specifically about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 178. [Dynamic Neural Optimal Transport for Subset Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24258) <a id="link178"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24258
**Authors:** Austin J. Brockmeier, Bilal Riaz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24258v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study optimal transport between distributions when only a subset of the target population is relevant to the source. The goal is to map the source distribution to a reweighted target distribution whose density ratio with respect to the full target lies in $[0, c]$. This constraint enables the transported distribution to select an appropriate target subpopulation while limiting how strongly any region of the target can be emphasized. We propose a dynamic neural optimal transport framework for this setting using two neural networks. The first parameterizes a time-dependent potential whose spatial gradient defines the velocity field and whose terminal value enforces the density-ratio constraint. The second parameterizes an interpolation between the source distribution and the learned terminal distribution. Unlike prior neural dynamic optimal transport methods, which typically assume a fixed terminal distribution, our formulation learns the terminal distribution within the support of the target. We demonstrate the method on positive-unlabeled learning, unpaired domain translation, and trajectory inference, where meaningful alignments exist between source and target distributions, but direct distribution matching is inappropriate because the target contains additional or irrelevant components

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a dynamic neural optimal transport method for subset alignment, which is a generative modeling technique but not related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 179. [Which Long Documents Matter? A Controlled Study of Data Selection for Context Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24469) <a id="link179"></a>

**Authors:** Junlong Jia, Jiang Zhou, Xing Wu, Chaochen Gao Tinghao Yu, Feng Zhang, Lilin Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24469v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Natural long documents are widely used for context extension, but which selection pipelines are effective remains unclear. We conduct a controlled comparison on Qwen2.5-3B at 128K under matched 6B-token continued-training budgets. Random sampling of natural long documents (random-long) outperforms repacking at longer evaluation lengths. Within the same candidate pool, selecting documents with high LongPPL key-token density yields further gains, unlike the evaluated generic-quality and LLM-judge selectors. We introduce truncLongPPL to reduce scoring cost by computing key-token density over a truncated prefix. At 128K, scoring the first half of the reference span yields downstream performance close to full-span scoring with a measured $2.28\times$ end-to-end scoring speedup. Across three training seeds, truncLongPPL achieves the highest mean scores among evaluated pipelines, exceeding random-long by 4.54 points on validation HELMET and 3.28 on held-out RULER, with gains most consistent in retrieval. At a 256K target context, it gains 2.82 average RULER points over random-long on Qwen2.5-3B. In short/long mixed training across Qwen2.5-3B, Qwen3-8B, and OLMo~3-7B, it gains 1.47--7.31 average RULER points over random-long mixing, with seven-task short-context averages within 0.23 points of the corresponding baselines. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Qwen3-8B and OLMo~3-7B models trained with selected long documents outperform their random-long counterparts by 1.13--1.67 points on LongBench v2 and 0.62--1.44 points on MRCR.

## 180. [ReTTT: Reflection-Driven Test-Time Training via Experience-Based Parametric Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23942) <a id="link180"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23942
**Authors:** Kangcong Li, Hangfan Zhang, Shuyue Hu, Shengji Tang, Tao Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23942v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents accumulate valuable task-specific information through their actions, observations, and environmental feedback during multi-turn task execution. When effectively used, this information can help agents refine subsequent decisions and improve task performance. However, the learning value of an interaction depends on its context and subsequent feedback, making it challenging to extract reliable, actionable lessons and revise them as the task unfolds. In this paper, we introduce ReTTT, which enables experience-driven self-adaptation through test-time training on lessons constructed and continually revised within an episode. ReTTT lets the agent reflect on interaction feedback, maintain the resulting lessons in an explicit memory, and learn from them by updating an episode-local LoRA adapter while keeping the backbone frozen. The adapted model then performs both subsequent actions and reflection, coupling policy adaptation with the construction of future learning experience. We evaluate ReTTT on ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and SWE-bench Lite using two model families. ReTTT outperforms trajectory-based test-time training across all six model--benchmark settings, improving task success by up to 12.14 percentage points. A frozen-reflection comparison further shows a 7.14-point benefit from adapting the reflection policy, while trajectory analysis illustrates how adapted reflection derives more task-specific guidance from failed attempts. These findings suggest that learning to reflect on its own behavior can boost agents' performance on long-horizon tasks.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new method for test-time training that adapts language model agents via reflection and LoRA adapters during multi-turn tasks. While it involves adaptation and fine-tuning steps, it does not explicitly focus on RLHF or instruction-following improvements (criterion 1), nor on contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is more about episodic adaptation in interactive tasks, which is outside the specific criteria.

**Novelty:** 5

## 181. [Competing Learning in Hybrid Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26830) <a id="link181"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26830
**Authors:** Xingyu Shen, Yingfa Chen, Ziqing Qiao, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han, Zhiyuan Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26830v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Many modern language models have adopted hybrid architectures that incorporate both linear-time recurrence and softmax attention. Modern recurrence layers provide a highly expressive mixing mechanism while ensuring high throughput by compressing contextual information into a fixed-size state, but underperform softmax attention in contextual information retrieval. In this paper, we reveal a Competing Learning phenomenon where different kinds of mixers compete to learn the same function, and when any layer learns a function, it suppresses the corresponding learning signal for other layers. Layer attribution analysis reveals that under typical data distributions, modern recurrence learns retrieval functions faster than softmax attention, causing attention to fail to learn retrieval in most cases. Stopping weight updates in recurrence in the middle of training helps attention learn retrieval. Based on these results, we propose a staged training procedure for pre-training hybrid architectures on natural language. Empirical results on models up to 1B parameters show that our method results in substantial improvement in language modeling loss, common-sense reasoning, as well as a wide range of retrieval-related tasks across different context lengths.

**Comment:** Related to improving training procedures for hybrid architectures combining recurrence and attention in language models, but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. Focuses on staged training to improve retrieval functions.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 182. [PACT: NVFP4 Data-Flow LoRA Fine-Tuning on Consumer GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25048) <a id="link182"></a>

**Authors:** Lei Gao, Hossein Entezari Zarch, Chaoyi Jiang, Murali Annavaram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25048v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-tuning language models on consumer GPUs is constrained by computation and memory. Although LoRA reduces trainable parameters and optimizer state, frozen projections remain expensive, and activations retained for backward can dominate memory usage. We present PACT, a LoRA fine-tuning system that accelerates frozen projections with native NVFP4 GEMMs while reducing quantization and dequantization overhead and limiting quantization error. To reduce activation memory, PACT reuses quantized RMSNorm outputs and saves gate/up activations in NVFP4 for backward. To limit numerical error, it preserves BF16 inputs for forward nonlinear operations and calibrates frozen weights offline using GPTQ adapted to NVFP4 scales and quantized activations. Fused nonlinear kernels and cached forward and backward weight layouts reduce quantization and dequantization overhead. Evaluations across multiple models and tasks on a 16\,GB RTX~5080 show lower memory usage and faster LoRA fine-tuning with competitive accuracy, enabling model adaptation on resource-constrained consumer hardware.

**Comment:** This paper presents a new LoRA fine-tuning system optimized for consumer GPUs. It focuses on efficient fine-tuning methods but does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following improvements, or contamination/membership inference. It is relevant to fine-tuning but not to the specific criteria about RLHF or instruction tuning.

## 183. [Stratified Sparse Attention: Reducing the Sparse-to-Dense Gap at Extreme Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28441) <a id="link183"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28441
**Authors:** Sahil Joshi, Prithvi Dixit, Aryan Katiyar, Agniva Chowdhury, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Anshumali Shrivastava, Aditya Desai, Kumar Krishna Agrawal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28441v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Model inference over long contexts is increasingly bottlenecked by the cost of attending to a growing Key-Value (KV) cache. Sparse attention reduces this cost by accessing only a fraction of the cache, but existing methods face a trade-off between deterministic retrieval, which captures high-scoring tokens but discards residual attention mass, and sampling, which improves coverage at the cost of higher variance. Hybrid methods combine top-$k$ retrieval with uniform sampling, yet still exhibit a substantial gap from dense attention at aggressive sparsity. We introduce \emph{Stratified Sparse Attention (SSA)}, a unified framework combining deterministic retrieval with score-guided sampling. SSA deterministically attends to high-scoring tokens while sampling the residual using proxy attention scores and correcting for their inclusion probabilities, thereby recovering attention mass beyond the retrieval set. SSA encompasses top-$k$, top-$p$, and hybrid top-$k$+sampling as special cases. Across RULER, LongBench, LOFT, and AIME, and five models ranging from 3B to 27B parameters, SSA consistently improves over the baselines at matched sparsity. On RULER-HARD with Ministral-3-8B, SSA improves accuracy over the strongest practical baseline from 81.8\% to 84.2\% at 32K and from 63.8\% to 69.3\% at 128K under $50\times$ attention sparsity, recovering 94\% and 85\% of dense-attention performance, respectively. With optimized CUDA kernels, SSA achieves up to $2.96\times$ decode speedup over FlashInfer at 1M context, demonstrating that its accuracy gains translate into practical long-context inference acceleration. We open-source our code at https://github.com/skylight-org/SSA-Paper-Code.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new sparse attention method for large language models improving inference efficiency and accuracy. While it relates to language model architecture and efficiency, it does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. No direct match to the specified criteria.

## 184. [Ask Less, Ask Better: Allocating User Attention in Proactive LLM Agents with Interruption Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24411) <a id="link184"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24411
**Authors:** Xuanming Zhang, Amir H. Rezaeian, Miguel Ballesteros, Weiyi Sun, Werner Geyer, Lydia B. Chilton, Zhou Yu, Yassine Benajiba, Dan Roth

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24411v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents can ask clarifying questions to complete tasks, but answering each question demands users' time and effort. Existing evaluations typically measure this burden by counting questions, treating necessary and avoidable questions equally. We propose an evaluation protocol that distinguishes how often agents ask from how well they ask. An interruption budget limits the number of questions, while a user-attention cost charges extra for questions that are hard to read or not worth asking, as rated by a human-validated LLM judge. Beyond question counts, this cost captures perceived effort: on conversation pairs with the same number of questions, human annotators agreed with the cost on which conversation required more effort 75% of the time. Across eight LLMs and four customer-service domains, limiting agents to one to three questions reduces user-attention cost by 36-73% while keeping 86-94% of the task reward they earn with unlimited questions. At the same budget, reviewing each question against the memory of users' earlier answers cuts attention cost by another 13% with little reward loss and yields more valuable questions. Models also differ sharply in how well they ask: at the same user-attention cost, the most efficient models gain more than twice as much reward from their questions as the others. These results show that asking less and asking better are distinct goals, and that evaluations of proactive agents should measure both.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper proposes an evaluation protocol for proactive LLM agents asking clarifying questions, focusing on user attention cost and question quality. While it relates to evaluation of LLM outputs, it does not fundamentally rethink open-ended text generation evaluation (criterion 4) but rather proposes a practical metric for question asking efficiency.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 185. [Measuring the Task Value of Precision Upgrades in Closed-Loop Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24346) <a id="link185"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24346
**Authors:** Sicheng Lyu, Xinyu Wang, Yu Gu, Lei Ding, Ziyang Song, Xiao-Wen Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24346v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A low-precision agent can call its full-precision (BF16) model at selected decisions as a fallback. A learned precision router chooses where to call it from the disagreement between the two models and is judged by end-to-end success. An inserted BF16 action, however, can rescue a failing task or damage a succeeding one. We argue that a fallback's value is the number of tasks it saves minus the number it spoils, measured for the intervention that will actually run. From restored states, we run paired branches that cross which model supplies the current action with which model continues, separating replacement of one action, takeover of later execution, and both. On Qwen3 agents with simulated 4-bit weights in tau2-bench, replacing the first action where the two models differed in tool use changed many task outcomes, but rescues and damages nearly cancelled, and on held-out telecom tasks the action-only estimate was negative. Replacing both the action and the continuation had a positive net gain in two of five exploratory lower-bit stress settings. Two references changed outcomes at fixed bit width: replacing an action with that of a second 4-bit checkpoint changed about as many outcomes as replacing it with the BF16 action, and reordering tool-argument keys changed outcomes while the executed tool call stayed identical. At common states, the additional value of the BF16 action over that checkpoint remained unresolved. A frozen uncertainty trigger found more outcome changes than random selection, while its net benefit stayed within the range of random selection. We recommend evaluating each fallback by its signed task benefit under the intended continuation, against same-precision references, before attributing gains to more bits.

**Comment:** Studies the value of precision upgrades in closed-loop agents by analyzing the impact of fallback actions on task success. This is an empirical study on precision and intervention effects, not about instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 186. [Compositional Activation Steering Across Languages and Behaviors with Contextual Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24906) <a id="link186"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24906
**Authors:** Daniil Gurgurov, Akansh Maurya, Josef van Genabith, Simon Ostermann, Hitomi Yanaka

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24906v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Activation steering has emerged as an effective and precise way to influence the behaviours of language models. However, composing multiple steering directions to control several behaviours simultaneously remains challenging, particularly in multilingual settings and with training-free steering vectors. We address this challenge with contextual steering, jointly training gates that adapt the strength of fixed steering directions to the current hidden state and interactions between interventions. Across a broad range of languages, behavioural attributes, and models, our approach outperforms constant-strength steering and four strong baselines, including prompting. It also better preserves general knowledge and keeps the model's internal computation closer to that of the prompted model. Our analysis further shows how jointly trained gates adapt their contributions during composition, explaining the mechanisms underlying effective compositional steering.

## 187. [Gaussian Process Bandits with Paired Auxiliary Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24737) <a id="link187"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24737
**Authors:** Weibo Huang, Qing Feng, Ruihao Zhu, Cheng Hua

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24737v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is widely used for expensive black-box optimization, yet observation noise makes each evaluation less informative and slows posterior learning. Standard BO learns from costly outcomes gathered during optimization. In many applications, however, low-cost auxiliary data are available beforehand. We introduce Auxiliary-Informed Gaussian Process Bandit (AI-GPB), a variance-reduction method that combines an offline estimate of the auxiliary mean with a correction coefficient learned from paired online observations. Notably, it exploits correlation between paired observation errors without requiring the auxiliary mean to approximate the objective. We establish finite-sample GP confidence bounds and regret guarantees, including explicit cumulative and simple regret rates for Mat\'ern kernels. Numerical experiments across continuous BO benchmarks with varied landscapes and dimensions show consistent reductions in cumulative and simple regret relative to vanilla GP-UCB, even when within-pair correlation is moderate and the auxiliary mean is estimated from noisy offline data.

**Comment:** No specific criteria matched. The paper proposes a Bayesian optimization method using auxiliary observations, which is a statistical ML contribution but unrelated to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or language model evaluation paradigms.

## 188. [M-Plan: Comprehensive Planning For LLM Agents with Memory Arrangement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27032) <a id="link188"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27032
**Authors:** Ziyang Liu, Jiakang Yuan, Jiayuan Fan, Tao Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27032v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents have recently demonstrated outstanding performance in single-turn tasks, yet they continue to struggle with long-horizon tasks, particularly in environments without prior training. A key limitation of single LLMs is their difficulty in processing long contexts, which stems from weaknesses in continuous decision-making and reasoning capabilities. Although existing multi-agent systems (MAS) equipped with long-term memory bases can partially address this gap, they mostly rely on knowledge reuse or summarization, an approach that is often constrained by directly retrieval without any adaptation for the current task, while the retrieved memory may contain much history noises waiting to be preprocessed. In contrast, MAS characterized by task-specific guidance often lack long-term memory specifically designed for the guidance. To address these challenges, we propose M-Plan, a closed-loop MAS framework that integrates long-term and short-term memory. By combining global-local and past-current guidance, M-Plan enhances agents' performance on multi-turn tasks while maintaining a plug-and-play design. During task execution, agents receive task-specific milestones and insights, and are provided with local hints only when confusion arises, which form global-local guidance together. These guidance generation and utilization construct the short-term memory as well. After task completion, relevant information about the current task is optimized or reorganized before being sent to the long-term memory storage for future retrieval. The designments mentioned above build a comprehensive framework different from other individual long-term or short-term memory works. Extensive experiments on multi-turn tasks including ALFWorld, Webshop, and PDDL across diverse models demonstrate that M-Plan outperforms multiple baseline methods in both accuracy and efficiency.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on multi-agent systems with memory for long-horizon tasks and planning, which is related to language model agents but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms explicitly.

## 189. [Behave Like Experts: Fusing Data with Semantic Knowledge for Effective and Efficient Semantic Map Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25122) <a id="link189"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25122
**Authors:** Zhu Liu, Zhen Hu, Yuxuan Liu, Ying Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25122v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Semantic map models (SMMs) construct a graph that reflects conceptual similarity by comparing cross-lingual form-meaning mappings. Experts build such models not only by obeying the data constraint, i.e., the connectivity hypothesis, which states that conceptual nodes belonging to the same form must be connected, but also by incorporating their prior semantic knowledge. Current automatic methods ignore this knowledge, which harms both accuracy and efficiency by enlarging the search space of candidate graphs. In this paper, we fuse the distributional semantic knowledge hidden in the internal representations of language models into a top-down, data-driven algorithm. This expert-like behavior re-sorts the candidates in a huge search space, guided by conceptual semantics similarity. Experiments on a self-collected benchmark of 10 datasets and comprehensive metrics show that our method builds SMMs both effectively and efficiently.

**Comment:** No match to any criteria. The paper discusses semantic map construction using language model internal representations but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 190. [From Encoding to Generation: Towards Empathetic Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27603) <a id="link190"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27603
**Authors:** Samhita Bollepally, Takashi Yamauchi, Marissa West

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27603v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Empathetic interaction involves two distinguishable capacities: representing another person's emotional state (cognitive empathy) and generating an appropriate response to that state (affective empathy). We apply this distinction to examine how emotion-related information is internally organized, selectively attended to, and carried through response generation in Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct across 10 emotion categories from the EmpatheticDialogues dataset. Layer-wise linear probing revealed a broad middle-layer region of peak emotion-category decodability, with the layer-11 L2 probe achieving 63.6% held-out accuracy against a 10% chance level and a 54.6% TF-IDF lexical baseline. During response generation, emotion-related user tokens received 1.89 times as much attention per token as other utterance tokens, concentrated across layers 10-15. Target-emotion alignment then decreased from a mean cosine similarity of 0.239 for input representations to 0.125 for generated responses, with positive drift in 77% of examples and the largest shifts for angry and furious. Emotion-category representation, attention allocation, and response-level alignment therefore emerge as distinguishable components of model processing that can be analyzed through a functional cognitive-affective empathy framework. These findings provide a foundation for monitoring emotion representations during generation and leveraging models' existing affective structure to support more naturally empathetic responses without relying on external prompting or explicit emotion conditioning.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper analyzes emotion representation and attention in Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, focusing on cognitive and affective empathy components. While it studies internal model representations and generation, it does not propose new instruction-following fine-tuning methods (criterion 1), new evaluation paradigms (criterion 4), or interpretability techniques (criterion 8).

## 191. [TideKV: Delayed KV Eviction via Layer-Wise Chunked Prefill for Memory-Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24499) <a id="link191"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24499
**Authors:** Yu Wang, Zhu Wang, Ning Hu, Yifan Guo, Xiu Ma, Bin Guo, Zhiwen Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24499v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is important for modern intelligent applications but remains constrained by peak memory during prompt prefill. Existing KV cache compression reduces decoding memory but typically requires full-prompt prefill to preserve global context, retaining a high construction peak. Online chunk-level eviction lowers memory earlier but selects before each layer has observed the complete prompt, potentially degrading cache construction and decoding. We present TideKV, an on-GPU cache-construction architecture using layer-major delayed eviction. TideKV processes all prompt chunks within each Transformer layer, retains full-prompt KV only for the active layer, and evicts at layer end. This schedule preserves full-prompt-aware selection while reducing activation and temporary memory. Experiments across multiple computing platforms show that TideKV preserves the inference quality and final cache size of the evaluated compression methods. At 64K on A100, it reduces the construction-time transient footprint by 83.89%. On RTX 4090, it increases time to first token by only 1.06% at 64K and enables 128K prefill. TideKV also completes 128K on Jetson, whereas matched post-prefill compression runs out of memory on both resource-constrained platforms. These results show that delayed layer-end eviction substantially expands feasible context lengths without sacrificing cache utility.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria; focuses on engineering improvements for memory-efficient long-context LLM inference rather than methodological or theoretical advances.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 192. [Table Serialization Determines Which Relations Language Models Preserve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24798) <a id="link192"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24798
**Authors:** Junwei Ma, Arun Kumar Chithanar, Palash Goyal, Chul Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24798v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study how text serialization affects table lookup in instruction-tuned language models. Our diagnostic asks the model to find the row containing a specified value under one named column, then return the value under another named column in that same row. We render the same synthetic 256-cell tables in row-major form and in column-major form with each header placed beside its values, while varying the table aspect ratio across five shapes. Across three models, the better format changes with table shape: row-major performs better on tall tables, whereas column-major performs better on wide tables. At 64 x 4, row-major leads by 42.3-54.0 percentage points; at 4 x 64, column-major leads by 35.0-59.0 points. Mapping wrong answers back to their source cells reveals a complementary error pattern: row-major tends to preserve the correct row but lose the target column, while column-major tends to preserve the correct column but lose the row. We use the term relational locality to describe this pattern: serialization keeps one relation locally available while making the other depend on positional alignment. A separate non-tabular ordering experiment supports this interpretation. Thus, apparent width-related failures in text-serialized tables depend on linearization, not table shape alone.

**Comment:** Does not match any specific criterion. Focuses on how table serialization affects instruction-following in language models but does not propose new instruction-following fine-tuning methods or contamination detection, nor diffusion or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 193. [InsightRAG: Aggregating Distributed Scholarly Judgments for Scientific Survey Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28085) <a id="link193"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28085
**Authors:** Yuquan Lin, Wenshuo Zhang, Yilin Ye, Shuchang Xu, Zhao Chen, Weiqi Wang, Huamin Qu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28085v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The rapid growth of scientific literature makes survey writing increasingly challenging, motivating automatic survey generation. Yet, existing systems must search an open space of papers and an open space of concepts with no trace of how the community itself has navigated them, leading to incomplete scope, weak taxonomies, and unreliable citations. Our core insight is that author-written Related Work sections contain the human judgments needed to address both limitations: authors curate the citations relevant to their contributions and explain how the cited works relate, yielding paths for scope reconstruction and relational evidence for organization. We propose InsightRAG. Its insight-guided retrieval follows author-supported citation relations to reconstruct a verified research scope and structure the resulting relational evidence. It aggregates these judgments under a minimum-conflict objective into a taxonomy that reflects recurring author-written perspectives in the retrieved literature, guides generation, and bounds each section's evidence. Results on ten topics show gains in scope reconstruction, taxonomy quality, citation support, and reference reliability.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on automatic scientific survey generation using citation relations, which is an application of retrieval and generation rather than new methods for instruction-following, contamination detection, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 194. [EGI-BENCH: An Enterprise General Intelligence Benchmark Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27234) <a id="link194"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27234
**Authors:** Zhiwei Liu, Zhujun Lan, Jielin Qiu, Zixiang Chen, Liangwei Yang, Ming Zhu, Akshara Prabhakar, Wenting Zhao, John Emmons, Jason Wu, Shafiq Rayhan Joty, Junnan Li, Shelby Heinecke, Silvio Savarese, Huan Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.27234v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Enterprise adoption of LLMs in agentic workflows is gated by evaluations that span a wide arc of abilities: CRM operations, heterogeneous-document retrieval, personal data understanding, multi-turn planning, tool use, function calling, long-horizon research, and long-context software engineering. These evaluations live today in separate repositories with divergent runners, dependencies, and scoring conventions, which makes cross-benchmark comparison difficult and obscures what a single aggregate number actually represents. We release EGI-BENCH, a unified LLM agent benchmark suite that consolidates eight enterprise facing agent benchmarks (CRMARENA, HERB, PERSONABENCH, USERBENCH, MCP-UNIVERSE, MCPEVAL, BENCHFORCE, LOCOBENCH) behind a single command-line interface with two runtime-budgeted execution modes. On top of the suite we define the Enterprise General Intelligence (EGI) Score, a six-dimensional framework (M1-M6) whose buckets are populated by fine-grained sub-metrics routed from each benchmark rather than by benchmark-level averages. We describe the suite, the sub-metric routing, and the quick/deep budgets, and report results for fourteen frontier and cost-efficient LLMs spanning the GPT, Claude, and Gemini families under quick mode. Top-line EGI ranges from 39.6 (GPT-4o mini) to 65.6 (gpt-5.5), and no single model dominates every capability axis: gpt-5.5 leads task outcome, planning, and retrieval grounding; Claude Sonnet 4.5 leads tool actioning and safety/abstention; and Claude Haiku 4.5 leads engineering quality. Code is available at https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/egi-bench.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Presents a benchmark suite for enterprise LLM evaluation but does not fundamentally rethink evaluation paradigms or address contamination, RLHF, or interpretability.

## 195. [Prosodic Structure as a Critical Component in Phonemic Language Modelling at BabyLM Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24172) <a id="link195"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24172
**Authors:** Bill K. S. Ku, Janet B. Pierrehumbert

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24172v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Human children learn language from continuous, prosodically structured speech long before they can read, yet almost all language models train on orthographic text. Building on previous phonemic BabyLM training (Goriely et al., 2024; arXiv:2410.22906), we ask whether supplying an estimate of the prosody infants exploit improves phonemic language modelling at BabyLM scale. We compare an orthographic baseline to three conditions forming a ladder of phonological richness. All conditions are trained as BERT MLMs at 10M and 100M words. Addressing the ecological validity of existing metrics in the small-data regime, we build a lexical decision task and two semantic organisation tasks. Our new lexical decision task offers improved insights into the distinction between wordlikeness, token familiarity, and lexicality as factors. Triphone phonotactic control and pseudo-log-likelihood scoring shows that these scores reflect phonotactic familiarity rather than wordhood: every model sits near or below chance on an adversarial inverted condition, revealing prior results of this kind as partly artefactual. On our new semantic tasks, a prosodically annotated phoneme stream gives the strongest taxonomic organisation at both the 10M and 100M word scales. Phonemic models with boundary structure are competitive with the orthographic baseline. These findings suggest that prosodic structure is a key component of phonemic language modelling in the small-data regime most analogous to infant learning.

## 196. [Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867) <a id="link196"></a>

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23867v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as simulated respondents in computational social science, yet accurately reproducing population-specific heterogeneous response distributions remains challenging. Existing methods such as demographic prompting are sensitive to prompt design and increase inference overhead, while parameter-based methods struggle to reuse demographic information efficiently, limiting generalization to unseen combinations. In this regard, we propose PARADE, a composable PARAmetric DEmographic injection framework that represents each demographic attribute value with a reusable and composable LoRA module. Specifically, PARADE separates task-common survey capability from demographic-specific value shifts via a shared Task LoRA and attribute-level Demographic LoRAs, with composition calibration to reduce parameter interference. At inference time, target population models can be constructed on demand by recombining the relevant modules, including demographic combinations not observed during training. Extensive experiments on the World Values Survey and SocioBench across three backbone models show that PARADE achieves strong population-level distribution alignment while maintaining competitive predictive performance, reducing KL divergence by up to 52.8% relative to the strongest baseline.

## 197. [First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24816) <a id="link197"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24816
**Authors:** Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, Dongsheng Luo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24816v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on efficient on-device fine-tuning using LoRA and hardware optimization for NPUs, which is an engineering/systems contribution rather than a new methodological improvement to RLHF or instruction-following (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 198. [Towards Figurative Language Understanding in Moroccan Darija: An Evaluation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25406) <a id="link198"></a>

**Authors:** Jihad R'baiti, Youssef Hmamouche, Emmanuele Chersoni, Chu-Ren Huang, Amal El Fallah Seghrouchni

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25406v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Figurative language remains a major challenge for Machine Translation (MT) systems, as the intended meaning of such expressions often goes beyond literal translation. Although recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) improved the translation of Arabic dialects, benchmark resources for evaluating figurative language translation in Moroccan Darija remain scarce. To this end, we introduce the Moroccan Darija Figurative Language Corpus (MDFC), a benchmark dataset for MT and multiple-choice question (MCQ) selection covering idioms, metaphors, and proverbs. Using MDFC, we evaluate 19 open- and closed-weight language models and MT systems at both the expression and contextual levels under zero-shot and six-shot settings. We also conduct layer-wise similarity-based probing to determine how model representations distinguish figurative from literal meanings. Our findings reveal that closed-weight models achieve the strongest translation performance, while the benefits of contextual information vary across models and tasks. Differences between translation performance and MCQ accuracy highlight the distinction between generating an appropriate figurative translation and recognizing the intended meaning among candidate translations.

## 199. [Evolving Agent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28601) <a id="link199"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28601
**Authors:** Saaket Agashe, Yuzhe Yang, Gonzalo Gonzalez-Pumariega, Bhuvan Chandra Koduru, Chih-Lun Li, Jiachen Yang, Ang Li, Xin Eric Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.28601v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Computer-use agents are limited by the interfaces humans give them. An agent may type a spreadsheet cell by cell when a single line of code would do, or print all two thousand lines of a file through a shell to find one function. Both the interfaces and the knowledge of when to use each are built by hand, which cannot keep pace with new applications, workflows, and models. We argue that interface design should itself be an agent capability. We introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds Evolving Agent Interfaces from task experience. A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, without success labels, and constructs and revises interfaces and usage knowledge in a repository. Population-based evolution then selects which repositories to develop further by their performance on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, favoring changes that generalize beyond the seen tasks. On OSWorld V2, Agent S4, with Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved repository of interfaces, sets a new state of the art in open-source computer-use agents, reaching 21.3% binary score, 3.8 times the same model with fixed interfaces. This is above both GPT-5.5 (13.0%) and Claude Opus 4.8 (20.6%), at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their respective costs. On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces improve the success rate from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. These results suggest that useful interfaces can be evolved from experience, making autonomous interface design a source of improvement complementary to advances in models and harnesses. We open-source our code at https://github.com/simular-ai/agent-s.

The paper focuses on evolving agent interfaces for computer-use agents, which is an application and system design topic rather than methodological improvements to RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. ## 200. [HoloCosm: Beyond Single-Metric Benchmark Compression Across Multiple Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26714) <a id="link200"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26714
**Authors:** Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Guoming Ling, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Yukai Shi, Liang Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26714v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmark compression reduces evaluation cost by selecting a small set of samples. Existing methods typically guide selection using a single metric, although benchmarks often assess models along multiple dimensions. We find that model rankings are often weakly correlated across metrics, indicating that different metrics capture distinct aspects of model performance. Samples selected for one metric do not reliably preserve scores and rankings on others when used for compressed evaluation. At 80\% compression, each of the five single-metric baselines yields higher reconstruction error than equal-size random sampling in over 70\% of cross-metric comparisons. To address this challenge, we present HoloCosm, which jointly minimizes normalized score-reconstruction error across source models and metrics while constraining ranking distortion. We evaluate HoloCosm across multiple compression rates on 15 language, multimodal, VLA, and agent benchmark configurations with 85 component metrics. With 80\% compression, HoloCosm outperforms Random, the strongest baseline overall: average NMAE drops by 35.3\% to 0.236, worst-component NMAE drops by 39.5\% to 0.330, and average Spearman correlation rises from 0.884 to 0.915. In addition, HoloCosm remains competitive with existing baselines in single-metric evaluation. We will release our code and experimental results.

**Comment:** This paper addresses benchmark compression across multiple metrics for model evaluation, which relates to evaluation methodology but does not fundamentally rethink open-ended text generation evaluation or instruction-following fine-tuning. It is not about RLHF or instruction tuning, nor about test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 201. [Predicting CVSS and CWE from CVE Descriptions with Fine-Tuned Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24297) <a id="link201"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24297
**Authors:** Tara Dixit, Jessie Dong, Summer Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24297v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Until April 2026, the National Vulnerability Database labeled nearly every published Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) entry with a Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) v3.1 vector, a severity tier, and a Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) weakness type. Now, NIST enriches only the high priority CVEs, leaving many newly published vulnerabilities unlabelled with the classifiers that security teams use for patch triage. In CVE enrichment, we predict the eight base components of CVSS v3.1 vector, base score, severity tier, and CWE from the vulnerability's textual description alone. To prevent leakage between near-duplicate descriptions, all systems are trained only on CVEs published in 2022 and 2023. Results are evaluated on all 36,325 usable 2024 CVEs in the pinned NVD snapshot. All scores are either directly predicted, or calculated from predicted vectors using the CVSS formula. A fine-tuned 3.8B-parameter model Phi-3.5-mini, trained with LoRA in 2.5 hours on a single Apple M5 Pro chip, achieves 69.9% accuracy on severity and 70.9% on CWE. It matches the official vector exactly for 48.8% of test CVEs, outperforming TF-IDF logistic regression by 2.8 to 4.7 points on all accuracy metrics based on zero-exclusion paired bootstrap intervals. When we evaluate the base model with a zero-shot prompt, it achieves 20.8% accuracy on severity in a sample of 2,000 CVEs. In this same sample, its stated scores match its own vector precisely for only 3.4% of CVEs. However, deriving scores from predicted vectors boosts the critical recall of TF-IDF logistic regression from 50.7% to 73.3% with no additional cost. And since the errors of the fine-tuned model and TF-IDF affect different CVEs, at least one system matches the correct severity tier for 75.8% of test CVEs.

**Comment:** Relevant to general language model fine-tuning and evaluation but does not match any specific criterion related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion models, or interpretability. Focuses on vulnerability classification using fine-tuned small LMs, an application domain.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 202. [When Planning Problems Become Hard for LLMs: Separating Structural Complexity from Semantic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26964) <a id="link202"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26964
**Authors:** Mayukh Debnath, Adeyinka Adedigba, Rammohan Mallipeddi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26964v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) struggle with classical planning, but benchmarks rarely distinguish structural difficulty from the difficulty of interpreting symbolic vocabulary. We examine seven STRIPS domains with BFS-optimal plan lengths. For predicting structural difficulty without solving an instance, the zero-training delete-relaxation heuristic $h_{\mathrm{add}}$ outperforms learned alternatives on held-out domains (mean Spearman $|\rho|=0.931$), including ridge regression ($0.707$), object count ($0.657$), and the episodic retrieval architecture ARC ($0.400$). ARC also fails to outperform a linear model using the same features. To isolate semantic effects, we progressively rename predicates while preserving states, transitions, and optimal plans. Valid LLM planning success drops from $67.5\%$ to $40.0\%$ between $0\%$ and $100\%$ renaming, while refusals remain at $0\%$. An LLM supplied with ARC's conditioning information predicts difficulty in context better than trained ARC (mean $|\rho|=0.826$). Hybrid routing between an LLM and a symbolic planner depends more on calibration to the target LLM's outcomes than on the structural score selected. Thus, established symbolic heuristics already estimate structural difficulty well; improving hybrid planning requires modeling LLM behavior sensitive to predicate semantics rather than developing more structural predictors.

**Comment:** This paper analyzes structural versus semantic difficulty in LLM planning tasks but does not propose new RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods, nor does it address test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is relevant to understanding LLM behavior but does not match any specific criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

---

## 203. [Self-Consistency of Entity Sets and Citations in a Deep Research System under Repeated Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26421) <a id="link203"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26421
**Authors:** Lauri Pyk\"al\"a, Kimmo K. Kaski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26421v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deep research agents (DRA) are commonly used for open-ended synthesis tasks where closed-world QA benchmarks are poorly suited to characterize relevant aspects of performance. We study the self-consistency of one commercial deep research system under repeated identical prompting, representing each report with the set of named entities it mentions, categorised by a custom schema, and the set of citations it retrieves. Across repeated runs of a single open-ended cybersecurity related prompt, we quantify pairwise overlap to characterize how stable the retrieved and synthesized output structure is under repeated identical prompts. In ten runs, the pairwise entity Jaccard results in a median of 0.12 and the pairwise citation Jaccard is below 0.02, with discovery curves that do not visibly saturate. The magnitude and structure of (dis)agreement between entity sets varies by entity class, which suggests that self-consistency in a DRA is better understood as a function of the underlying entity space than as a stable property of the system.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper studies self-consistency and stability of outputs from a deep research system under repeated prompting, focusing on entity and citation overlap. This relates loosely to open-ended generation evaluation but does not propose new paradigms for evaluation (criterion 4).
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

---

## 204. [How Well Can LLMs Verbalize Mathematical Formulas? A Multi-Domain Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24917) <a id="link204"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24917
**Authors:** Peter Volbach, Tim Schlippe

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24917v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities across natural language tasks. But their ability to generate accurate written verbalizations of mathematical formulas has not been systematically evaluated. Such verbalization is important for text-to-speech systems supporting audio-based learning and visually impaired users. We evaluated five state-of-the-art LLMs across six scientific domains and constructed Read-Aloud Math, a balanced multi-domain corpus with LaTeX formulas, manually validated simple and extended transcriptions, multiple variants, textual context, domain labels, and formula complexity levels. Using multi-reference BLEU, GPT-5.2 achieved the highest score for simple transcriptions (75.9% without context, 64.9% with context), although without context it did not significantly outperform Grok 4.1 Fast or Claude Sonnet 4.5. Claude Sonnet 4.5 achieved the highest score for extended transcriptions without context (36.5%), and Grok 4.1 Fast with context (48.8%). For extended transcriptions, Statistics and Mathematics scored highest across the two context settings, while Physics scored lowest. Formula length was significantly negatively associated with BLEU for simple transcriptions without context, but not with context, whereas BLEU increased significantly with formula length for extended transcriptions in both settings. Grouping symbols were most frequently involved in transcription differences, while variable names, capitalization, and Greek letters were associated with lower BLEU scores. Overall, LLM-based formula verbalization is promising but remains sensitive to transcription type, textual context, and formula characteristics.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper evaluates LLMs on verbalizing mathematical formulas, which is an application-focused evaluation rather than methodological improvements to instruction-following or RLHF, or new evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

---

## 205. [Analyzing Repetition and Interference Effects in Memory-Constrained User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24232) <a id="link205"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24232
**Authors:** Cara Su-Yi Leong, Nicholas Tomlin, Tal Linzen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24232v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: User simulators can help evaluate and train AI systems to interact with humans at scale. To do so, evaluation of user simulators must measure behavioral parity with people. We evaluate whether memory-constrained user simulators are sensitive to two factors known to affect human information retention: repetition of a fact and interference from competing information. We find that some simulators achieve human-like overall accuracy, and replicate the magnitude of repetition and interference effects, but none match people's accuracy on particular questions: while humans misremember facts, models forget them, leading to different error patterns. We release this data as a challenge benchmark for future work on user simulators.

**Comment:** This paper studies memory-constrained user simulators and their behavioral fidelity to humans, but it does not propose new RLHF or instruction-following methods, nor does it address test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is somewhat relevant to modeling and simulation but not to the specified criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

---


---

## Paper selection prompt
 1. New methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following which are specific fine-tuning steps that are taken to make language models better at following user instructions across a range of tasks.
    - Relevant: papers that discuss specific methods like RLHF, or instruction-tuning datasets, improving these methods, or analyzing them. Usually these papers will explicitly mention RLHF, instruction-following or instruction-tuning.
    - Not relevant: papers about adaptation to some task. Simply following instructions or inputs are not sufficient.
 2. Shows new powerful test set contamination or membership inference methods for language models. Test set contamination is the phenomenon where a language model observes a benchmark dataset during pretraining.
    - Relevant: test statistics that can detect contamination of benchmarks in language models. statistics that can provide guarantees are more interesting. membership inference methods that are general enough to apply to language models are also relevant.
    - Not relevant: any papers that do not consider language models, or that do not consider test set contamination.
 3. Shows a significant advance in the performance of diffusion language models.
    - Relevant: papers that study language models that are also diffusion models. Continuous diffusions are even more relevant, while discrete diffusions are less so.
    - Not relevant: papers about image diffusions like DALL-E or Stable Diffusion, or papers that do not explicitly mention language models or applications to text.
 4. Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation. Evaluating the outputs of language models is hard, especially in open-ended settings like for chatbots.
    - Relevant: papers that fundamentally rethink language model evaluation -- especially by accounting for subjectivity or using adversaries.
    - Not relevant: specific evaluations for specific tasks, identifying new properties or flaws of language models, or simply collecting new data.
 5. Conducts surveys or provides data into real-world usage and safety properties of language models.
    - Relevant: papers that create new datasets or surveys on real-world usage of language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that apply language models to new real-world tasks.
 6. Studies 'scaling laws' in the context of neural networks. Scaling laws refer to the very clear power-law relationship between the size or computational power used to train a model and the performance of that model.
    - Relevant: theoretical or conceptual explanation behind scaling laws for language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that have experiments at different model scales (but do not explicitly fit a scaling law) or papers that mention scaling laws, but the scaling laws are not the central subject of the paper
 7. Presents new methods or improvements for sparse autoencoders in neural network interpretability. Sparse autoencoders are unsupervised learning techniques that learn sparse representations of neural network activations to understand what features networks detect.
    - Relevant: papers that explicitly mention sparse autoencoders, sparse coding, or sparse feature extraction applied to understanding neural networks, language models, or other AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers about general autoencoders without sparsity constraints, or sparse methods not applied to interpretability.
 8. Demonstrates new mechanistic interpretability techniques or discoveries about how neural networks work internally. Mechanistic interpretability seeks to understand the algorithms and circuits that neural networks learn.
    - Relevant: papers about circuit analysis, feature visualization, activation patching, causal interventions on neural networks, or reverse-engineering specific algorithms learned by models.
    - Not relevant: papers that only do black-box analysis or correlation studies without mechanistic understanding.
 9. Shows advances in machine unlearning techniques for removing specific knowledge or behaviors from trained models.
    - Relevant: papers on forgetting specific data, removing harmful capabilities, or selectively editing model knowledge while preserving other capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about general model editing or fine-tuning without specific unlearning focus.
 10. Presents methods for tamper resistance, robustness against adversarial modifications, or secure AI systems.
    - Relevant: papers on preventing model poisoning, backdoor attacks, or making AI systems resistant to tampering or manipulation.
    - Not relevant: papers only about general adversarial robustness without security/tamper resistance focus.
 11. Studies AI alignment techniques, value learning, or methods to ensure AI systems behave according to human values and intentions.
    - Relevant: papers on reward modeling, constitutional AI, value alignment, or techniques to make AI systems more aligned with human preferences.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on performance improvement without alignment considerations.
 12. Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, chain-of-thought reasoning, or understanding how language models perform complex reasoning tasks.
    - Relevant: papers on step-by-step reasoning, logical inference in LLMs, reasoning chain analysis, or methods to improve reasoning capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about reasoning in non-LLM contexts or that don't focus on reasoning mechanisms.
 13. Presents new methods for AI control, oversight, or monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment.
    - Relevant: papers on AI governance techniques, monitoring systems, control mechanisms, or methods to maintain oversight of deployed AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on training-time controls without deployment considerations.
 14. Shows advances in chain-of-thought monitoring, reasoning transparency, or methods to understand AI decision-making processes.
    - Relevant: papers on monitoring reasoning chains, making AI reasoning more transparent, or techniques to track how models arrive at conclusions.
    - Not relevant: papers about general explainability without specific focus on reasoning processes.
 15. Studies ControlArena, Inspect framework, or similar evaluation platforms for AI safety and control.
    - Relevant: papers using or extending these evaluation frameworks, comparative studies using these platforms, or methodological improvements to these tools.
    - Not relevant: papers that mention these tools only in passing without substantial use or analysis.
 16. Advances in latent reasoning, implicit reasoning processes, or understanding hidden computational processes in neural networks.
    - Relevant: papers on uncovering implicit reasoning, analyzing hidden computational steps, or methods to make latent reasoning processes more explicit.
    - Not relevant: papers about explicit reasoning without focus on hidden or latent processes.
 17. Studies data poisoning attacks or defenses in machine learning systems.
    - Relevant: papers on poisoning training data to manipulate model behavior, detecting poisoned data, or defending against data poisoning attacks.
    - Not relevant: papers about general adversarial examples at inference time without training data manipulation.
 18. Advances in data-centric AI approaches that focus on improving data quality, curation, or data-driven methods for ML.
    - Relevant: papers on data quality assessment, data selection, data augmentation strategies, curriculum learning based on data properties, or systematic approaches to dataset creation and curation.
    - Not relevant: papers that simply use standard datasets without focusing on data-centric methodology.
 19. Studies security of LLMs and methods to protect language models from attacks or exploitation.
    - Relevant: papers on prompt injection defenses, jailbreak prevention, securing LLM deployments, protecting against extraction attacks, or hardening LLM systems against adversarial users.
    - Not relevant: papers about using LLMs for security applications, or general robustness without security focus.

 In suggesting papers to your friend, remember that he enjoys papers on statistical machine learning, and generative modeling in natural language processing.
 Your friend also likes learning about surprising empirical results in language models, as well as clever statistical tricks.
 He does not want to read papers that are about primarily applications of methods to specific domains.
